complete metric. Its displayed metric need not itself be complete.
We use the product topology for each product of topological groups,
and the subspace topology on a cone unless a different topology is
explicitly specified.

We write $\boldsymbol\Delta^0_2$ for the sets that are simultaneously
$F_\sigma$ and $G_\delta$. This class is closed under finite Boolean
operations and continuous inverse images. Every open or closed subset
of a metrizable space belongs to this class. A $G_\delta$ subspace of a
Polish space is Polish. For reference, if $X=\bigcap_n U_n$ in a complete
metric space $X_0$, the map
\[
 x\longmapsto\left(x,
       \bigl(d(x,X_0\setminus U_n)^{-1}\bigr)_{n\in\N}\right)
\]
embeds $X$ as a closed graph in a product of complete spaces, with the
empty-complement coordinates omitted. This gives the usual complete
metrizability argument.

\section{The real-coordinate model}
\label{pma:cp:sec:real}

\begin{theorem}[An affirmative construction; = \cref{pma:pr:thm:main}(1)]
\label{pma:cp:thm:real}
The structure $M_{\R}$ of \eqref{pma:cp:eq:realmodel}, with the operations and
order described in Section~\ref{pma:cp:sec:introduction}, is an uncountable model
of $\Th(\N;0,1,+,<)$. Its topology is Polish, locally compact, and has no isolated
points. Addition is jointly continuous. Therefore Question 2 of
\cite{Glazer}, as stated there, has an affirmative answer.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Consider the abelian group
\[
G_{\R}=\R\times\Z,
\qquad (r,n)+(s,m)=(r+s,n+m).
\]
Give it the order
\begin{equation}
\label{pma:cp:eq:lexreal}
(r,n)<(s,m)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
r<s\ \text{ or }\ (r=s\text{ and }n<m).
\end{equation}
This is a translation-invariant total order. Its least positive element is
$e=(0,1)$: a positive pair either has positive real coordinate and exceeds
every $(0,n)$, or has real coordinate zero and a positive integer coordinate.

The subgroup $\Z e$ is $\{0\}\times\Z$, and
$G_{\R}/\Z e\cong(\R,+)$ is divisible. Hence $G_{\R}$ is a $\Z$-group,
and its nonnegative cone is a model of $\Th(\N;0,1,+,<)$. Computing that cone gives
exactly \eqref{pma:cp:eq:realmodel}.

Topologically, $\R\times\Z_{\mathrm{discrete}}$ is Polish, and
\begin{equation}
\label{pma:cp:eq:localclosed}
M_{\R}=([0,\infty)\times\Z)
\setminus(\{0\}\times\Z_{<0}).
\end{equation}
The first set is closed, and the removed set is closed. Thus $M_{\R}$ is
open in a closed Polish subspace, so is Polish. Equivalently, it is the
countable topological disjoint sum of the slices
\[
M_{\R}\cap(\R\times\{n\})\cong
\begin{cases}
[0,\infty),&n\geq0,\\
(0,\infty),&n<0.
\end{cases}
\]
Each slice is locally compact and has no isolated points. Those properties
therefore hold for the disjoint sum. The map $r\mapsto(r,0)$ embeds
$[0,\infty)$ into the model, proving uncountability.

Ambient coordinatewise addition is continuous. The sum of two nonnegative
pairs is again nonnegative, so its restriction is a continuous map
$M_{\R}^2\to M_{\R}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{figure}[ht]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1.35cm,y=.63cm,>=Stealth]
  \draw[->,gray] (-.5,-2.8)--(-.5,3.2) node[above] {$n$};
  \draw[->,gray] (-.25,-3.05)--(5.15,-3.05) node[right] {$r$};
  \foreach \k in {-2,-1,0,1,2} {
    \draw[very thick,MidnightBlue,->] (0,\k)--(4.85,\k);
    \node[left] at (-.55,\k) {$\k$};
  }
  \foreach \k in {-2,-1} {
    \draw[thick,MidnightBlue,fill=white] (0,\k) circle (2.8pt);
  }
  \foreach \k in {0,1,2} {
    \fill[MidnightBlue] (0,\k) circle (2.8pt);
  }
  \node[below] at (0,-3.05) {$0$};
  \node[above right] at (0,2.5) {one open topological slice for each $n\in\Z$};
  \node[below right] at (1.1,.1) {all points with $r>0$ are arithmetically infinite};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The space $M_{\R}$. Filled endpoints are present; hollow endpoints
are absent. Different horizontal slices are topologically separated.
Arithmetic order compares the horizontal coordinate before the slice label.}
\label{pma:cp:fig:real}
\end{figure}

\noindent\writetag{} \Cref{pma:cp:thm:real} and its proof are those of
\cref{pma:pr:thm:main}(1); source~07 adds the picture in \cref{pma:cp:fig:real}
and a complete metric that uses $\log r$ on the negative slices
(\cref{pma:cp:eq:completemetric}; compare \eqref{pma:pr:eq:completemetric}).
Source~06's account follows in \cref{pma:cp:s06:sec:real}.

\subsection{A complete metric, rather than an incomplete inherited metric}

Being a Polish subspace does not mean that the restriction of the ambient
Euclidean product metric is complete. For example, $(1/j,-1)$ approaches a
missing endpoint in the ambient space. A compatible complete metric is easy
to exhibit.

For the slice with integer coordinate $n$, put
\[
h_n(r)=
\begin{cases}
r,&n\geq0,\\
\log r,&n<0.
\end{cases}
\]
Define
\begin{equation}
\label{pma:cp:eq:completemetric}
d((r,n),(s,m))=
\begin{cases}
1,&n\ne m,\\
\min\{1,|h_n(r)-h_n(s)|\},&n=m.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
The triangle inequality holds within each slice and also across slices.
A Cauchy sequence is eventually in a single slice. In a nonnegative slice
it is complete because $[0,\infty)$ is complete; in a negative slice the
logarithm identifies it with a complete copy of $\R$. Thus $d$ is complete
and gives precisely the topology used in Theorem~\ref{pma:cp:thm:real}.

\subsection{Division and the meaning of nonstandard induction}

For a standard integer $d\geq1$, divide the ordinary integer $n$ as
$n=dq+r$, where $0\leq r<d$. Then
\begin{equation}
\label{pma:cp:eq:divisionreal}
(a,n)=d(a/d,q)+(0,r).
\end{equation}
When $(a,n)\in M_{\R}$, its quotient $(a/d,q)$ is also in $M_{\R}$.
This remains true for negative $n$: such an input has $a>0$, so its
positive real coordinate determines positivity of the quotient.

For example,
\[
(5/2,-7)=3(5/6,-3)+(0,2).
\]
The integer coordinate is permitted to be negative in a positive element.
Forbidding it would destroy predecessor and division properties and would
give the wrong model.

Every $(a,n)$ with $a>0$ exceeds every standard numeral $(0,k)$.
Nevertheless, each induction instance in the first-order additive language
holds, by the $\Z$-group characterization. The external set of standard
numerals is proper and successor-closed, but is not definable in this model,
even with parameters: a definition would violate the corresponding
induction instance. This is the usual distinction between first-order and
full second-order induction, manifested in very elementary coordinates.

\subsection{A perfect locally compact model \src{06}}
\label{pma:cp:s06:sec:real}

\begin{theorem}[An affirmative answer to Glazer's question \src{06}; = \cref{pma:pr:thm:main}(1)]
\label{pma:cp:s06:thm:real}
With the usual topology on $\R$ and the discrete topology on $\Z$, the
space
\begin{equation}
 M_{\R}=((0,\infty)\times\Z)\ \cup\ (\{0\}\times\N)
 \label{pma:cp:s06:eq:realmodel}
\end{equation}
is uncountable, perfect, locally compact, and Polish. With coordinatewise
addition, $0=(0,0)$, $1=(0,1)$, and lexicographic order, it is a model of
$\Pres$ with jointly continuous addition.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The algebra follows from Lemma~\ref{pma:cp:lem:split} with $D=\R$.
For each integer $z$, the slice with second coordinate $z$ is clopen and
is homeomorphic to
\[
 I_z=\begin{cases}[0,\infty),&z\geq0,\\(0,\infty),&z<0.
 \end{cases}
\]
Thus $M_{\R}$ is a countable topological disjoint union of locally compact
Polish rays. It is second countable and has no isolated points.

One can verify complete metrizability without relying on the disjoint
sum assertion. Put $\phi_z(r)=r$ for $z\geq0$ and $\phi_z(r)=\log r$ for
$z<0$, and define
\[
 \rho((r,z),(s,w))=
 \begin{cases}
 2,&z\neq w,\\
 \min\{1,|\phi_z(r)-\phi_z(s)|\},&z=w.
 \end{cases}
\]
This is a compatible complete metric: a Cauchy sequence eventually stays
in one slice, where it corresponds to a Cauchy sequence in $[0,\infty)$
or $\R$. Rational points in all slices give a countable dense set.

Finally, $\R\times\Z$ is a topological group under coordinatewise
addition, and its nonnegative cone is closed under addition. Restricting
the ambient operation gives a continuous map
$M_{\R}\times M_{\R}\to M_{\R}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Arithmetic order and topological proximity \src{06}]
Every $(r,z)$ with $r>0$ is larger, arithmetically, than every standard
natural number $(0,n)$. Nevertheless
\[
 (1/k,0)\longrightarrow(0,0).
\]
There is no contradiction: the topology is the product topology, not
the order topology. In fact, the order topology of any discretely ordered
group or its nonnegative cone is discrete, since consecutive integers
relative to any point isolate it.
\end{example}

The explicit quotient and remainder maps in \eqref{pma:cp:eq:division} are
continuous for every fixed standard $n$. In this example they are
\[
 Q_n(r,z)=(r/n,\lfloor z/n\rfloor),\qquad
 R_n(r,z)=(0,z-n\lfloor z/n\rfloor).
\]
The residue classes form a finite clopen partition. These are fixed
standard-divisor operations; the assertion does not introduce division
by a variable nonstandard element into Presburger arithmetic.

\section{Borel orders on Polish abelian groups}
\label{pma:cp:sec:cones}

The real construction can be extended whenever the positive cone of a
divisible ordered Polish group is a Polish subspace. The following theorem
shows that Borel definability of the order is already enough. We include
the full proof because it is the descriptive engine for the general result.

\begin{theorem}[Low complexity of an invariant Borel order]
\label{pma:cp:thm:cones}
Let $A$ be a Polish abelian group with a translation-invariant total order.
If $P=A_{>0}$ is Borel, then all four sign cones are $\Delta^0_2$.
Both order relations $<$ and $\leq$ are $\Delta^0_2$ subsets of $A^2$.
Moreover, there is a canonically defined countable decreasing chain of
closed convex subgroups that decomposes $P$ into countably many locally
closed pieces.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For a closed subgroup $H\leq A$, write
\[
P_H=P\cap H,\qquad
C(H)=\cl_H(P_H\cup\{0\}),\qquad
D(H)=C(H)\cap(-C(H)).
\]
The set $C(H)$ is a closed additive monoid. Indeed, approximate two of its
points by nonnegative points of $H$ and use continuity of addition.
Consequently $D(H)$ is a closed subgroup. Totality of the order gives
\begin{equation}
\label{pma:cp:eq:coneunion}
H=C(H)\cup(-C(H)).
\end{equation}

We first show that $D(H)\subsetneq H$ whenever $H\ne\{0\}$. The strict
cone $P_H$ is nonmeagre in $H$. In the nondiscrete case this follows from
the Baire category theorem and
\[
H=P_H\ \dot\cup\ (-P_H)\ \dot\cup\ \{0\},
\]
since $\{0\}$ is nowhere dense and negation is a homeomorphism. In the
discrete case $P_H$ is nonempty and hence nonmeagre.

As a Borel set, $P_H$ has the Baire property. There is therefore a nonempty
open set $V\subseteq H$ on which it is comeagre. For every $t\in V+V$,
the set $V\cap(t-V)$ is nonempty and open. Both $P_H$ and $t-P_H$ are
comeagre there, so they intersect. Hence
\begin{equation}
\label{pma:cp:eq:sumsetinterior}
V+V\subseteq P_H+P_H\subseteq P_H.
\end{equation}
This is the sumset form of the category argument often associated with
Piccard and Pettis; the displayed proof fixes the version needed here.

If $C(H)=H$, then $-P_H\cup\{0\}$ is dense in $H$. It would meet the
nonempty open set $V+V\subseteq P_H$, which is impossible. Thus
$C(H)\ne H$, and a fortiori $D(H)\ne H$.

Now form the transfinite recursion
\begin{equation}
\label{pma:cp:eq:conerecursion}
H_0=A,\qquad H_{\xi+1}=D(H_\xi),\qquad
H_\lambda=\bigcap_{\xi<\lambda}H_\xi
\quad(\lambda\text{ a limit}).
\end{equation}
Every term is a closed subgroup, with a strict successor decrease whenever
the current subgroup is nonzero. This process reaches $\{0\}$ at a
countable ordinal $\eta$.

To prove the last claim, suppose instead that there were a strict decrease
at every $\xi<\omega_1$. Fix a countable base $\mathcal B$ of $A$.
For each $\xi$, choose the first basic set $B_\xi$ meeting $H_\xi$ and
disjoint from $H_{\xi+1}$. Such a basic set exists because
$H_{\xi+1}$ is closed and properly contained in $H_\xi$.
If $\zeta>\xi$, then $B_\xi$ misses $H_\zeta$, whereas $B_\zeta$ meets
it. The $B_\xi$ are therefore distinct, contradicting countability.

For $\xi<\eta$, set $C_\xi=C(H_\xi)$ and
\[
L_\xi=H_\xi\setminus(-C_\xi).
\]
Every member of $L_\xi$ is positive, since a nonpositive member would
already lie in $-P_{H_\xi}\cup\{0\}\subseteq-C_\xi$. Equation
\eqref{pma:cp:eq:coneunion} gives the disjoint decomposition
\[
H_\xi\setminus H_{\xi+1}=L_\xi\ \dot\cup\ (-L_\xi).
\]
A nonzero element leaves the decreasing chain at a successor stage:
its first absent stage cannot be a limit, by the definition of intersection.
It follows that
\begin{equation}
\label{pma:cp:eq:layerdecomposition}
P=\bigcup_{\xi<\eta}L_\xi,\qquad
-P=\bigcup_{\xi<\eta}(-L_\xi).
\end{equation}
Each layer is relatively open in the closed set $H_\xi$, and hence is
$F_\sigma$ in the ambient metrizable space. Both unions in
\eqref{pma:cp:eq:layerdecomposition} are countable. Thus $P$ and $-P$ are
$F_\sigma$, and their complements, obtained by adjoining $\{0\}$ to
the opposite cone, are also $F_\sigma$. This proves all four
$\Delta^0_2$ assertions. Applying the continuous difference map
$(x,y)\mapsto y-x$ proves the assertions for order relations.

Finally, every $H_\xi$ is order-convex. The limit step is immediate.
For a successor step, suppose $H$ is convex, $h\in D(H)$, and
$0\leq x\leq h$. Then $x\in H$ and $x\in C(H)$. Also
\[
-x=(-h)+(h-x)\in C(H)+C(H)\subseteq C(H).
\]
Hence $x\in D(H)$, proving convexity.
\end{proof}

\noindent\writetag{} The sumset step \eqref{pma:cp:eq:sumsetinterior} is the
Pettis argument that source~08 uses in \cref{pma:lc:lem:flag,pma:lc:lem:auto}
and source~09 states as \cref{pma:ser:lem:pettis}. For locally compact
second-countable groups, part~(1) of \cref{pma:lc:thm:cones} in Part~IV is
the special case of \cref{pma:cp:thm:cones}, with a different proof through
finite-dimensional cuts.

\begin{remark}[The hypotheses that do the work]
The topology must make $A$ a Polish \emph{group}. A standard Borel group
without an established compatible Polish group topology is not enough for
this proof. Borelness is used on every closed subgroup; merely knowing that
the cone has the Baire property in the ambient space does not automatically
give that hereditary regularity.
\end{remark}

There is classical related work on measurable orders on locally compact
abelian groups and their convex subgroup structure; see Asmar and
Montgomery-Smith \cite{AsmarMS}. We use a direct category proof on Polish
groups, including groups that are not locally compact. No historical
priority claim for the precise formulation of Theorem~\ref{pma:cp:thm:cones}
is needed for the positive construction.

\subsection{A countable ordinal invariant of the order and topology}

\begin{definition}
The \emph{cone closure rank} $\rho(A,<)$ is the least $\eta$ for which the
recursion \eqref{pma:cp:eq:conerecursion} reaches $H_\eta=\{0\}$.
It is an invariant of the ordered topological group, not just of its
abstract group.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}
\label{pma:cp:prop:coneranks}
For every countable ordinal $\gamma$, the product group $\R^\gamma$ with
its product topology and lexicographic order has cone closure rank $\gamma$.
For infinite countable $\gamma$, these are different orders on topological
groups all isomorphic to $\R^{\N}$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For $\beta\leq\gamma$ put
\[
K_\beta=\{x\in\R^\gamma:x_\xi=0\text{ for all }\xi<\beta\}.
\]
These are closed subgroups. The positive cone is Borel, since it is the
countable union, over $\beta<\gamma$, of the sets with zero prefix below
$\beta$ and positive $\beta$-coordinate. For $\beta<\gamma$, inside
$K_\beta$, the closure
of the nonnegative cone is exactly
\[
\{x\in K_\beta:x_\beta\geq0\}.
\]
The inclusion from left to right follows by coordinate continuity. For
the reverse inclusion, points with $x_\beta=0$ are limits of
$x+j^{-1}e_\beta$, which are positive. Intersecting with the negative
closure gives $K_{\beta+1}$. At limit stages the intersection is the
corresponding $K_\lambda$. Thus $H_\beta=K_\beta$ throughout, with the
first zero subgroup at $\gamma$. Reindexing the countably infinite
coordinate set gives the asserted topological group isomorphism.
\end{proof}

Even very low Borel complexity therefore permits arbitrarily long countable
closure recursions. The $\Delta^0_2$ conclusion is sharp: the lexicographic
positive cone of $\R^2$ is neither open nor closed.

\section{A general Polish realization theorem}
\label{pma:cp:sec:general}

\begin{theorem}[Divisible-group realization]
\label{pma:cp:thm:general}
Let $A$ be a divisible Polish abelian group carrying a translation-invariant
total order with Borel positive cone. Define
\begin{equation}
\label{pma:cp:eq:generalmodel}
G_A=A\lex\Z,\qquad
M_A=(A_{>0}\times\Z)\cup(\{0\}\times\N).
\end{equation}
Use coordinatewise addition, $0=(0,0)$, $1=(0,1)$, and the product subspace
topology. Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $M_A$ is a Polish model of $\Th(\N;0,1,+,<)$, and addition is jointly continuous.
\item Every fixed-standard-divisor quotient and remainder map is continuous.
Partial subtraction on its arithmetic domain is continuous.
\item Every relation definable with parameters in a finite Cartesian power
of $M_A$ is $\Delta^0_2$ in the product topology.
\item If $A$ is uncountable, so is $M_A$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The order on $G_A$ is lexicographic with $A$ the more significant
coordinate. Its least positive element is $(0,1)$, and its quotient by
$\{0\}\times\Z$ is the divisible group $A$. Thus it is a $\Z$-group
and $M_A$ is its nonnegative cone.

Theorem~\ref{pma:cp:thm:cones} says that $A_{>0}$ and $A_{\geq0}$ are
$\Delta^0_2$. In the clopen slice $A\times\{k\}$, membership in $M_A$
means $a>0$ if $k<0$ and $a\geq0$ if $k\geq0$. Both $M_A$ and its
complement are therefore countable unions of $F_\sigma$ sets. In
particular $M_A$ is $G_\delta$ in $A\times\Z$, so is Polish.
Coordinatewise addition restricts continuously to it.

An ordered group is torsion-free. Consequently multiplication by a standard
integer $d\geq1$ is a continuous bijective homomorphism on the divisible
Polish group $A$. Its inverse, division by $d$, is continuous. Here is a
short justification of openness. Given a neighborhood $U$ of zero, choose
open $V$ with $V-V\subseteq U$. Countably many translates of $V$ cover
$A$, so countably many translates of $dV$ do as well. The analytic set
$dV$ has the Baire property and is nonmeagre. The difference form of the
category argument then gives a neighborhood of zero inside
$dV-dV\subseteq dU$. Thus multiplication by $d$ is open.

Writing $k=dq+r$, $0\leq r<d$, the desired maps are
\begin{equation}
\label{pma:cp:eq:generaldivision}
Q_d(a,k)=(a/d,q),\qquad R_d(a,k)=(0,r).
\end{equation}
They are restrictions of continuous coordinate maps and take their values
in $M_A$. They give the unique decomposition $x=dQ_d(x)+R_d(x)$.
Partial subtraction is the restriction of the ambient continuous map
$(x,y)\mapsto x-y$ to the subspace where $y\leq x$.

For (iii), consider affine integer-linear expressions on $G_A$. Their
$A$-coordinate is a continuous affine map into $A$, and their
integer coordinate is a continuous map into the discrete group $\Z$.
The nonnegative cone of $G_A$ is $\Delta^0_2$, by the same slice
argument used for $M_A$. Consequently each linear comparison is
$\Delta^0_2$; equalities are closed. Since $A$ is divisible, congruence
modulo $d$ depends only on the integer coordinate modulo $d$, so is
clopen. Classical Presburger quantifier elimination reduces every
parameter-definable relation to a finite Boolean combination of these
atoms. Restricting to $M_A^k$ preserves their complexity.

Finally, if $A$ is uncountable, totality and negation show that $A_{>0}$
is uncountable. It embeds into $M_A$ via $a\mapsto(a,0)$.
\end{proof}

\noindent\writetag{} Part~(ii) of \cref{pma:cp:thm:general} includes, for
$A=\R$ and $A=\Q^{\N}$, the continuity statements of
\cref{pma:pr:prop:selfsimilar} and of source~04's \cref{pma:pr:thm:transfer};
part~(iii) extends \cref{pma:pr:thm:definableborel,pma:pr:thm:delta2} to every
divisible Polish group with a Borel cone. Source~06 proves part~(iii) for its
own families in \cref{pma:cp:thm:delta2}.

\begin{remark}
Only fixed standard divisors occur in \eqref{pma:cp:eq:generaldivision}. A function
taking a \emph{variable} divisor as input belongs to a stronger language.
The theorem also distinguishes continuous partial subtraction from total
truncated subtraction, which will be impossible in an uncountable Polish
presentation.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Definable Borelness for arbitrary continuous presentations}

The special $\Delta^0_2$ estimate uses the ambient Polish group. A weaker
conclusion holds for every Polish Presburger presentation with continuous
addition, whether or not it comes from our construction.

\begin{proposition}
\label{pma:cp:prop:allborel}
Let $M\models\Th(\N;0,1,+,<)$ have a Polish topology making addition continuous.
Every relation definable with parameters is Borel. Every definable partial
function between finite Cartesian powers has Borel domain and is Borel
measurable on its domain.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Equation~\eqref{pma:cp:eq:orderfromaddition} makes $<$ analytic: it is the
projection of the closed equality relation $x+z+1=y$. Its complement
$y\leq x$ is analytic for the same reason. Hence $<$ is Borel by the
analytic--coanalytic characterization of Borel sets.

For a fixed $d\geq1$ and $0\leq r<d$, the map $x\mapsto dx+r$ is
continuous and injective. Its image is Borel by the Borel injective-image
theorem. These $d$ residue classes partition $M$. Congruence relations are
therefore Borel, as are integer-linear comparisons after negative
coefficients have been moved to the other side. Quantifier elimination
now gives every definable relation Borel.

The graph of a definable partial function is a Borel set. Projection from
that graph to its input coordinates is Borel and injective. Its image,
the domain, is Borel, and its inverse is Borel. Composing that inverse
with projection to output coordinates gives the desired function.
\end{proof}

\section{A family of models on Baire space}
\label{pma:cp:sec:baire}

\subsection{The countable-ordinal coefficient groups}

Fix a countably infinite ordinal $\alpha$. Let
\begin{equation}
\label{pma:cp:eq:alphamodel}
A_\alpha=\Q^\alpha,\qquad
G_\alpha=A_\alpha\lex\Z,\qquad
M_\alpha=(G_\alpha)_{\geq0}.
\end{equation}
Here $\Q^\alpha$ is the full Cartesian product, not the finite-support
direct sum. Give each copy of $\Q$ the \emph{discrete} topology.
On $A_\alpha$, the least coordinate at which two vectors differ determines
their lexicographic comparison. Such a coordinate exists because $\alpha$
is well ordered. Addition and division by a nonzero integer are
coordinatewise.

The product topology uses finite coordinate restrictions. In particular,
when $\alpha>\omega$, it does not treat an entire transfinite prefix as
one open condition. Keeping these two uses of coordinates separate is the
main point in the topology proof.

\begin{theorem}[Continuous arithmetic on Baire space; for $\alpha=\omega$, \cref{pma:pr:thm:baire}]
\label{pma:cp:thm:baire}
For every countably infinite ordinal $\alpha$, $M_\alpha$ is a
continuum-sized model of $\Th(\N;0,1,+,<)$ homeomorphic to $\Baire$. Addition,
every quotient and remainder by a fixed positive standard integer, and
partial subtraction are continuous. Every parameter-definable relation in
every finite arity is $\Delta^0_2$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The group $A_\alpha$ is divisible, ordered, and Polish: it is a countable
product of countable discrete groups. Its topology is zero-dimensional.
For each $\beta<\alpha$ define
\[
C_\beta^+=\{a:a_\gamma=0\ (\gamma<\beta),\ a_\beta>0\},\qquad
C_\beta^-=\{a:a_\gamma=0\ (\gamma<\beta),\ a_\beta<0\}.
\]
Each set is closed. The prefix-zero condition is an intersection of
closed coordinate conditions, and the sign condition on a discrete
rational coordinate is clopen. Therefore
\[
(A_\alpha)_{>0}=\bigcup_{\beta<\alpha}C_\beta^+,
\qquad
(A_\alpha)_{<0}=\bigcup_{\beta<\alpha}C_\beta^-
\]
are $F_\sigma$, with $F_\sigma$ complements after adjoining $\{0\}$.
Theorem~\ref{pma:cp:thm:general} now gives Polishness and all the arithmetic
and definability assertions. Cardinality is $\cp$ because the entire
fiber $a_0=1$ lies in the positive cone and has countably infinitely many
free rational coordinates.

It remains to identify the underlying Polish space. We prove that every
nonempty open subset of $M_\alpha$ contains a closed noncompact cylinder.
Let $U$ be nonempty and choose $x=(a,n)\in U$. For some finite
$F\subseteq\alpha$, the ambient cylinder
\[
B=\{(b,n):b_\gamma=a_\gamma\text{ for }\gamma\in F\}
\]
satisfies $x\in B\cap M_\alpha\subseteq U$. Let $j$ be the least
natural number not in $F$. All indices $i<j$ are in $F$.

If the finite prefix $(a_0,\ldots,a_{j-1})$ has a nonzero entry, its first
nonzero entry is positive, since $a\geq0$. Positivity is already forced;
in this case additionally set $b_j=0$. If that prefix is zero, additionally
set $b_j=1$. In both cases the resulting cylinder $C$ fixes only finitely
many coordinates, is nonempty, and has strictly positive $A_\alpha$
coordinate. Thus
\[
C\subseteq B\cap M_\alpha\subseteq U.
\]
The set $C$ is clopen in the ambient product and closed in $M_\alpha$.
Infinitely many rational coordinates remain free. Projection onto any one
of them maps $C$ continuously onto the infinite discrete space $\Q$, so
$C$ is not compact.

If a compact subset $K\subseteq M_\alpha$ had nonempty interior $U$,
the cylinder just constructed would be a closed subset of $K$ and hence
compact, a contradiction. Thus every compact subset has empty interior.
The space is nonempty, zero-dimensional, and Polish. The Baire-space
characterization gives $M_\alpha\cong\Baire$.
\end{proof}

\noindent\writetag{} For $\alpha=\omega$, $M_\omega$ is Part~I's Baire model
$\MB$, and \cref{pma:cp:thm:baire} is \cref{pma:pr:thm:baire}, where an
explicit and computable homeomorphism is given
(\cref{pma:pr:thm:effectiveops,pma:pr:thm:effective}; also source~06's
\cref{pma:cp:s06:thm:baire,pma:cp:thm:effective} below). For $\alpha>\omega$
the theorem is new to the collection, and the homeomorphism above comes from
the topological characterization of Baire space.

\begin{remark}[Why the transfinite cases require the full argument]
For $\alpha=\omega$, both strict sign cones of $A_\alpha$ are open,
since a finite prefix detects each nonzero vector's sign. When
$\alpha>\omega$, a vector first nonzero at $\omega$ is not an interior
point of its sign cone. The closed-piece proof works in both cases.
Likewise, the cylinder argument above covers neighborhoods of such
transfinite-leading vectors and of zero, not just the dense set of vectors
with finite leading coordinate.
\end{remark}

Choose a homeomorphism $h:\Baire\to M_\alpha$ and transport the
arithmetic:
\[
x\oplus y=h^{-1}(h(x)+h(y)),\qquad
0_{\Baire}=h^{-1}(0),\qquad 1_{\Baire}=h^{-1}(1).
\]
The operation $\oplus$ is jointly continuous. The coefficient model is
explicit; the homeomorphism here is supplied by a topological
characterization. We do not claim a computable homeomorphism for an
arbitrary presentation of a countable ordinal.

\subsection{Residue classes as copies of the whole space}

\begin{corollary}[extends \cref{pma:pr:prop:selfsimilar}]
\label{pma:cp:cor:residuehomeo}
For each standard $d\geq1$, the map
\[
x\longmapsto\bigl(Q_d(x),r_d(x)\bigr)
\]
is a homeomorphism $M_A\to M_A\times\{0,\ldots,d-1\}$, where
$R_d(x)=r_d(x)1$. Each residue class is clopen and homeomorphic to $M_A$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Continuity of the forward map follows from
Theorem~\ref{pma:cp:thm:general}. Its inverse is
$(q,r)\mapsto dq+r1$, continuous because the second factor is finite
discrete and addition is continuous. Division with remainder proves that
the maps are inverse.
\end{proof}

\noindent\writetag{} For $M_A=\MR$ and $M_A=\MB$ this is
\cref{pma:pr:prop:selfsimilar}.

This is an arithmetic explanation of a topological self-similarity.
It also applies to $M_{\R}$, whose countably many ray components can be
rearranged into any positive finite number of copies.

\subsection{An uncountable ordinal spectrum of models}

For positive elements $g,h$ of an ordered abelian group, define
\[
g\asymp h\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
(\exists k,\ell\in\N_{>0})\ (g\leq kh\ \text{and}\ h\leq\ell g).
\]
These are the Archimedean equivalence classes. We list the classes in
\emph{decreasing magnitude}: a class occurs earlier when its members
exceed every standard multiple of a member of the later class.

\begin{theorem}[An ordinal spectrum on one topological space]
\label{pma:cp:thm:spectrum}
The Archimedean classes of $G_\alpha$, listed in decreasing magnitude,
have order type $\alpha+1$. Therefore the models $M_\alpha$, for
countably infinite $\alpha$, are pairwise nonisomorphic, even as additive
monoids. There are at least $\aleph_1$ pairwise nonisomorphic
continuum-sized Presburger structures continuously presented on $\Baire$.
No continuum hypothesis is used.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For a positive $(a,n)$ with $a\ne0$, let $\lambda(a,n)$ be the least
$\beta<\alpha$ with $a_\beta\ne0$. For $a=0$, let
$\lambda(0,n)=\alpha$. If two positive elements have the same leading
index $\beta<\alpha$, their leading coefficients are positive rationals.
Each can be made larger than the other by multiplication by a sufficiently
large standard integer; lower coordinates do not affect that comparison.
Thus they are Archimedean equivalent. The same holds in the integer class.

If $\lambda(g)<\lambda(h)$, then $g>kh$ for every standard positive
integer $k$, since the leading coordinate of $g$ is positive while that
coordinate of $kh$ is zero. Every index $\beta\leq\alpha$ occurs.
This proves the asserted class order.

An isomorphism of additive positive monoids extends uniquely to an
isomorphism of their group completions: represent a group element as a
difference of two positives and use cancellation to check independence of
the representation. The extension preserves positivity and hence order.
It preserves the Archimedean class order. Thus an isomorphism between
$M_\alpha$ and $M_\delta$ would imply $\alpha+1=\delta+1$, so
$\alpha=\delta$.

The set of countably infinite ordinals has cardinality $\aleph_1$.
Theorem~\ref{pma:cp:thm:baire} places each corresponding model on Baire space.
\end{proof}

The invariant uses addition. It does not by itself distinguish the pure
underlying linear orders; no classification of those pure orders is claimed.

\subsection{The optimal number of models is the continuum}

The ordinal family gives an uncountable hierarchy with a transparent
invariant. A different parameter reaches the largest possible number of
isomorphism types on a fixed Baire space, without any hypothesis about
the value of the continuum.

\begin{theorem}[Sharp continuum multiplicity]
\label{pma:cp:thm:continuum}
There are exactly $\cp=2^{\aleph_0}$ isomorphism types of Presburger
models continuously presentable on $\Baire$. The lower bound can be
realized with all parameter-definable relations $\Delta^0_2$ and with
continuous quotient and remainder by every fixed standard positive integer.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For an irrational $s\in(1,2)$ let $B_s=\Q+\Q s$, ordered as a subgroup
of $\R$ but given the discrete topology. This is a countable divisible
ordered abelian group. Put
\[
A_s=B_s\lex\Q^\omega,\qquad
G_s=A_s\lex\Z,\qquad M_s=(G_s)_{\geq0}.
\]
The product of the discrete coordinate topologies is Polish. The leading
coordinate test makes the sign cones Borel. Theorem~\ref{pma:cp:thm:general}
gives all the arithmetic and definability properties. The finite-cylinder
proof of Theorem~\ref{pma:cp:thm:baire} applies with $B_s$ as the first
coordinate, so $M_s$ is homeomorphic to Baire space.

The subgroup
\[
H_s=\{0\}\times\Q^\omega\times\Z
\]
is the unique largest proper convex subgroup of $G_s$. Indeed it is
proper and convex. If a convex subgroup contains an element $g$ with
nonzero $B_s$-coordinate, standard multiples of $|g|$ dominate every
element of $G_s$, because $B_s\subseteq\R$ is Archimedean.
Convexity forces such a subgroup to be the whole group. Therefore an
isomorphism $M_s\cong M_t$, extended to group completions, induces an
ordered additive isomorphism $B_s\cong B_t$.

Every increasing additive isomorphism $f:B_s\to B_t$ is multiplication
by a positive real scalar $\lambda$. To see this, let $\lambda=f(1)>0$.
Additivity and divisibility give $f(q)=q\lambda$ for every rational $q$.
For any $x\in B_s$, comparison with every rational shows that
$f(x)/\lambda$ and $x$ have the same rational cut in $\R$, hence are
equal. Surjectivity now supplies rational $a,b,c,d$ with
\[
1=\lambda(c+ds),\qquad t=\lambda(a+bs),
\qquad
t=\frac{a+bs}{c+ds}.
\]
The denominator is nonzero, and $ad-bc\ne0$, since otherwise $t$ would
be rational. Thus for each fixed $s$ only countably many parameters $t$
can yield an isomorphic model. The continuum-sized set of irrational
parameters therefore yields continuum many isomorphism types in ZFC.

For the upper bound, fix $X=\Baire$ and a countable dense set
$D\subseteq X^2$. Since $X$ is Hausdorff, a continuous map $X^2\to X$
is determined by its values on $D$. There are at most
$|X|^{\aleph_0}=\cp$ possible such maps. Choosing finitely many constants
does not increase this bound, and all Presburger relations are determined
by the addition and constants. Consequently there are at most $\cp$
continuously presented structures and at most $\cp$ isomorphism types.
\end{proof}

\noindent\writetag{} Source~06 proves the same count with a different family
and adds that the lower bound is attained with the topology, the arithmetic
order, $0$ and $1$ fixed (\cref{pma:cp:s06:thm:continuum}); source~07's
statement adds the $\Dtwo$ and continuous-division clauses, and
\cref{pma:cp:cor:continuumranks} below adds every oscillation rank. The two
refinements are independent, so both statements are printed.

\subsection{An explicit model on Baire space \src{06}}
\label{pma:cp:s06:sec:baire}

Let $H=\Q^{\N}$, giving every rational coordinate the \emph{discrete}
topology. Order $H$ lexicographically: for a nonzero sequence $h$, its
sign is the sign of its first nonzero coordinate. Put
\[
 G_\omega=H\lex\Z,\qquad M_\omega=(G_\omega)_{\geq0}.
\]
The coordinatewise group $H$ is divisible. It follows at once that
$M_\omega\models\Pres$ and that addition is continuous in the product
subspace topology. The remaining issue is to identify that space.

\begin{theorem}[Baire-space realization \src{06}; = \cref{pma:pr:thm:baire}]
\label{pma:cp:s06:thm:baire}
The space $M_\omega$ is homeomorphic to $\Baire$. Consequently Baire space
supports a model of Presburger arithmetic with jointly continuous
addition.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We give homeomorphisms rather than merely a cardinality argument.
The strict cone $H_{>0}$ is the disjoint union of cylinders
\[
 C_{j,q}=\{h:h(0)=\cdots=h(j-1)=0,\ h(j)=q\},
 \quad j\in\N,\ q\in\Q_{>0}.
\]
Each cylinder is clopen and is homeomorphic to $\Q^{\N}$ by deleting
its fixed prefix. A coordinatewise enumeration of $\Q$ identifies
$\Q^{\N}$ with $\Baire$. A countable disjoint union of Baire spaces
is again Baire space, by using the first coordinate to record the
component. Hence $H_{>0}\cong\Baire$.

The closed cone $H_{\geq0}$ has an equally direct description. Consider
the tree of finite rational words whose first nonzero entry, if present,
is positive. An all-zero word can be extended by any nonnegative
rational; a word already having positive first nonzero entry can be
extended by any rational. Every node therefore has countably infinitely
many successors. Choose a bijection from $\N$ to each successor set.
The resulting rooted-tree isomorphism with $\N^{<\N}$ induces a
homeomorphism of branch spaces $H_{\geq0}\cong\Baire$.

The fixed-$z$ slice of $M_\omega$ is clopen, and equals $H_{>0}$ when
$z<0$ and $H_{\geq0}$ when $z\geq0$. Their countable disjoint union is
therefore Baire space. Transport the arithmetic structure through any
of these explicit homeomorphisms.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{A computable stream presentation \src{06}}

\begin{theorem}[Effective addition \src{06}; = \cref{pma:pr:thm:effectiveops,pma:pr:thm:effective}]
\label{pma:cp:thm:effective}
The homeomorphism in Theorem~\ref{pma:cp:s06:thm:baire} can be chosen computable in
both directions for exact rational-coordinate streams. Under this
homeomorphism, addition on $\Baire$ is Type-2 computable. The constants
$0,1$ and the quotient and remainder maps for each fixed standard divisor
are computable as well.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Fix computable bijections, with computable inverses, between $\N$ and
each of $\Q$, $\Q_{>0}$, $\Q_{\geq0}$, and $\Z$. Canonical reduced
fractions provide such enumerations. Choose the nonnegative enumeration
to send $0$ to $0$. Also fix a computable pairing bijection
$\N\leftrightarrow\N^2$.

For $H_{\geq0}$, decode successive input coordinates using the
nonnegative rational enumeration until the first positive output; after
that, use the enumeration of all rationals. The inverse uses exactly
the same finite-prefix state. Thus each coordinate in either direction
requires only finitely much input.

For $H_{>0}$, decode the first input coordinate as $(j,k)$, emit $j$
zeros and then the $k$th positive rational, and use the remaining input
to produce an arbitrary rational tail. The inverse searches for the
first nonzero coordinate. This search terminates on its promised
domain $H_{>0}$.

Finally, the first coordinate of a Baire input selects $z\in\Z$; the
remaining stream selects a point of $H_{>0}$ or $H_{\geq0}$ according to
the sign of $z$. This defines a computable homeomorphism
\[
 F:\Baire\longrightarrow M_\omega.
\]
Coordinatewise rational addition and integer addition are computable.
Therefore
\[
 a\oplus b=F^{-1}\bigl(F(a)+F(b)\bigr)
\]
is Type-2 computable. If the output integer coordinate is negative, the
output rational sequence is necessarily strictly positive: a sum of
nonnegative ordered-group elements has zero rational part only when
both rational parts vanish, in which case both input integers are
nonnegative. Thus the search used by $F^{-1}$ always terminates when it
is invoked on that branch. Fixed standard division is coordinatewise
rational division together with ordinary integer division, so the same
argument applies to it and to remainders.
\end{proof}

Here ``computable'' concerns functions on infinite streams: every
requested output coordinate is produced after finitely much input has
been inspected. It does not assert a computable enumeration of this
uncountable model or decidability of equality between arbitrary streams.
It therefore has no conflict with the classical countable-presentation
Tennenbaum theorem.

\subsection{How many continuous models fit on Baire space? \src{06}}
\label{pma:cp:sec:abundance}

The existence result has substantial room for variation. The next theorem
counts abstract isomorphism types, not merely different coordinate
descriptions of one model.

\begin{theorem}[Sharp abundance \src{06}; the count of \cref{pma:cp:thm:continuum}]
\label{pma:cp:s06:thm:continuum}
There are exactly $2^{\aleph_0}$ isomorphism types of Presburger models
on Baire space with jointly continuous addition. The lower bound can be
realized while keeping the underlying topology, arithmetic order, $0$,
and $1$ fixed and varying only addition.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $A_0=\Q$ and $A_1=\Q+\Q\sqrt2$, as additive ordered subgroups of
$\R$, and give both groups discrete topology. For $s\in2^{\N}$ put
\[
 H'_s=\prod_{n\in\N} A_{s(n)},\qquad
 G'_s=H'_s\lex\Z,\qquad M'_s=(G'_s)_{\geq0},
\]
using the first differing coordinate for the order on $H'_s$.
Both coefficient groups are countable and divisible. Each has
countably infinitely many positive and nonnegative elements.
The proof of Theorem~\ref{pma:cp:s06:thm:baire}, with its coordinate-dependent
enumerations, therefore applies unchanged. Hence each $M'_s$ is a
Baire-space model with continuous addition.

For positive elements of an ordered abelian group, say $x\asymp y$
if $x\leq my$ and $y\leq nx$ for some positive standard integers $m,n$.
This is Archimedean equivalence. In $G'_s$, two positive elements are
equivalent exactly when they have the same first nonzero coordinate,
or when both are ordinary integers. Indeed, equal-rank real leading
coefficients dominate one another after sufficiently large standard
integer multiplication; an earlier nonzero coordinate dominates every
standard multiple of a later one. The classes, ordered from largest
magnitude to smallest, consequently have order type $\omega+1$.

For each $n$, define the convex subgroup
\[
 U_n=\{(h,z):h(0)=\cdots=h(n-1)=0\}.
\]
It is determined intrinsically by the Archimedean classes of rank at
least $n$, and
\begin{equation}
 U_n/U_{n+1}\cong A_{s(n)}.
 \label{pma:cp:eq:quotient-invariant}
\end{equation}
Any additive-monoid isomorphism $f:M'_s\to M'_t$ extends to an ordered-group
isomorphism of their difference groups by
\[
 \widetilde f(a-b)=f(a)-f(b).
\]
This is well-defined since $a-b=c-d$ means $a+d=c+b$; every signed group
element is a difference of nonnegative ones. The order is preserved
because $a\leq b$ in a cone is equivalent to
$\exists c\,(a+c=b)$.

The ordered set $\omega+1$ has no nontrivial order automorphism, so
$\widetilde f$ must preserve each rank and send $U_n$ to its counterpart.
The quotients in \eqref{pma:cp:eq:quotient-invariant} then recover $s(n)$:
$A_0$ has rational vector-space dimension $1$, whereas $A_1$ has
dimension $2$. An additive isomorphism between divisible torsion-free
groups is automatically $\Q$-linear. Thus $M'_s\cong M'_t$ implies $s=t$.
This gives $2^{\aleph_0}$ distinct isomorphism types.

For the fixed-order refinement, $A_0$ and $A_1$ are countable dense
linear orders without endpoints. A back-and-forth construction gives
an order isomorphism $\theta:A_0\to A_1$ fixing $0$: alternately place
the next unused element of each enumerated order into the appropriate
nonempty interval of the other. Since both topologies are discrete,
$\theta$ is a homeomorphism. Apply either $\theta$ or the identity in
coordinate $n$ according to $s(n)$, and leave the integer coordinate
unchanged. This gives an order-preserving homeomorphism
$M_\omega\to M'_s$ fixing $0,1$. Pulling back all the additions, and then
using one homeomorphism $M_\omega\cong\Baire$, proves the refinement.

Finally, a continuous function ${\Baire}^2\to\Baire$ is determined by its
values on a fixed countable dense subset. There are at most
$(2^{\aleph_0})^{\aleph_0}=2^{\aleph_0}$ such value assignments. Choices
of the two constants do not enlarge this bound, and in Presburger
arithmetic the order is determined by addition. Thus no more than
continuum many isomorphism types are possible.
\end{proof}

The continuous family in this theorem is not asserted to be an
uncountable family of absolutely computable operations. There are only
countably many algorithms. The effective assertion proved above applies
to the particular rational $\omega$-indexed model; computable variations
can be handled separately, and noncomputable sequences $s$ may serve as
oracles.

\subsubsection{Countably transfinite Archimedean scales \src{06}}

There is a different useful variation, relevant to the repository's
interest in ordered groups and ordinal scales.

\begin{theorem}[Prescribed well-ordered scales \src{06}; = \cref{pma:cp:thm:baire,pma:cp:thm:spectrum}]
\label{pma:cp:thm:ordinals}
For every countable ordinal $\alpha\geq\omega$, the positive cone
\[
 M_\alpha=(\Q^\alpha\lex\Z)_{\geq0},
\]
with discrete rational coordinates and the product subspace topology,
is a Baire-space Presburger model with continuous addition. Its
Archimedean classes, in decreasing magnitude, have order type
$\alpha+1$. In particular distinct such ordinals give nonisomorphic
additive models.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $\beta<\alpha$, let
\[
 C_\beta^\pm=\{h:(\forall\gamma<\beta)\ h(\gamma)=0,
                         \ \pm h(\beta)>0\}.
\]
Each set is closed, including at limit indices, since the preceding
zero conditions are closed and the sign condition is clopen in a
discrete coordinate. Both
\[
 M_\alpha=\bigcup_{\beta<\alpha}(C_\beta^+\times\Z)
                  \ \cup\ (\{0\}\times\N)
\]
and its complement in $\Q^\alpha\times\Z$ are $F_\sigma$. Therefore
$M_\alpha$ is $G_\delta$ in a Polish space, hence Polish and
zero-dimensional. Its algebra and addition follow from
Lemma~\ref{pma:cp:lem:split}.

Every nonempty open set in $M_\alpha$ contains a closed copy of
$\Q^{\N}$. To see this, choose a point and a neighborhood fixing its
integer coordinate and finitely many rational coordinates. If the
chosen point first becomes positive at a finite coordinate $j$, fix
all its coordinates through $j$. If it vanishes on every finite
coordinate, choose a finite $j$ beyond all the finitely prescribed
finite indices, put zeros below $j$, and put $1$ at $j$. Keep the other
prescribed coordinates. In either case, the resulting finite cylinder
is clopen in the ambient product, lies inside the positive cone and
the chosen neighborhood, and has countably infinitely many free
coordinates. Hence no compact subset of $M_\alpha$ has nonempty
interior.

For completeness, a nonempty zero-dimensional Polish space $X$ with
this last property is homeomorphic to Baire space. Use a complete
compatible metric. Every nonempty clopen set is complete and
noncompact, and hence is not totally bounded. It therefore has a
countable clopen cover of arbitrarily small diameter with no finite
subcover. Removing earlier members from each member disjointifies it
into countably infinitely many nonempty clopen sets. Repeat within
each piece, with diameters tending to zero. Each branch of the
resulting $\N$-branching tree determines exactly one point by
completeness, and each point determines a branch. The pieces are
clopen, so the resulting bijection in both directions is continuous.
Apply this construction to $M_\alpha$.

Finally, the same leading-coordinate argument as in
Theorem~\ref{pma:cp:s06:thm:continuum} identifies the Archimedean class of a
noninteger element with its least nonzero index $\beta<\alpha$; the
integer class is last. Their ordered quotient is exactly $\alpha+1$.
An additive isomorphism preserves this quotient. Equality of the
successor order types implies equality of $\alpha$ and $\beta$ by
removing their final elements.
\end{proof}

The countable ordinals in this theorem describe \emph{classes of
magnitude}, not an increasing or decreasing $\omega_1$-sequence inside
one arithmetic order. There is therefore no conflict with the
uncountable-monotone-sequence obstruction in \cite{EHW}.

\section{Unbounded oscillation at Borel level two}
\label{pma:cp:sec:oscillation}

The Borel-level conclusion may suggest that all definable sets in these
models have similarly simple topological behavior. A finer invariant shows
that they do not. One fixed Presburger formula already has arbitrarily
high countable boundary oscillation ranks across the models.

\subsection{The derivative and its exact convention}

Let $X$ be a space and $E\subseteq X$. For a closed $F\subseteq X$, put
\[
D_E(F)=\cl_F(E\cap F)\cap\cl_F(F\setminus E).
\]
Starting with $F_0=X$, set
\begin{equation}
\label{pma:cp:eq:oscillationrecursion}
F_{\xi+1}=D_E(F_\xi),\qquad
F_\lambda=\bigcap_{\xi<\lambda}F_\xi
\quad(\lambda>0\text{ a limit}).
\end{equation}
The \emph{boundary oscillation rank} used here is the least $\xi$ with
$F_\xi=\varnothing$, when such a stage exists; otherwise we assign rank
$\infty$. For the characteristic function $\one_E$, this is
the oscillation derivative at any threshold $0<\varepsilon\leq1$.
The definition fixes the indexing convention; no identification with all
possible conventions for difference-hierarchy ranks is intended.

In $A_\alpha$, let $u$ be the vector with $u_0=1$ and $u_\gamma=0$
for $\gamma>0$. Set
\[
p=(u,0),\qquad E_\alpha=\{x\in M_\alpha:x<p\}.
\]
This is the fixed formula $\varphi(x;y)\equiv x<y$, unary in $x$, with
the indicated parameter $p$. Its chosen positive zeroth coordinate ensures
that all boundary perturbations below stay inside the model.

\begin{theorem}[Exact boundary oscillation ranks]
\label{pma:cp:thm:oscillation}
Write the countably infinite ordinal $\alpha$ uniquely as
\[
\alpha=\omega\cdot\beta+n,
\qquad 0<\beta<\omega_1,\quad n<\omega.
\]
For $0<\mu\leq\alpha$, write
\[
C_\mu=\{(a,z)\in M_\alpha:a_\gamma=u_\gamma
\text{ for all }\gamma<\mu\}.
\]
The recursion \eqref{pma:cp:eq:oscillationrecursion} for $E_\alpha$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
\label{pma:cp:eq:exactrank}
F_\xi=C_{\omega\cdot\xi}\quad(1\leq\xi\leq\beta),
\qquad F_{\beta+1}=\varnothing.
\end{equation}
Its rank is exactly $\beta+1$. Consequently this one formula realizes
every countable successor rank at least $2$, and its ranks are unbounded
below $\omega_1$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Suppose first that $x=(a,z)$ differs from $p$ at a finite first rational
coordinate $k<\omega$. Fixing coordinates $0,\ldots,k$ decides its
comparison with $p$ on a neighborhood of $x$. Such a point is not in
$F_1$.

Conversely, suppose that $a_k=u_k$ for every $k<\omega$. Every basic
neighborhood of $x$ fixes only finitely many coordinates. Choose an
unfixed positive natural index $k$, change its value from $0$ to $-1$
or to $1$, and leave everything else unchanged. All earlier coordinates
still agree with $u$. The two resulting points are respectively below and
above $p$, and remain positive because the zeroth coordinate stays $1$.
Thus $F_1=C_\omega$.

For the successor step assume $1\leq\xi<\beta$ and
$F_\xi=C_\mu$, where $\mu=\omega\cdot\xi$. At least $\omega$
coordinates remain before $\alpha$. Inside $C_\mu$, a first residual
difference at $\mu+k$, $k<\omega$, is decided by fixing the finite
residual prefix $\mu,\ldots,\mu+k$. These points leave the derivative.
If a point agrees with $u$ throughout the block
$[\mu,\mu+\omega)$, every basic relative neighborhood leaves some
$\mu+k$ unfixed. Perturbing this coordinate in the two directions gives
points on both sides of $p$, still in $C_\mu$ and in $M_\alpha$.
Therefore
\[
F_{\xi+1}=C_{\mu+\omega}=C_{\omega\cdot(\xi+1)}.
\]

At a nonzero limit $\lambda\leq\beta$, intersecting the prefix
conditions and using continuity of ordinal multiplication in its right
argument gives
\[
F_\lambda=\bigcap_{1\leq\xi<\lambda}C_{\omega\cdot\xi}
=C_{\omega\cdot\lambda}.
\]
At stage $\beta$, only $n$ rational coordinates and the integer coordinate
are free. The resulting fiber is homeomorphic to the discrete space
$\Q^n\times\Z$. Every subset of that fiber is relatively clopen, so
the next derivative is empty. Each earlier displayed stage contains
$\{u\}\times\Z$, proving that no earlier stage is empty.

Taking $\alpha=\omega\cdot\beta$ gives all stated successor ranks.
\end{proof}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ll}
\toprule
Coordinate order $\alpha$ & Boundary oscillation rank of $E_\alpha$\\
\midrule
$\omega$ & $2$\\
$\omega\cdot2$ & $3$\\
$\omega^2$ & $\omega+1$\\
$\omega\cdot(\omega+1)$ & $\omega+2$\\
$\omega^\omega$ & $\omega^\omega+1$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The first derivative removes points where a finite prefix decides the
comparison. Each later derivative removes one further block of $\omega$
coordinates. This is a topological calculation, although the defining
formula itself never changes.

\subsection{Explicit continuous approximations}

There is also a direct approximation of $\one_{E_\alpha}$ by continuous
functions. Choose an enumeration $\gamma_0,\gamma_1,\ldots$ of $\alpha$
and put $S_N=\{\gamma_0,\ldots,\gamma_N\}$. Sort this finite set in
its ordinal order. Define $c_N(a,z)$ to be $1$ exactly when comparison of
the coordinates in $S_N$, followed by the integer coordinate, places
$(a,z)$ below $(u,0)$.

Each $c_N:M_\alpha\to\{0,1\}$ is continuous, because it depends on
finitely many discrete coordinates. If $a\ne u$, its true first
differing coordinate eventually belongs to $S_N$. From that point on,
every $c_N(a,z)$ is the correct answer; coordinates preceding the true
first difference are equal and cannot change it. If $a=u$, every
$c_N(a,z)$ already equals $\one_{\{z<0\}}$. Hence
\[
c_N(x)\longrightarrow\one_{E_\alpha}(x)
\]
with eventual equality at each point.

Thus these characteristic functions are explicitly Baire class $1$,
while their boundary oscillation ranks have no countable uniform bound
across the family. Low Borel level and finite formula length do not give
a uniform bound on this finer topological invariant.

\begin{corollary}[Optimal multiplicity at every specified rank]
\label{pma:cp:cor:continuumranks}
For every countable $\beta>0$, there are $\cp$ pairwise nonisomorphic
continuous Presburger models on Baire space with a parameter $p$ for which
the cut $x<p$ has boundary oscillation rank $\beta+1$. All their definable
relations are $\Delta^0_2$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
In Theorem~\ref{pma:cp:thm:continuum}, replace the tail $\Q^\omega$ by
$\Q^{\omega\cdot\beta}$ and choose $p=(1,0,0)$. The model remains
Polish and homeomorphic to Baire space by the same closed-cylinder proof.
An isomorphism still induces an isomorphism of the greatest Archimedean
quotients $B_s$, so the continuum lower bound is unchanged.

For the cut at $p$, comparison at the discrete $B_s$-coordinate is locally
decided unless that coordinate equals $1$. On that fiber, the derivative
removes successive blocks of $\omega$ rational coordinates, exactly as
in Theorem~\ref{pma:cp:thm:oscillation}. The first derivative fixes the
$B_s$-coordinate to $1$ and the first $\omega$ tail coordinates to zero;
the $\xi$th derivative fixes the first $\omega\cdot\xi$ tail
coordinates. The $\beta$th derivative is the discrete integer fiber and
the next is empty. The rank is $\beta+1$.
\end{proof}

\section{What continuity permits and forbids}
\label{pma:cp:sec:obstructions}

\subsection{The arithmetic order is not the Polish topology}

In any Presburger model, the intrinsic order topology is discrete.
For $a>0$, the arithmetic interval $(a-1,a+1)$ is the singleton $\{a\}$;
at zero the initial interval $[0,1)$ is $\{0\}$.
An uncountable discrete space is not second countable and hence not Polish.
Accordingly, a positive answer to Glazer's question must distinguish
continuity of addition from compatibility with the intrinsic order topology.

In $M_{\R}$,
\[
x_j=(1/j,0)\longrightarrow(0,0),
\qquad x_j>(0,k)\quad\text{for every standard }k.
\]
Topological convergence to zero is entirely compatible with being larger
than every standard numeral. In $M_\alpha$, let $v_j$ be the coordinate
unit vector at the natural index $j$. Then the analogous sequence
$(v_j,0)$ converges to zero and exceeds every standard numeral.

These sequences show explicitly that strict order is neither open nor
closed. The ordered pair $(0,1)$ belongs to $<$ but is approached by
$(x_j,1)$, which do not. The pairs $(1,x_j)$ belong to $<$ and converge
to $(1,0)$, which does not. The following general obstruction explains why
this behavior is unavoidable.

\begin{proposition}[Closed arithmetic order forces discreteness; = \cref{pma:pr:thm:orderoptimal}]
\label{pma:cp:prop:closedorder}
Let $M$ be a Hausdorff model of $\Th(\N;0,1,+,<)$ with continuous successor.
If $\leq$ is closed in $M^2$, then the topology of $M$ is discrete.
If $M$ is uncountable Polish with continuous addition, neither $<$ nor
$\leq$ is open or closed.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
If $\leq$ is closed, then $<$ is open because its complement is the
reverse non-strict order. Sections of $<$ are open. The singleton
intervals just described are therefore open, so the topology is discrete.

If $<$ is open, the same singleton argument applies. If $<$ is closed,
then $x\leq y\Longleftrightarrow x<y+1$ and continuity of successor
make $\leq$ closed. If $\leq$ is open, then
$x<y\Longleftrightarrow x+1\leq y$ makes $<$ open. Each of these four
possibilities forces discreteness. An uncountable second-countable space
cannot be discrete.
\end{proof}

\noindent\writetag{} \Cref{pma:cp:prop:closedorder} is \cref{pma:pr:thm:orderoptimal}
(source~05), and its first sentence also follows from source~04's
\cref{pma:pr:thm:closedorder}, which needs no continuity of successor. The
monus clause of \cref{pma:cp:cor:orderoperations} is source~04's
\cref{pma:pr:cor:monusobstruction}; the $\min$ and $\max$ clauses are new.

\begin{corollary}[Three individually forbidden operations]
\label{pma:cp:cor:orderoperations}
On an uncountable Polish Presburger model with continuous addition, none
of the following operations can be continuous:
\[
\min(x,y),\qquad \max(x,y),\qquad
x\mathbin{\monus}y=\max\{x-y,0\}.
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
In a Hausdorff space equalizers of continuous maps are closed. Each of
the three operations, if continuous, would make $\leq$ closed through
one of the equivalences
\[
x\leq y\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \min(x,y)=x
\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \max(x,y)=y
\ \Longleftrightarrow\ x\mathbin{\monus}y=0.
\]
Apply Proposition~\ref{pma:cp:prop:closedorder}.
\end{proof}

This does not contradict continuity of partial subtraction: its domain is
the subspace of pairs with $y\leq x$, and it is not extended across that
domain's boundary by assigning the value zero.

\subsection{Compactness remains impossible}

The following obstruction is established in the earlier work of Enayat,
Hamkins, and Wcis\l{}o \cite{EHW}. We include the short jointly continuous
case to locate our locally compact example relative to compact spaces.

\begin{proposition}[= \cref{pma:pr:cor:compact}]
\label{pma:cp:prop:compact}
No infinite compact Hausdorff space supports a Presburger model with
jointly continuous addition. In particular, Cantor space does not.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The set
\[
C=\{(x,y,z)\in M^3:x+z=y\}
\]
is closed and compact. Its projection onto $(x,y)$ is $\leq$, so this
relation is compact and closed in the Hausdorff space $M^2$.
Proposition~\ref{pma:cp:prop:closedorder} makes $M$ discrete, contradicting
compactness and infinitude.
\end{proof}

\noindent\writetag{} This is \cref{pma:pr:cor:compact} of Part~I, there with
the compact-cancellative-monoid proof.

The model $M_{\R}$ is locally compact but not compact. Its separated
ray components and its incomplete ambient-metric ends present no conflict
with this obstruction.

\subsection{A second topology makes predecessor continuous}

In the topology of Theorem~\ref{pma:cp:thm:real}, total truncated predecessor
\[
\operatorname{pred}(x)=
\begin{cases}0,&x=0,\\x-1,&x>0\end{cases}
\]
is discontinuous at zero: $x_j=(1/j,0)\to0$, whereas
$\operatorname{pred}(x_j)=(1/j,-1)$ cannot converge to zero.
That particular discontinuity is not forced by additive arithmetic.

\begin{proposition}
\label{pma:cp:prop:isolatedstandard}
The same algebraic model $M_{\R}$ admits a second Polish topology making
addition, successor, and truncated predecessor continuous. Its isolated
points are exactly the standard numerals.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Use the topological disjoint sum
\[
M_{\R}^{\sharp}
=\N_{\mathrm{discrete}}\ \sqcup\
\bigl((0,\infty)\times\Z_{\mathrm{discrete}}\bigr),
\]
with the same underlying identification $(0,n)\leftrightarrow n$.
It is a countable sum of Polish spaces. Standard points are isolated,
and nonstandard points lie in open real rays, so have no isolated points.

The product splits into four clopen pieces according to whether each
input is standard. On two standard inputs addition is a map on a discrete
space. On a mixed piece it is, on each standard slice, translation of
the integer coordinate. On two nonstandard inputs it is ordinary
coordinate addition on positive real coordinates. Each restriction is
continuous, and a sum involving a nonstandard input remains nonstandard.
Hence addition is continuous on the whole product.

Successor and truncated predecessor act on the standard discrete part
as the familiar maps, and on the nonstandard part as translations by
$\pm1$ of the integer coordinate. They are continuous on the clopen
pieces and therefore globally.
\end{proof}

\noindent\mergetag{} Under the identification $(a,n)\mapsto aX+n$ of
\cref{pma:sec:boundary}, $M_{\R}^{\sharp}$ is exactly the clopen stratum
$(C_{\R},\tau_{\FS})$ of 03's cone in \cref{pma:prop:stratum}, whose parts
(iii) and (iv) found the same topology with continuous addition;
\cref{pma:cp:prop:isolatedstandard} adds that successor and truncated
predecessor are continuous. It is also the subspace of polynomials of degree
at most one in source~06's cone (\cref{pma:cp:prop:meet}). Part~I does not
ask whether the discontinuity of predecessor in \cref{pma:pr:prop:pred} is
forced; this proposition shows that it depends on the topology.

The two presentations are not homeomorphic: one has no isolated points
and the other has countably many. Binary truncated subtraction still
cannot be continuous in the second presentation, by
Corollary~\ref{pma:cp:cor:orderoperations}.

\subsection{The explicit models have no ordered-semiring expansion}

Our examples are subject to an algebraic obstruction stronger than a
mere failure of Borel regularity of multiplication.

\begin{proposition}[Cofinal multiples obstruct multiplication; compare \cref{pma:pr:thm:no_semiring}]
\label{pma:cp:prop:nomultiplication}
Suppose $M\models\Th(\N;0,1,+,<)$ has an element $c$ such that
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $c>n1$ for every standard $n\in\N$;
\item the standard multiples $\{nc:n\in\N\}$ are cofinal in $M$.
\end{enumerate}
Then there is no multiplication, with the given addition and identity
$1$, making $M$ the nonnegative cone of a discretely ordered ring.
More generally, no semiring multiplication can satisfy distributivity,
the given identity, and monotonicity under this arithmetic order.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Suppose such a multiplication exists. By cofinality choose a standard
$k$ with $c^2<kc$; increasing a weak cofinal bound by one gives a strict
bound. Since $c>(k+1)1$, additive order gives
$c=(k+1)1+u$ for some $u>0$. Distributivity and nonnegativity of products
give
\[
c^2=(k+1)c+uc\geq(k+1)c>kc,
\]
a contradiction.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
\label{pma:cp:cor:noPA}
Neither $M_{\R}$ nor any $M_\alpha$ has a Peano-arithmetic expansion
with the given addition, regardless of the regularity of multiplication.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For $M_{\R}$ take $c=(1,0)$. For $M_\alpha$ take $c=(u,0)$ with
$u_0=1$ and all other coordinates zero. In each case $c$ exceeds every
standard numeral. Given any element, choose a standard integer larger
than its leading real or rational coordinate; that multiple of $c$
strictly exceeds the element. Proposition~\ref{pma:cp:prop:nomultiplication}
applies.
\end{proof}

This proof shows exactly why the additive construction cannot be promoted
to a model of full arithmetic by selecting a sufficiently irregular
product. It also leaves room for studying other additive models, without
the cofinal-multiple property, against Glazer's more general topological
obstructions.

\noindent\writetag{} \Cref{pma:cp:prop:nomultiplication} is a weaker form of
\cref{pma:pr:thm:no_semiring}, which needs neither monotonicity nor
commutativity; \cref{pma:cp:cor:noPA} applies it to the whole ordinal family.
Source~06's \cref{pma:cp:lem:unit,pma:cp:thm:nomult} remove the remaining
hypothesis that the multiplicative unit be the named $1$.

\subsection{Definable regularity and an algebraic obstruction \src{06}}
\label{pma:cp:sec:regularity}

\subsubsection{All additive definable relations are low in the Borel hierarchy \src{06}}

\begin{theorem}[Definable relations \src{06}; extends \cref{pma:pr:thm:definableborel}]
\label{pma:cp:thm:delta2}
In $M_{\R}$, in every $M_\alpha$ above, and in every $M'_s$ of
Theorem~\ref{pma:cp:s06:thm:continuum}, every first-order Presburger-definable
relation with parameters is $\boldsymbol\Delta^0_2$ in the corresponding
finite product topology. Fixed-standard-modulus residue classes are
clopen.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
In the ambient group $\R\lex\Z$, the nonnegative cone and its
complement are each a union of an open real-coordinate condition and
a closed zero-coordinate condition. Thus the cone is
$\boldsymbol\Delta^0_2$. For the countable rational and mixed-coefficient
products, the decompositions by first nonzero index used above give
the same conclusion. Equality is closed in the ambient Hausdorff
topological group.

Every integer-linear affine map from a finite power of the ambient
group to that group is continuous. Consequently its comparison with
zero defines a $\boldsymbol\Delta^0_2$ set. Congruence modulo $n$
depends only on the discrete final integer coordinate, because the
other factor is divisible. Such congruences are clopen, including
for affine linear combinations and parameters.

Apply the quantifier-elimination conclusion of
Lemma~\ref{pma:cp:lem:zgroup}. A formula on the nonnegative cone is translated
to the group by restricting its quantified variables to that cone.
Elimination produces a finite Boolean combination of the described
atomic conditions. Restrict it back to the cone and use closure of
$\boldsymbol\Delta^0_2$ under finite Boolean operations.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Graphs versus functions \src{06}]
Low Borel complexity of a definable graph does not imply continuity
of its function. In the real example the truncated predecessor
\[
 p(x)=\begin{cases}0,&x=0,\\x-1,&x>0\end{cases}
\]
is definable but discontinuous at zero: $(1/k,0)\to0$, whereas
$p(1/k,0)=(1/k,-1)$ stays in a different integer slice from $p(0)$.
In the Baire model, let $e_k$ be the rational sequence with $1$ in
coordinate $k$ and zeros elsewhere. Then $(e_k,0)\to0$ gives the
identical obstruction.

The order relation itself is neither open nor closed in these two
basic models. Write $x_k=(e_k,0)$ in the Baire model and
$x_k=(1/k,0)$ in the real model. In both cases $x_k>1$ and $x_k\to0$.
Thus the pairs $(x_k,1)$ lie outside the relation $<$ but converge to
$(0,1)$ inside it. The pairs $(1,x_k)$ lie inside $<$ but converge to
$(1,0)$ outside it.
\end{remark}

The standard cut $\{0\}\times\N$ is closed in these models and is
not definable, even with parameters, in the Presburger language. If it
were defined by $\varphi(x,a)$, it would contain zero and be closed
under successor, while omitting nonstandard elements. The induction
instance for $\varphi$ would then fail. This supplies a concrete
distinction between topological visibility and first-order definability.

\subsubsection{Why the lexicographic models cannot acquire multiplication \src{06}}

The failure of multiplication for our first examples is stronger than
the absence of a continuous or Borel multiplication.

\begin{lemma}[The unit is forced \src{06}]
\label{pma:cp:lem:unit}
Suppose a nontrivial Presburger additive monoid $M$, with least positive
element $e$, is given any unital semiring multiplication, with the same
addition and zero. Then its multiplicative identity is $e$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The natural order $x\leq y\iff\exists z\,(x+z=y)$ is preserved by
multiplication on either side, by distributivity. Write $u$ for the
multiplicative identity. It is nonzero, so $e\leq u$ and
$0\leq e^2\leq eu=e$. By discreteness, $e^2$ is either $0$ or $e$.
If $e^2=0$, use division by two in the Presburger monoid to write
$u=2q+re$, where $r=0$ or $1$. Multiplication by $e$ gives
$e=ue=2(qe)$, impossible for the least positive element. Therefore
$e^2=e$. If $u>e$, write $u=e+v$ with $v\geq e$. Then
$e=ue=e^2+ve\geq2e$, again impossible. Thus $u=e$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[No semiring expansion \src{06}; compare \cref{pma:pr:thm:no_semiring}]
\label{pma:cp:thm:nomult}
None of $M_{\R}$, the nontrivial $M_\alpha$, or the $M'_s$ admits a
unital semiring multiplication with its prescribed additive monoid.
This remains true without imposing any topology or prescribing the
multiplicative identity in advance.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
In each example choose $a$ with leading coordinate $1$ in the first
divisible coordinate and zero elsewhere. Then $a>n\cdot1$ for every
standard $n$. On the other hand, for every $b\in M$ there is a standard
$N$ with $b<Na$, since an ordinary integer can be chosen larger than
the real leading coefficient of $b$. Thus standard multiples of $a$
are cofinal in $M$.

Any proposed multiplication has identity $1$ by
Lemma~\ref{pma:cp:lem:unit}. Since $a\geq n\cdot1$, distributivity and the
natural order imply $a^2\geq na$ for every standard $n$. Taking $b=a^2$
in cofinality gives $a^2<Na$ for some standard $N$, a contradiction.
\end{proof}

The existing repository report contains a more general obstruction to
PA expansions of nonstandard split $\Z$-groups, using residue types
\cite{s06:RepoGroups}. The argument here has a different and stronger
conclusion for the displayed groups: their largest Archimedean
magnitude class already rules out every unital semiring structure.
The next example escapes this obstruction by having polynomial degrees
of arbitrarily large magnitude.

\section{A Polish semiring with full additive induction \src{06}}
\label{pma:cp:sec:polynomial}

\subsection{The ring and its order \src{06}}

Let
\[
 A=\Z+t\R[t]
 =\left\{m+\sum_{i=1}^d a_i t^i:
                     m\in\Z,\ a_i\in\R,\ d<\omega\right\}.
\]
The indeterminate $t$ is ordered as positive infinite: a nonzero
polynomial is positive exactly when its leading coefficient is positive.
This is an ordered subring of $\R[t]$ under its leading-term order.
It is discrete because a positive constant in $A$ is a positive integer,
while every positive nonconstant polynomial exceeds every integer.
Put $P=A_{\geq0}$.

Algebraically, $A/\Z\cong t\R[t]$ is a divisible ordered abelian
group. Thus $P$ is a model of $\Pres$ as an additive structure, and its
inherited polynomial multiplication makes it a model of $\PAminus$.
This underlying polynomial construction has classical precedents and
occurs explicitly in the repository \cite{CookPitassi,s06:RepoPolynomial}.
Our task is to choose and verify a topology.

\subsection{The topology by degree and constant coefficient \src{06}}

For $d\geq1$ and $m\in\Z$, let
\[
 P_{d,m}=\left\{m+a_1t+\cdots+a_dt^d:a_d>0\right\}.
\]
Give $P_{d,m}$ the coefficient topology
$\R^{d-1}\times(0,\infty)$, and make each $n\in\N$ a singleton
component. Define $P$ to be the topological disjoint union
\begin{equation}
 P=\N_{\mathrm{discrete}}\ \sqcup\
          \bigsqcup_{d\geq1,\,m\in\Z}P_{d,m}.
 \label{pma:cp:eq:poly-topology}
\end{equation}
In particular, convergence within a nonconstant stratum eventually has
fixed degree and constant coefficient.

\begin{theorem}[A continuous weak arithmetic semiring \src{06}]
\label{pma:cp:thm:polynomial}
The space in \eqref{pma:cp:eq:poly-topology} is uncountable, locally compact,
and Polish. Both polynomial addition and polynomial multiplication are
jointly continuous on it. Its additive reduct satisfies full Presburger
arithmetic, and its semiring structure satisfies $\PAminus$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Each $P_{d,m}$ is homeomorphic to $\R^d$ by applying $\log$ to the
positive leading coefficient. The countably many components in
\eqref{pma:cp:eq:poly-topology} are locally compact Polish spaces. A complete
metric is obtained by bounding complete component metrics by $1$ and
putting distance $2$ between components. Countable dense sets in the
components give separability. The algebraic assertions were verified
above.

It remains to prove joint continuity rather than only separate
continuity. Products of components form an open cover of $P^2$.
On $P_{d,m}\times P_{e,n}$, positivity of both leading coefficients
prevents cancellation in addition, so
\[
 \deg(p+q)=\max(d,e),\qquad (p+q)(0)=m+n.
\]
Thus this whole product maps into the fixed component
$P_{\max(d,e),m+n}$, and the coefficient map there is linear and
continuous.

On the same product,
\[
 \deg(pq)=d+e,\qquad (pq)(0)=mn,
\]
and the leading coefficient is a positive product. It maps into the
fixed component $P_{d+e,mn}$, with continuous polynomial coefficient
maps. If one input is a positive standard integer, its component is a
singleton and the corresponding operations are coefficient translation
or scalar multiplication. If an input is zero, multiplication is
constant on its open singleton component. Pairs of standard integers
are handled by singleton products. This proves continuity on every
member of an open cover of $P^2$, hence joint continuity globally.
\end{proof}

This theorem concerns the nonnegative cone. The analogous topology
on all signed polynomials would not make the entire ring a topological
ring: cancellation can reduce degree. Even partial subtraction on the
cone fails continuity. For instance
\[
 ((1+1/k)t,t)\longrightarrow(t,t),\qquad
 (1+1/k)t-t=(1/k)t\not\longrightarrow0,
\]
because zero is isolated. This is consistent with the continuity just
proved for addition and multiplication of nonnegative elements.

\subsection{An exact failed induction instance \src{06}}

\begin{theorem}[Failure of open induction \src{06}]
\label{pma:cp:thm:openfailure}
In the polynomial model $P$, the quantifier-free formula
\[
 \varphi(x;t)\ \equiv\ x^2<t
\]
defines exactly the standard natural numbers. Consequently $P$ fails
$\IOpen$, despite satisfying every first-order additive induction
instance.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
If $x=n\in\N$, then $n^2<t$. If $x$ is a positive nonconstant
polynomial of degree $d\geq1$, then $x^2$ has degree $2d\geq2$ and
positive leading coefficient, so $x^2>t$. Therefore
$\varphi(P;t)=\N$. It contains zero and is closed under successor,
but omits $t$. This falsifies the open-induction instance with
parameter $t$.
\end{proof}

The full omnific integer cone and the polynomial subring are different
arithmetic structures. This counterexample concerns the subring
$\Z+t\R[t]$ only; it does not transfer to the full omnific integer
cone simply because the subring embeds there.

\begin{proposition}[Additive definability in the polynomial topology \src{06}]
Every relation definable with parameters in the Presburger reduct of
$P$ is $\boldsymbol\Delta^0_2$. Quotient and remainder by each fixed
standard divisor are continuous.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
On a fixed product of coefficient components, an affine
integer-linear expression has bounded degree and continuous
coefficient functions, with fixed integer constant coefficient.
Its positive and negative sign conditions are finite unions of sets
where all higher coefficients vanish and a specified coefficient has
the required sign; the residual constant case is also included.
These sets and their complements are $F_\sigma$ on each component.
There are countably many clopen product components, so the conditions
are $\boldsymbol\Delta^0_2$ globally. Congruences depend only on the
integer constant term and are clopen. Apply Presburger elimination.

For standard $n\geq1$, division sends the nonconstant coefficients
$a_i$ to $a_i/n$ and the constant term $m$ to $\lfloor m/n\rfloor$;
the positive leading coefficient preserves degree. Remainder is the
standard integer $m\bmod n$. These maps are continuous on each clopen
component, hence on all of $P$.
\end{proof}

\section{Zero must be isolated when both operations are continuous \src{06}}
\label{pma:cp:sec:zero}

The preceding polynomial example has isolated points, whereas our
additive examples are perfect. There is an exact structural reason
for this contrast. The essential subset-sum and probability method
below comes from Glazer's proof of his first theorem \cite{Glazer}.
An audit of its assumptions removes the arithmetic induction
hypothesis. We also avoid any need to prove injectivity of the
subset-sum limit map.

\begin{theorem}[Isolated zero \src{06}]
\label{pma:cp:thm:zero}
Let $A$ be a discretely ordered commutative unital ring, and put
$M=A_{\geq0}$. If $M$ carries a Polish topology under which its
restricted addition and multiplication are jointly continuous, then
$0$ is isolated.
\end{theorem}

\begin{corollary}[\src{06}]
\label{pma:cp:cor:perfect}
No perfect Polish space supports a topological model of $\PAminus$
with continuous addition and multiplication. In particular Baire
space supports the additive models constructed above, but cannot
support such a weak-arithmetic semiring structure.
\end{corollary}

\subsection{Borel order from continuous addition \src{06}}

We first record a point that prevents any hidden regularity assumption.
For $a,b\in M$,
\begin{align*}
 a<b&\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
       \exists c\in M\ (b=a+c+1),\\
 a\geq b&\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
       \exists c\in M\ (a=b+c).
\end{align*}
The equations define closed subsets of $M^3$, because addition is
continuous and $M$ is Hausdorff. Their projections are analytic.
They are complementary relations, so the analytic/coanalytic
separation theorem gives that the order is Borel \cite{Kechris}. This argument
uses no order-topology compatibility.

\subsection{A uniformly convergent subset-sum map \src{06}}

Suppose, toward a contradiction, that $0$ is not isolated. Since
$[0,5]=\{0,1,\ldots,5\}$ and finite sets are closed, one can choose
$x_k>5$ with $x_k\to0$. Fix a complete compatible metric $d$ on $M$.
Set $y_0=1$. After choosing $y_0,\ldots,y_{n-1}$, choose $k(n)$ so
large that, with
\[
 y_n=x_{k(n)}y_{n-1},
\]
we have
\begin{equation}
 d(s,s+y_n)<2^{-n}
 \quad\text{for every subset sum }s
       \text{ of }y_0,\ldots,y_{n-1}.
 \label{pma:cp:eq:small-increments}
\end{equation}
There are only finitely many such sums. For each, continuity implies
$s+x_k y_{n-1}\to s$, so a common choice exists. At the same time,
ordered-ring positivity gives
\begin{equation}
 y_n>5y_{n-1}>0. \label{pma:cp:eq:growth}
\end{equation}

For $r\in\Cantor$, define
\[
 \sigma_N(r)=\sum_{i<N} r(i)y_i.
\]
Each $\sigma_N$ is continuous, since it depends on finitely many bits.
For $N\geq1$ the distance between its next two partial sums is at
most $2^{-N}$, uniformly in $r$. Thus $\sigma_N$ converges uniformly
to a continuous function $r\mapsto z_r\in M$. Completeness is used
at precisely this point.

If $r,s$ differ only at finitely many positions, continuous addition
implies the following \emph{algebraic} identity in $A$:
\begin{equation}
 z_s-z_r=\sum_i(s(i)-r(i))y_i.
 \label{pma:cp:eq:finiteflip}
\end{equation}
For justification, add the finite removed terms to the appropriate
sides of the finite partial-sum identity, then pass to the limit
using addition only. Rearranging the resulting equality is an
algebraic operation in $A$. No topology on $A$ and no continuity of
subtraction are being assumed.

Let $\bar r$ denote coordinatewise bit complementation and set
\[
 \Delta_r=z_r-z_{\bar r}
\]
as notation for an element of the algebraic ring. Equation
\eqref{pma:cp:eq:finiteflip} yields
\begin{equation}
 \Delta_s-\Delta_r=2\sum_i(s(i)-r(i))y_i
 \label{pma:cp:eq:deltachange}
\end{equation}
whenever $r,s$ differ finitely.

\subsection{Bounded complementary differences have probability zero \src{06}}

Give $\Cantor$ fair independent product measure $\mu$. Put
$S_j=\sum_{i<j}y_i$. External finite induction, using
\eqref{pma:cp:eq:growth}, gives
\begin{equation}
 4S_j<y_j\qquad(j\in\N).
 \label{pma:cp:eq:sumgrowth}
\end{equation}
Indeed it holds for $j=0$, and
$4S_{j+1}=4S_j+4y_j<5y_j<y_{j+1}$.

For fixed $n$ consider
\[
 E_n=\{r:|\Delta_r|\leq y_n\}.
\]
This is a Borel set. To see this without treating $\Delta$ as a map
