into a topological ring, write its definition as the conjunction
\[
 z_r\leq z_{\bar r}+y_n,
 \qquad z_{\bar r}\leq z_r+y_n.
\]
All terms are continuous $M$-valued maps and the order is Borel.

Freeze all coordinates except $n+1,\ldots,n+k$. If two assignments
$r,s$ on these coordinates differ last at $j>n$, then
\eqref{pma:cp:eq:deltachange} and \eqref{pma:cp:eq:sumgrowth} imply
\[
 |\Delta_s-\Delta_r|
 \geq2(y_j-S_j)>2y_n.
\]
For the last inequality, $S_j\geq y_n$ and $y_j>4S_j$.
Two values lying in the ordered interval $[-y_n,y_n]$ cannot be
this far apart. Thus at most one of the $2^k$ assignments in each
fiber lies in $E_n$. Integrating over the frozen coordinates gives
\[
 \mu(E_n)\leq2^{-k}
\]
for every $k$, and hence $\mu(E_n)=0$. Therefore
\begin{equation}
 C=\bigcap_{n\in\N}\{r:|\Delta_r|>y_n\}
 \label{pma:cp:eq:conull}
\end{equation}
is Borel and has measure one. In particular, complementary subset
sums are unequal almost surely. No injectivity of $r\mapsto z_r$
is needed.

\subsection{The sign is a tail event \src{06}}

If $r,s$ differ only at positions at most $j$, write
$b=\Delta_s-\Delta_r$. Equations \eqref{pma:cp:eq:deltachange} and
\eqref{pma:cp:eq:sumgrowth} give
\[
 |b|\leq2S_{j+1}<3y_j.
\]
For an arbitrary $n$, take $m=\max(n,j)+1$. Then
\[
 y_n+|b|<4y_{m-1}<y_m.
\]
If $r\in C$, it follows that
$|\Delta_s|\geq|\Delta_r|-|b|>y_n$ and that $\Delta_s$ has the same
sign as $\Delta_r$. Since $n$ was arbitrary, $C$ and the sign on $C$
are invariant under every finite change of coordinates.

Consequently the Borel event
\[
 B=\{r\in C:z_{\bar r}<z_r\}
\]
belongs to the tail sigma algebra. Such an event has probability
zero or one. One quick proof is that it is independent of the first
$N$ coordinates for every $N$; a monotone-class argument makes it
independent of the full sigma algebra, and in particular of itself.
Thus $\mu(B)=\mu(B)^2$.

But $r\mapsto\bar r$ preserves measure and $C$, and reverses the sign
of $\Delta_r$. Since $C$ has measure one, it follows that
$\mu(B)=1/2$, a contradiction. This proves
Theorem~\ref{pma:cp:thm:zero}.\hfill$\square$

\begin{remark}[Exactly what the proof uses \src{06}]
The proof uses the ordered-ring laws, discreteness, separability,
complete metrizability, continuity, and ordinary external finite
induction. It does not use induction \emph{inside} $M$, standard
division in $M$, internal exponentiation, saturation, or an
integer-part theorem. It concludes that zero is isolated. It does
not by itself imply that every standard numeral is isolated, since
predecessor has not been assumed continuous.
\end{remark}

\section{Surreal and omnific realizations}
\label{pma:cp:sec:surreal}

\subsection{The real model as an omnific subgroup}

The most direct connection to the repository is the map
\begin{equation}
\label{pma:cp:eq:realomnific}
J_{\R}:G_{\R}\longrightarrow\No,
\qquad J_{\R}(r,n)=r\omega+n.
\end{equation}
Here multiplication and addition on the right are surreal field operations.
Conway normal forms compare the coefficient of the dominant monomial
$\omega$ before the constant coefficient. Thus $J_{\R}$ is an
ordered additive isomorphism onto $\R\omega+\Z$.

An omnific integer has a Conway normal form with no negative exponents
and with integer constant coefficient. This description places
$\R\omega+\Z$ inside $\Oz$. The standard normal-form facts used here
and below are recalled in \cite{BerarducciMantova}; a general treatment
of initial ordered subgroups appears in \cite{EhrlichKaplan}.

Transport the topology of $M_{\R}$ through $J_{\R}$. The nonnegative
part of $\R\omega+\Z$ becomes an uncountable Polish additive model
with continuous addition. This is a topology on a \emph{set-sized
substructure}, described by its coefficients. It is not the intrinsic
surreal order topology.

For example, in this transported topology,
\[
\frac{\omega}{j}\longrightarrow0,
\]
although $\omega/j$ exceeds every real number for every positive standard
$j$. A coefficient limit is not a claim about order convergence of
surreal numbers.

\subsection{Normal forms for the entire ordinal family}

\begin{theorem}[Explicit omnific embedding; extends \cref{pma:pr:thm:omnific}]
\label{pma:cp:thm:omnific}
Fix a countably infinite ordinal $\alpha$. Regard each ordinal
$\beta+1$ as a surreal number and put
\[
\varepsilon_\beta=\frac{1}{\beta+1}\qquad(\beta<\alpha).
\]
Then
\begin{equation}
\label{pma:cp:eq:normalformmap}
J_\alpha(a,n)
=\sum_{\beta<\alpha}a_\beta\omega^{\varepsilon_\beta}+n
\end{equation}
is a well-defined ordered additive embedding of $G_\alpha$ into $\Oz$,
sending $(0,1)$ to $1$. Its nonnegative image, with transported coefficient
topology, has all the properties of Theorem~\ref{pma:cp:thm:baire} and
Theorem~\ref{pma:cp:thm:oscillation}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The positive surreal exponents $\varepsilon_\beta$ are strictly
decreasing: ordinal inclusion agrees with surreal order, and reciprocation
reverses strict order on positive elements of a field. Every subset of
$\{\varepsilon_\beta:\beta<\alpha\}$ has a largest element,
corresponding to its least index. Thus every support in
\eqref{pma:cp:eq:normalformmap} is a set-sized reverse well order, as required
for a Conway normal form. The sum denotes this formal normal form,
not a limit in an unspecified topology.

Uniqueness of coefficients proves injectivity. Addition of normal forms
with a common set of monomials is coefficientwise, proving additivity.
For unequal vectors, the least differing $\beta$ supplies the largest
monomial in their difference, so determines its sign. If all rational
coordinates agree, the integer constant does. This proves order
preservation and reflection.

All displayed exponents are positive, and the constant coefficient is an
integer. Hence every value is omnific. The remaining assertions follow
by transporting the topology and structure along this isomorphism onto
its image.
\end{proof}

\noindent\writetag{} For $\alpha=\omega$, $J_\omega$ is Part~I's map
$\iota_{\mathrm B}$ of \cref{pma:pr:thm:omnific}, and $J_{\R}$ is $\iota_{\R}$.
Source~06's realizations (\cref{pma:cp:sec:repo}) use the exponents
$1/(i+1)$ for $\alpha=\omega$ and $\omega^{-\beta}$ for larger $\alpha$; both
choices are strictly decreasing sequences of positive surreal exponents.
Whether these images are initial in the sense of
\replabel{isg:cf:main:presburger} is not checked here; the groups are split,
so that theorem applies to them abstractly (\cref{pma:sec:repo}).

\begin{remark}[Ordinals, reciprocals, and the omega-map]
In \eqref{pma:cp:eq:normalformmap}, $1/(\beta+1)$ is a reciprocal in the
surreal field, not ordinal division. Likewise $\omega^x$ denotes
Conway's monomial map. It should not be silently replaced by a different
surreal exponential convention. For $\alpha=\omega$ the exponents
are simply $1,1/2,1/3,\ldots$.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Omnific realization of the continuum family]
For $B_s=\Q+\Q s$ as in Theorem~\ref{pma:cp:thm:continuum} and any countably
infinite ordinal $\alpha$, the map
\[
J_{s,\alpha}(b,a,n)
=b\omega^2+\sum_{\beta<\alpha}a_\beta\omega^{1/(\beta+1)}+n
\]
is an ordered additive embedding of
$B_s\lex\Q^\alpha\lex\Z$ into $\Oz$.
Consequently the continuum family, including the models in
Corollary~\ref{pma:cp:cor:continuumranks}, also has explicit omnific realizations
with its topology transported from the coefficient space.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
All tail exponents are positive and at most $1$, so the extra exponent
$2$ lies strictly above them. The same normal-form argument applies, with
the real coefficient $b\in B_s$ determining the leading component and
the integer $n$ the constant term.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Set size and formal foundations}

For a fixed $\alpha$, the domain $\Q^\alpha\times\Z$ is a set, and
the exponents in \eqref{pma:cp:eq:normalformmap} form a set. In the sign-sequence
presentation of surreal numbers, the normal-form operation assigns a
unique surreal set-code to each input. Replacement therefore makes the
whole image a set. Its birthdays have an ordinal supremum; after
increasing that bound, the entire image lies in a single set-sized initial
segment of the surreal class. The same argument applies to the real model
and to each fixed parameter $s$ in the continuum family.

Accordingly, ordinary ZFC suffices to quantify over the model, all its
subsets, its topology, and all its finite Cartesian powers. No ``set of all
surreals'' is introduced. The proper class $\No$ is used as a definable
ambient class of set-codes, not as an element of itself or as a domain of
unrestricted set comprehension.

The images are additive ordered subgroups. We do not claim that the
particular normal-form images are initial under surreal simplicity,
closed under surreal multiplication, or closed under arbitrary
surreal-analytic operations. Already $(\omega)^2$ is outside the
rank-two group $\R\omega+\Z$. Proposition~\ref{pma:cp:prop:nomultiplication}
also forbids manufacturing a different compatible semiring product
internally in these examples.

\subsection{Surreal realizations and a formalization route \src{06}}
\label{pma:cp:sec:repo}

\subsubsection{The same algebra at explicit surreal scales \src{06}}

The connection with the repository's surreal work is visible directly
in Conway normal forms. Using the usual normal-form arithmetic, the
map
\begin{equation}
 (r,n)\longmapsto r\omega+n
 \label{pma:cp:eq:real-surreal}
\end{equation}
identifies $\R\lex\Z$ with an ordered additive subgroup of the
omnific integers. The rational-sequence model has the realization
\begin{equation}
 ((q_i)_{i\in\N},n)\longmapsto
       n+\sum_{i\in\N}q_i\omega^{1/(i+1)}.
 \label{pma:cp:eq:stream-surreal}
\end{equation}
The exponents are strictly decreasing positive real numbers; every
subset of this sequence is reverse well ordered. The expression is
therefore a valid set-supported normal form. Its leading coefficient
determines the sign, and addition is coefficientwise, exactly as in
our lexicographic group. For arbitrary countable $\alpha$, one can
similarly use the strictly decreasing positive surreal exponents
$\omega^{-\beta}$, $\beta<\alpha$.

Finally the substitution $t\mapsto\omega$ identifies
$\Z+t\R[t]$ with the ring
\[
 \left\{n+\sum_{i=1}^d a_i\omega^i:
               n\in\Z,\ a_i\in\R,\ d<\omega\right\},
\]
which is explicitly studied in the repository
\cite{s06:RepoPolynomial}. These identifications use standard surreal
normal-form facts, also documented in the cited repository reports
\cite{s06:RepoGroups,s06:RepoPolynomial}; none is needed for the proofs of our
Polish models.

Two distinctions matter. First, the subgroup in
\eqref{pma:cp:eq:stream-surreal} is being used additively; no closure under
surreal multiplication is asserted. Second, all Polish topologies
here must be \emph{transported} through the displayed identifications.
They are not claimed to be inherited from a topology on the proper
class $\No$. Our constructions are set-sized throughout and need no
class of all subsets, global enumeration, or paradox-prone
quantification over all surreal numbers.

\subsubsection{What the existing formal development certifies \src{06}}

The repository's Presburger development supplies a promising
implementation starting point. In the inspected snapshot, its
\texttt{Syntax.lean} interprets formulas under valuations
\texttt{List Int}. The central results in
\texttt{Decision.lean}, including
\texttt{holds\_iff\_quantifierEliminate}, therefore concern standard
integer semantics. They do not already assert that an arbitrary
$\Z$-group satisfies every eliminated formula.

A faithful formalization can separate four layers.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Algebraic semantics.} Define a discretely ordered
abelian group with its least positive element and the standard
division/remainder schema. Interpret affine terms and formulas in
an arbitrary such group. Generalize the Cooper residue and interval
lemmas so they no longer mention \texttt{Int} in their semantic
valuation type.
\item \textbf{Concrete models.} Build the ordered groups
$\R\lex\Z$, $\Q^{\N}\lex\Z$, and the additive group of
$\Z+t\R[t]$. Prove the explicit division identity and transfer
the generic semantics to their nonnegative cones.
\item \textbf{Topology.} Supply the product or disjoint-sum
topologies, prove the specified operations continuous, and certify
Polishness by the explicit homeomorphisms or complete metrics.
The polynomial model requires a topology on the positive cone,
not an incompatible claim about a topological signed ring.
\item \textbf{Boundary results.} Formalize the cofinal-scale
obstruction and the single failed induction instance. The
isolated-zero theorem can be a separate later target, adding
descriptive-set-theoretic and probability dependencies.
\end{enumerate}

An adjacent interface requires care.
The inspected file \texttt{PAHF/PASyntax.lean} contains a shallow
\texttt{PA.Model} interface with induction for every Lean predicate
$\alpha\to\mathrm{Prop}$. Such full external induction excludes the
nonstandard objects of interest. The appropriate route uses raw
\texttt{PreModel} operations, syntactic formulas and satisfaction,
and first-order induction instances \cite{s06:RepoPAHF}. For the
polynomial construction, one must also keep the additive language
distinct from the language with multiplication.

These are proposed formalization steps, not claims that a
formalization was run in this project. The deliverable is a
self-contained mathematical article; its full proofs make the
remaining kernel-checking task precise and reviewable.

\section{Formalization scope and a concrete development plan}
\label{pma:cp:sec:formalization}

\subsection{What the existing repository actually proves}

The repository's executable development has a specific semantic domain.
In the inspected source,
\begin{center}
\codeD{Formula.holds : Formula -> List Int -> Prop}
\end{center}
interprets quantifiers over Lean's ordinary integer type. In particular,
the existential case is an existential over \codeD{Int}. The theorem
\codeD{holds_iff_quantifierEliminate} proves the computed elimination
correct for these integer valuations \cite{s07:RepoPresburger}.

This is relevant infrastructure, but does not formally establish that an
arbitrary ordered group with a divisible quotient is a Presburger model.
It also does not verify a topology on $\R\times\Z$ or $\Q^\alpha$.
The source was inspected during this investigation; no Lean or Rocq build
was run. The new theorems in this report are ordinary mathematical proofs,
not claims of a successful proof-assistant execution.

\noindent\writetag{} The claims about the formal project hold at the
repository's current state: \fn{Syntax.lean} defines
\codeD{holds : Formula -> List Int -> Prop} with an existential over
\codeD{Int} (lines 192--197), and \codeD{holds_iff_quantifierEliminate} is in
\fn{Decision.lean} (line 18); the project's Lean files are unchanged since
both pins. \Cref{pma:pr:sec:formalrepo} names the declarations that Part~I's
porting requirement concerns. \textbf{Nothing in this part is formalized.}

\subsection{A small first formalization target}

The best first target is Theorem~\ref{pma:cp:thm:real}, with the algebra and
topology proved in separate modules whose assumptions meet in one final
statement. A suitable sequence is:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Define the coordinate type $\R\times\Z$, its lexicographic order,
and the nonnegative subtype $M_{\R}$. Prove the least-positive property
and the explicit division identity \eqref{pma:cp:eq:divisionreal}.
\item Prove, or port, the semantic theorem that a $\Z$-group satisfies
the complete Presburger theory. Generalize formula interpretation from
\codeD{List Int} to valuations in a structure. Avoid assuming the new
model satisfies the theory as an unproved axiom.
\item Put the product topology on the coordinates, independently of the
lexicographic order instance. Prove closure under addition, restricted
continuity, and Polishness using \eqref{pma:cp:eq:localclosed} or the explicit
metric \eqref{pma:cp:eq:completemetric}.
\item Combine the semantic and topological theorems. State cardinality
through an explicit injection from $\R_{\geq0}$, so uncountability is
not hidden in a typeclass declaration.
\end{enumerate}

The central implementation risk is accidentally selecting the intrinsic
order topology when defining a lexicographically ordered type. That
topology is discrete. The product topology used here must be installed
explicitly and its compatibility claims stated only for the operations
that have actually been proved continuous.

\subsection{Extending the verified boundary}

Once the real-coordinate construction is complete, the $\alpha=\omega$
Baire model is the next manageable target. Its strict coefficient cones
are open, avoiding the transfinite Borel decomposition. A coordinate
enumeration, cylinder basis, and the noncompact-cylinder lemma can be
implemented without any proper-class formalization.

The general cone theorem requires more library infrastructure: Baire
category for closed subgroups, Borel sets' Baire property, countable-base
arguments, and transfinite recursion on closed subgroups. The oscillation
theorem needs ordinal arithmetic and a general relative-boundary
recursion. Those are substantive formalization tasks, not consequences of
running the existing integer decision procedure.

The surreal embedding can be isolated still further. One may first work
with a set-sized Hahn additive group supported on the fixed exponent set,
prove its coordinate order and addition facts, and then use a separately
verified normal-form map into a universe-bounded surreal type. This avoids
making the core topological result depend on a full surreal field
development.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{.25\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{.35\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{.29\textwidth}}
\toprule
Layer & Mathematical obligation & Present status\\
\midrule
Ordinary integer formulas & Executable Cooper elimination and semantic correctness & Existing repository sources inspected\\
Generic additive models & $\Z$-group characterization and full theory & Classical theorem used explicitly\\
Real Polish model & Cone, metric, continuity, uncountability & Proved in this report\\
Baire ordinal family & $G_\delta$ cone, cylinder argument, nonisomorphism & Proved in this report\\
Oscillation ranks & Relative derivatives and ordinal calculation & Proved in this report\\
Omnific embedding & Normal-form existence and coefficient transport & Classical normal forms plus proof here\\
New Lean/Rocq certificates & Kernel-checked versions of the report's new claims & Not supplied\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\section{Where Part III meets Parts I and II \mergetag}\label{pma:cp:sec:meet}

Source~06's ring $A=\Z+t\R[t]$, with $t$ positive infinite, becomes under
$t\mapsto X$ a subring of 03's finite principal-part ring
$\A=\Z\oplus\bigoplus_{q\in\Q_{>0}}\R X^q$ of \cref{pma:part:bor}: the
elements with integer exponents. Both rings are ordered by the sign of the
leading coefficient, so $P=A_{\ge0}=A\cap\PP$. Write $\tau_P$ for source~06's
topology \eqref{pma:cp:eq:poly-topology} on $P$.

\begin{proposition}[The stratum of degree one, and two topologies on $P$ \mergetag]\label{pma:cp:prop:meet}
The following hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item The set of elements of degree at most one,
\[\textstyle P_{\le1}=\{x\in P:\deg x\le1\}=\N\cup\bigcup_{m\in\Z}P_{1,m},\]
is clopen in $(P,\tau_P)$. The map $m+at\mapsto(a,m)$ is an isomorphism of
ordered additive monoids and a homeomorphism from $P_{\le1}$ onto source~07's
$M_{\R}^{\sharp}$ (\cref{pma:cp:prop:isolatedstandard}), and $t\mapsto X$
maps $P_{\le1}$ homeomorphically onto the stratum $(C_{\R},\tau_{\FS})$ of
\cref{pma:prop:stratum}.
\item On $P$, the topology $\tau_P$ is strictly coarser than the topology
induced by 03's $\tau_{\FS}$.
\item Addition and multiplication are continuous on $(P,\tau_P)$
(\cref{pma:cp:thm:polynomial}), but addition is not continuous on $P$ with the
topology induced by $\tau_{\FS}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
(i) $P_{\le1}$ is a union of components of \eqref{pma:cp:eq:poly-topology}.
Each $P_{1,m}=\{m+at:a>0\}$ carries the topology of $(0,\infty)$ in the
coefficient $a$, and the points of $\N$ are isolated. These are exactly the
components of $M_{\R}^{\sharp}$, namely the rays $(0,\infty)\times\{m\}$ and the
isolated standard numerals, and of $C_{\R}$, namely $U_{\{1\},m}$ and
$U_{\varnothing,n}$, $n\ge0$. The algebra is the lexicographic algebra of
$\R\lex\Z$ in all three cases.

(ii) A component $U_{S,n}$ of $\tau_{\FS}$ that lies in $P$ has
$S\subseteq\{1,\dots,d\}$ with $\max S=d$, so it lies in $P_{d,n}$, and its
inclusion there, which inserts zero coefficients, is continuous. Hence every
$\tau_P$-open set meets each such component in an open set, and $\tau_P$ is
coarser. It is strictly coarser: $t^2+t/j\to t^2$ in $\tau_P$, inside the
component $P_{2,0}$, while $t^2+t/j$ and $t^2$ lie in the different
components $U_{\{1,2\},0}$ and $U_{\{2\},0}$ of $\tau_{\FS}$.

(iii) Put $x_j=t^2+(1+1/j)t$ and $y=t^2-t$; both lie in $P$, and
$x_j\to x=t^2+t$ for $\tau_{\FS}$. Then $x_j+y=2t^2+t/j$ lies in
$U_{\{1,2\},0}$ while $x+y=2t^2$ lies in $U_{\{2\},0}$, so $x_j+y$ does not
converge to $x+y$. The pair $(x,y)$ lies on the cancellation locus
\eqref{pma:bor:eq:Dplus} of \cref{pma:bor:thm:cancellation}.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Glazer's Corollary~1 inside one ring.} \Cref{pma:sec:boundary}
placed the real model as a stratum of 03's cone $\PP$, which satisfies
$Q+\IOpen$ with Borel multiplication and on which no Borel-compatible Polish
topology makes addition continuous (\cref{pma:bor:thm:glazer}). The integer-
exponent subcone $P$ sits between the two: it carries a Polish topology with
both operations continuous, and Corollary~1 does not apply to it because $P$
fails open induction at the single formula $x^2<t$
(\cref{pma:cp:thm:openfailure}). In $\A$ the same formula is satisfied by
$x=\tfrac12X^{1/2}$, which is not a standard integer. Source~06's
isolated-zero theorem (\cref{pma:cp:thm:zero}) explains why $\tau_P$ isolates
$0$, whereas the additive models of Part~I are perfect.

\paragraph{Cone topologies versus group topologies.} The same ring is
source~08's $A_*=\Z+X\R[X]$ (\cref{pma:lc:ex:nonsep}) and, under
$t\mapsto\omega$, the ring $\Z+\omega\R[\omega]$ of \cref{pma:lc:cor:omnific}
and \replabel{odg:frac:prop:intersection} of the omnific Diophantine report.
By source~08's \cref{pma:lc:thm:headline}, no Hausdorff locally compact
second-countable additive-group topology on $A$ has a Borel order, so
$\tau_P$, a locally compact Polish topology on the cone, is induced by no
such group topology. Source~06 already notes that the signed analogue of
$\tau_P$ is not a ring topology. This is the situation of source~08's
\cref{pma:lc:q:completion} and source~09's \cref{pma:ser:q:cones}, which ask
when a cone topology extends to the group of differences; the Hilbert's-tenth
programme's triage of the arrival
(\fn{Computability/HilbertTenthProblem/Papers/research-wip/native-stream-queue/recent_incoming_substrate_triage_20261003.md})
makes the same caveat.

\paragraph{The same failed instance elsewhere.} The formula $x^2<X$ defining
the standard cut in $\Z+X\Q[X]$ is used by batch~86's manuscript~10, written
into the report
\fn{Algebra/SurrealNumbers/docs/surreal/discrete-initial-subgroups-and-omnific-normalization}.
\writetag{} [Batch-86 reciprocal notes, 3 October 2026.] There it is
source~03, Sections~19--22. It is motivated by the same paper of Glazer but
is not topological, and its obstruction precedes any topology: the additive
group of the difference ring of a nonstandard model of $\mathsf I\Delta_0$
has no nonzero homomorphism to $\Z$ (\replabel{isg:sc:thm:nochar}), so no
such model has a unit-preserving additive map into $\Oz$
(\replabel{isg:sc:cor:omnific}), and by that report's Presburger criterion
the ordered group completion of its additive monoid is not initially
realizable (\replabel{isg:sc:cor:fragment}). It answers neither of Glazer's
questions.
For $\Oz$ itself the collection records existential failures of induction
(\replabel{odg:thm:induction}, \replabel{odg:rem:ringinduction}); as source~06
says, the failure for $P$ does not transfer to $\Oz$.

\section{Further research questions and topics}
\label{pma:cp:sec:questions}

The following program separates an explicitly published question from
questions suggested by the constructions. Unless stated otherwise, these
are proposed directions for investigation; we do not assert that every
formulation is historically new or unresolved in all existing literature.

\noindent\src{06} Source~06 lists twelve numbered projects rather than
question environments. Each one that coincides with a question of source~07
is printed after it, with source~06's number:

\subsection{Published reverse-mathematical question}

\begin{question}[Glazer's Question 1]
Can the stronger topological Tennenbaum theorem in \cite{Glazer} be
proved over $\mathrm{ATR}_0$?
\end{question}
This report does not answer that question. The affirmative Presburger
construction shows that a proposed route from a weaker metatheory to a
negative Presburger conclusion cannot yield the latter conclusion as
stated. It does not determine the metatheoretic strength of the theorem
about arithmetic with multiplication.

\noindent\writetag{} Source~06's project with the same subject:

\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{\arabic*.},leftmargin=*,start=6]

\item \textbf{Exact strength of the isolated-zero principle.}
Formalize the proof of Theorem~\ref{pma:cp:thm:zero} over weak subsystems
of second-order arithmetic using codes for Polish spaces and
continuous functions. Locate the strength of analytic/coanalytic
separation and of the coded measure argument. This is related to,
but does not resolve, Glazer's published Question~1 asking whether
his full Theorem~2 is provable in $\mathsf{ATR}_0$ \cite{Glazer}.

\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Classification and topology}

\begin{question}[Which spaces and which additive models?]
Characterize the uncountable Polish spaces that support continuous
Presburger addition, and characterize which continuum-sized Presburger
models admit such a presentation.
\end{question}
The real-coordinate model, Baire-space family, and isolated-standard
variant give several positive topological types. Compact spaces are
excluded. A useful first refinement is to classify locally compact
presentations in terms of their components and the position of the
standard numerals. A separate algebraic problem asks how much of the
existence theory is captured by split $\Z$-groups.

\noindent\writetag{} Source~06's project with the same subject:

\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{\arabic*.},leftmargin=*,start=4]

\item \textbf{Which Polish spaces support additive arithmetic?}
Classify the possible underlying spaces. We have a locally compact
perfect example and a nowhere locally compact example. Compact
Hausdorff spaces are excluded by \cite{EHW}. A useful next step is
to analyze connected spaces and spaces with prescribed sets of
isolated points, while specifying whether a group-completion
topology is also required.

\end{enumerate}

\begin{question}[Non-split integer extensions]
Which ordered extensions $0\to\Z\to G\to D\to0$ with divisible
$D$, but without a group-theoretic splitting, have positive cones with
Polish topologies making addition continuous?
\end{question}
The present construction starts with a splitting. General $\Z$-groups
need not possess it. The residue and extension invariants appearing in
the repository's omnific research provide a natural algebraic language
for formulating obstructions, but do not automatically decide the
topological question.

\noindent\writetag{} Source~06's project with the same subject:

\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{\arabic*.},leftmargin=*,start=2]

\item \textbf{Which additive groups admit Polish presentations?}
Find necessary and sufficient conditions for a continuum-sized
$\Z$-group to have a nonnegative cone with a Polish topology and
continuous addition. Our split products are a substantial class,
but a general $\Z$-group need not split. In particular, investigate
how a nonsplit extension of a divisible ordered group by $\Z$
affects the problem.

\end{enumerate}

\begin{question}[Connected examples]
Does a connected uncountable Polish space support a continuous
Presburger presentation?
\end{question}
The examples here have clopen residue classes and hence are disconnected.
In an arbitrary presentation, residue classes are Borel by
Proposition~\ref{pma:cp:prop:allborel}; that alone does not make them clopen.
The gap between these two regularities is a concrete place to investigate.

\subsection{Definability and descriptive invariants}

\begin{question}[Ranks for a fixed model]
For one fixed continuous presentation, which boundary oscillation ranks
occur among parameter-definable sets? How do they depend on arity and on
the chosen parameter?
\end{question}
Theorem~\ref{pma:cp:thm:oscillation} varies the model and uses a parameter with
positive zeroth coordinate. A full parameter spectrum requires analyzing
cuts based at vectors with a transfinite first nonzero coordinate, where
the boundary of the model itself affects the derivative. It should also
distinguish the rank of a relation from the rank of a unary section.

\begin{question}[Uniform definability bounds]
Must every Polish Presburger presentation with continuous addition have
all definable relations at some uniformly bounded Borel level? Is the
$\Delta^0_2$ bound forced, or special to the ambient-group construction?
\end{question}
Proposition~\ref{pma:cp:prop:allborel} proves Borelness in complete generality.
Theorem~\ref{pma:cp:thm:general} proves the stronger level-two conclusion under
an additional structural hypothesis. Closing that gap would turn the
examples into a general regularity theorem.

\noindent\writetag{} For the cones of locally compact signed groups with Borel
order, source~08's \cref{pma:lc:thm:definable} gives the uniform $\Dtwo$
bound; for an arbitrary Polish presentation the question stays open.

\begin{question}[Interpreting the cone closure rank]
Which structural features of an ordered Polish group are detected by
$\rho(A,<)$? How does the rank behave under closed convex subgroups,
quotients, products, and order-preserving topological embeddings?
\end{question}
Proposition~\ref{pma:cp:prop:coneranks} realizes every countable rank on real
coordinate groups. A useful next goal is an intrinsic characterization
of the first few ranks and precise inequalities for those operations.
The rank records the interaction of order with topology; it need not
agree with the algebraic Archimedean rank.

\subsection{Arithmetic expansions and formal verification}

\begin{question}[Additional continuous functions]
Which natural expansions of Presburger arithmetic still admit
uncountable Polish presentations with all named functions continuous?
\end{question}
Fixed-divisor quotient and remainder are positive examples. Total
truncated subtraction, minimum, and maximum are impossible individually.
Predecessor depends on the presentation. Expansions by selected unary
functions, or restricted coding functions, offer an intermediate program
before multiplication is considered.

\noindent\writetag{} Source~06's project with the same subject:

\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{\arabic*.},leftmargin=*,start=9]

\item \textbf{Effective comparison and definable functions.}
For the explicit computable Baire model, calibrate the effective
complexity of its order, equality, minimum, and truncated
predecessor. The continuous addition algorithm does not decide
an infinite stream's equality to zero. A useful target is an
exact classification of the discontinuity or computational
strength required by each definable operation.

\end{enumerate}

\begin{question}[Removing the cofinal-multiple obstruction]
Construct and analyze continuous Polish Presburger models in which no
nonstandard element has cofinal standard multiples. Which of their
additive structures admit an ordered-ring expansion at all?
\end{question}
The explicit family in this report cannot answer the latter question
positively. Divisible coefficient groups with no greatest Archimedean
class are natural candidates for a next stage. Any resulting multiplication
must then be tested against the appropriate arithmetic axioms and
Glazer's regularity obstruction.

\noindent\writetag{} Source~06's project with the same subject:

\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{\arabic*.},leftmargin=*,start=3]

\item \textbf{Presburger reducts of genuine PA models.}
Can the additive reduct of a nonstandard model of PA have an
uncountable Polish continuous-addition presentation if its
multiplication is allowed to be non-Borel? Glazer's published
obstruction rules out a Borel multiplication under stronger
arithmetic hypotheses. Our lexicographic examples do not answer
this question, because their additive structure already excludes
a semiring multiplication.

\end{enumerate}

\begin{question}[Weak foundations and certified models]
What is the weakest useful coded metatheory for the real and Baire
constructions? Can the generic Presburger semantic theorem and the
Polish topology be joined in a short audited Lean development?
\end{question}
The explicit coordinate formulas suggest a substantially lighter proof
than the general transfinite cone theorem. A careful analysis must still
specify codes for Borel sets, complete metrics, countable bases, and
satisfaction of first-order formulas. Section~\ref{pma:cp:sec:formalization}
gives an implementation order with a concrete first target.

\noindent\writetag{} Source~06's project with the same subject:

\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{\arabic*.},leftmargin=*,start=1]

\item \textbf{Formal generic Presburger semantics.}
Complete the four-layer route in Section~\ref{pma:cp:sec:repo}, beginning
with generic $\Z$-group soundness for the repository's Cooper
eliminator. The first deliverable should be a theorem about all
models of the division/remainder axioms, followed by the real
cone as its first nonstandard instance. This would convert the
main answer into a directly auditable formal result.

\end{enumerate}

\begin{question}[Simplicity and coefficient topologies]
Which initially realized omnific additive groups admit coefficient
topologies of this kind, and which initiality operations preserve
Polishness and continuity of addition?
\end{question}
Theorem~\ref{pma:cp:thm:omnific} supplies an explicit ordered embedding without
asserting initiality of the displayed image. Combining a proved initial
realization theorem with a transported Polish topology is straightforward
as an abstract existence argument; preserving a natural coefficient
description during that transport is a sharper and more informative goal.

\noindent\writetag{} Source~06's project with the same subject:

\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{\arabic*.},leftmargin=*,start=12]

\item \textbf{Surreal topology without class-level assumptions.}
Study the transported topologies on set-sized omnific subgroups
and subrings such as \eqref{pma:cp:eq:real-surreal} and
\eqref{pma:cp:eq:stream-surreal}. Ask which algebraic embeddings are
continuous for these explicit topologies. Keep the support set,

proper class of all surreal numbers is not itself an ordinary

\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Further projects of source 06 \src{06}}\label{pma:cp:sec:future}

The remaining projects of source~06 have no counterpart among source~07's
questions.

\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{\arabic*.},leftmargin=*,start=5]

\item \textbf{How many isolated points are necessary for semirings?}
Theorem~\ref{pma:cp:thm:zero} forces zero to be isolated, and the
polynomial example isolates every standard numeral. Determine
whether a Polish topological $\PAminus$ model can have zero as its
only isolated point, or classify which patterns of isolated
numerals are possible. Requiring continuous predecessor is an
additional hypothesis and should be treated separately.

\end{enumerate}

\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{\arabic*.},leftmargin=*,start=7]

\item \textbf{Completeness and separability thresholds.}
Find sharp replacements for Polishness in the isolated-zero
theorem. Completeness supplies limits of all subset sums, and
separability supplies descriptive regularity of the order. One
can test these roles separately using incomplete metrizable
models and complete nonseparable presentations.

\item \textbf{Classification beyond cardinality.}
Theorem~\ref{pma:cp:s06:thm:continuum} gives the exact number of isomorphism
types but no full classification. Determine the descriptive
complexity of isomorphism for suitably coded Polish Presburger
presentations. Convex quotients and residue extensions provide
natural invariants. Compare with the countable-model Scott
analysis in \cite{Block}, without assuming that countable and
Polish presentations have identical classification behavior.

\end{enumerate}

\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{\arabic*.},leftmargin=*,start=10]

\item \textbf{Coefficient fields and other support families.}
Replace $\R$ in the polynomial construction by an ordered
Polish topological field whose positive cone is Polish. The
same degree-stratum proof applies whenever coefficient
operations are continuous. More ambitiously, determine which
infinite support families allow a Polish positive-cone topology
with both operations continuous, and identify where leading-term
cancellation or completeness obstructs such a topology.

\item \textbf{A bridge from additive induction to open induction.}
The formula $x^2<t$ isolates one exact failure in the polynomial
model. Seek a useful classification of additional multiplication
principles that remain compatible with a Polish continuous
semiring presentation. The class must stop before the hypotheses
of Glazer's additive obstruction, but the interval between
additive induction and $\IOpen$ contains many distinct theories.

\end{enumerate}

(\cref{pma:cp:thm:openfailure}), so the clause of that question about

\part{Local compactness forces arithmetic rigidity}\label{pma:part:lc}

\noindent\writetag{} This part is source~08 of batch~86, \emph{Local
Compactness Forces Arithmetic Rigidity: Borel ordered groups, Presburger
models, and multiplication without regularity assumptions} (dated
October~3, 2026; ``prepared with ChatGPT''), pinned to repository commit
\texttt{883e0b3b2}. All unmarked text is source~08's; its labels carry the
prefix \texttt{pma:lc:}. Source~08 cites two earlier manuscripts as
``user-library'' files: \cite{s08:PriorPres} is source~05, the base of
\cref{pma:part:pr}, and \cite{s08:PriorBorel} is source~03,
\cref{pma:part:bor}. Its finite checks are shipped as
\fn{code/08-local-compactness-verify.py} with the record
\fn{data/08-local-compactness-verification_results.json}
(\cref{pma:lc:app:audit}). Its central theorem is complementary to source~09's
\cref{pma:ser:thm:discrete} in \cref{pma:part:ser}: both make an uncountable
discretely ordered ring impossible under a topology, 08 assuming local
compactness and no regularity of multiplication, 09 assuming Borel scalar
maps and no local compactness. Neither result bears directly on Glazer's
Question~2, which concerns models without multiplication
(\cref{pma:sec:glazer}).

\section{The intersection and the contribution}
\label{pma:lc:sec:intersection}

The point of contact is the topology of arithmetic presentations. In
\emph{A Topological Tennenbaum Theorem}, Elliot Glazer rules out continuous
addition with Borel multiplication for uncountable Polish models of
$Q+\IOpen$; his Question~2 asks for an uncountable Polish Presburger model
with continuous addition \cite[Corollary~1 and Question~2]{Glazer}.
ProveIt's relevant interfaces are its Cooper-elimination development and
its ordered surreal and omnific constructions \cite{s08:RepoPres,s08:RepoSurreal}.
These suggest separating three issues: addition, the regularity of order,
and the possibility of installing multiplication.

The earlier companion manuscript \emph{Polish Presburger Arithmetic inside
the Omnific Integers} already constructs the model
\begin{equation}\label{pma:lc:eq:realmodel}
 M_{\R}=(\R_{>0}\times\Z)\cup(\{0\}\times\N)
\end{equation}
with continuous addition, and gives example-specific multiplication
obstructions \cite{s08:PriorPres}. Repeating that construction would not be an
advance. The question pursued here is structural:
\begin{quote}
What does local compactness force on every Borel ordered additive-group
presentation, and can multiplication evade the obstruction by being
completely nonmeasurable?
\end{quote}
Our answer is negative for discretely ordered rings. The obstruction occurs
before induction, definability of multiplication, or a choice of particular
normal forms enters the argument.

\noindent\writetag{} \Cref{pma:lc:eq:realmodel} is Part~I's real model
(\cref{pma:pr:thm:main}); source~07's \cref{pma:cp:thm:real} and source~06's
\cref{pma:cp:s06:thm:real} prove it again.

\subsection{The central theorem}
\begin{theorem}[Arithmetic rigidity]\label{pma:lc:thm:headline}
Let $A$ be a nonzero commutative unital ring with a total order such that
addition preserves order, products of positive elements are positive, and
$1$ is the least positive element. Suppose that $A$ has a Hausdorff locally
compact second-countable topology making $(A,+,-,0)$ a topological group,
and that $A_{>0}$ is Borel. Then the topology is discrete and $A$ is countable.
No regularity assumption is imposed on the multiplication map.
\end{theorem}

The proof is given in \cref{pma:lc:sec:rigidity}. Its two essential parts are an
algebraic growth argument and a classical topological structure argument.
The former produces more cosets than the latter permits. In particular,
it does not attempt to pass a discontinuous multiplication through a limit.

\subsection{Other results proved here}
The structural conclusions have independent content even when no ring
multiplication exists. For signed Presburger models, we obtain an exact
classification of the \emph{pointed topological additive groups}, the
local-compactness threshold for their positive cones, and a uniform
$\Dtwo$ bound on all first-order definable relations. We do not claim that
every order itself is the standard product lexicographic order.

There is also a second, distinct obstruction. On these same additive groups,
an arbitrary integral-domain multiplication, not required to respect the
specified order, has only countably many scalars whose multiplication maps
are Borel. For the additive type $\R^d\times\Z$, these scalars are exactly
the ordinary integers. This theorem is not vacuous: an explicit algebraic
transport construction supplies such ring structures.

\subsection{What is classical, and what remains uncertified}
Measurable orders on locally compact abelian groups were studied by Hewitt
and Koshi and by Asmar and Montgomery-Smith \cite{HewittKoshi,AsmarMS}.
The compact-group obstruction, finite-dimensional separation arguments,
and the structure theorem for locally compact abelian groups are classical
inputs, not discoveries claimed here. We supply the needed proofs except
for the principal structure theorem itself. Presburger quantifier elimination
is likewise classical \cite{Cluckers}; \cref{pma:lc:sec:presburger} records the
version needed for nonstandard groups.

The contribution proposed for independent review is the amplification and
arithmetic-rigidity package, together with its classification, scalar,
and omnific consequences. A bounded literature search did not establish
priority for these formulations. In particular, lack of a matching search
result is not evidence of a first proof. The existing companion constructions
are cited as prior work, not relabelled as new.

\section{Conventions, topology, and foundations}
\label{pma:lc:sec:conventions}

All groups in this paper are abelian and written additively. An ordered
group has a translation-invariant \emph{total} order. The associated
strict positive cone is $P=G_{>0}$, and $G_{\ge0}=P\cup\{0\}$.
For a topological group, $P$ is Borel if and only if the binary order
relation is Borel: one direction takes a section at zero, and the other
uses the continuous difference map $(x,y)\mapsto y-x$.

The phrase \emph{discretely ordered} refers to the \emph{algebraic order}:
there is a least positive element, denoted $1$. It does not say that the
presentation topology is discrete. In a ring, $\std n=n1$ denotes the
ordinary integer numeral. Throughout, $n,m,N$ used as moduli, finite
indices, or bounds are external ordinary integers, not arbitrary elements
of a nonstandard model. We write $|x|=\max(x,-x)$.

A Hausdorff locally compact second-countable topological group is Polish.
Here, a Polish space is a separable completely metrizable space. The
notation $\Dtwo$ means both $F_\sigma$ and $G_\delta$ in the specified
topology (usually Polish); $\Done$ means clopen. No order topology is silently
substituted for the given topology.

\begin{definition}[Order unit]
If $V$ is an ordered additive subgroup, an \emph{order unit in $V$} is an
 element $u>0$ in $V$ such that for every $v\in V$ there exists an ordinary
 integer $N\ge1$ with $|v|\le Nu$.
\end{definition}

This is an additive notion. It neither says that $u$ is a ring unit nor
that $V$ is closed under multiplication. The distinction is indispensable
in the main proof.

All theorems are statements about sets in ZFC. When surreals are mentioned,
we work with explicitly set-sized subgroups or subrings of $\No$ or $\Oz$.
We never equip the proper class of all surreals with a Polish topology,
form a set of all its subsets, or apply Haar measure to a proper class.
The algebraic splitting arguments use the ordinary ZFC fact that divisible
abelian groups are injective. No assertion about the minimum choice or
reverse-mathematical strength of the complete proof is made.

\section{An algebraic amplification theorem}
\label{pma:lc:sec:amplification}

The key obstruction can be proved without topology. The strongest useful
hypothesis is weaker than divisibility.

\begin{lemma}[Finite elements are ordinary integers]\label{pma:lc:lem:finite}
In a discretely ordered ring $A$, every element of $[0,\std n]$, for an
ordinary $n\in\N$, is one of $\std0,\ldots,\std n$. Consequently, for
an additive subgroup $V\subseteq A$ the following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $V\cap\Z1=\{0\}$;
\item for every $v\in V\setminus\{0\}$ and every ordinary $n\ge0$,
      $|v|>\std n$.
\end{enumerate}
Every divisible additive subgroup of $A$ satisfies these conditions.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The interval assertion follows by external induction on $n$. If $0<x\le
\std{n+1}$, then $x\ge1$ and $0\le x-1\le\std n$; the induction
hypothesis applies. This proves the equivalence because $|v|\in V$.

For the last assertion, take $v>0$ in a divisible subgroup and an ordinary
$n\ge0$. Write $v=(n+1)w$ with $w\in V$. Ordered-group torsion-freeness
and positivity imply $w>0$, hence $w\ge1$. Therefore $v\ge\std{n+1}>
\std n$. Apply the same argument to $-v$ for negative elements.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Coset and polynomial amplification]\label{pma:lc:thm:amplification}
Let $A$ be a discretely ordered ring, and let $V\subseteq A$ be a nonzero
additive subgroup with $V\cap\Z1=\{0\}$. Suppose $u\in V$ is an order
unit. Then:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $uV\cap V=\{0\}$, and the additive map
\begin{equation}\label{pma:lc:eq:cosetmap}
 V\longrightarrow A/V,\qquad v\longmapsto uv+V
\end{equation}
is injective. In particular, $|A/V|\ge|V|$.
\item The finite-sum map
\begin{equation}\label{pma:lc:eq:polymap}
 \Psi:\bigoplus_{j\ge0} V\longrightarrow A,
 \qquad (v_j)_{j\ge0}\longmapsto\sum_{j\ge0}u^jv_j
\end{equation}
is an injective additive homomorphism. If the source is ordered by the
sign of its \emph{highest-index nonzero coefficient}, then $\Psi$ is an
order embedding.
\item The induced additive map $\bigoplus_{j\ge1}V\to A/V$ is injective.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Suppose $uv\in V$ for $v\ne0$. Since $u>0$, the order-unit condition
provides an ordinary $N$ with
\[
 u|v|=|uv|\le Nu.
\]
Cancellation of the positive factor $u$ gives $|v|\le\std N$, contrary
to \cref{pma:lc:lem:finite}. Thus $uv\in V$ implies $v=0$, which proves the
first assertion and injectivity of \eqref{pma:lc:eq:cosetmap}.

For the second assertion, take a nonzero finite tuple and let $k$ be its
largest nonzero index. If $k=0$, the result is immediate. For each $j<k$
choose an ordinary $N_j\ge1$ with $|v_j|\le N_ju$. Put
$N=\sum_{j<k}N_j$. By \cref{pma:lc:lem:finite}, $u>1$ and $|v_k|>\std N$.
The lower-degree terms satisfy
\begin{align}
 \left|\sum_{j<k}u^jv_j\right|
 &\le\sum_{j<k}u^j|v_j|
 \le\sum_{j<k}N_j u^{j+1}
 \le N u^k
 < u^k|v_k|. \label{pma:lc:eq:domination}
\end{align}
Therefore the sum in \eqref{pma:lc:eq:polymap} is nonzero and has the sign of
$v_k$. Additivity is distributivity. Applying the sign statement to the
difference of two tuples proves the claimed order embedding.

Finally, if a finite sum $\sum_{j\ge1}u^jv_j$ belongs to $V$, subtract
that element as the zeroth coefficient. Injectivity of $\Psi$ makes all
coefficients zero, proving the third assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What the polynomial language does not mean]
The domain of $\Psi$ is an ordered \emph{additive} direct sum. In general
$V$ is not a coefficient ring, and the theorem does not define a ring
$V[t]$ or an evaluation homomorphism from such a ring. The quotient $A/V$
is also only an additive quotient: $V$ need not be an ideal or convex.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Divisible-subgroup obstruction]\label{pma:lc:cor:divisible}
If a discretely ordered ring contains an uncountable divisible additive
subgroup $V$ with an order unit, then $A/V$ is uncountable. More precisely,
$A/V$ contains an additive copy of a countable direct sum of copies of $V$.
\end{corollary}

The strength of this result is its insensitivity to the regularity of
multiplication. The map \eqref{pma:lc:eq:cosetmap} may be arbitrarily nonmeasurable;
its existence as an injection is already enough for a contradiction.

\section{Borel orders on locally compact additive groups}
\label{pma:lc:sec:lca}

This section assembles the classical topological ingredients in the form
needed later. The measurable-order literature \cite{HewittKoshi,AsmarMS}
contains broader structure results. Borelness is useful here because it
survives restriction to every closed subgroup.

\subsection{A compact subgroup cannot carry the restricted order}
\begin{lemma}[Compact-order obstruction]\label{pma:lc:lem:compact}
A second-countable topological abelian group with a Borel invariant total
order has no nontrivial compact subgroup.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $K$ be a compact subgroup. Ordered groups are torsion-free, so a
nontrivial $K$ cannot be finite. Let $\mu$ be normalized Haar measure on
an infinite $K$, and set $P_K=K\cap G_{>0}$. Singletons have measure zero:
otherwise arbitrarily many disjoint translates of one singleton would
have total measure greater than one. Since inversion preserves Haar
measure, the partition
\[
 K=P_K\ \dot\cup\ (-P_K)\ \dot\cup\ \{0\}
\]
gives $p:=\mu(P_K)=1/2$.

For every $t\in K$, the two upper rays $P_K$ and $P_K+t$ are nested.
Their measures are equal by translation invariance. Thus
\[
 \mu(P_K\cap(P_K+t))=p\qquad(t\in K).
\]
On the other hand, the relevant indicator on $K\times K$ is Borel;
second countability permits the usual product-measure Fubini theorem.
Consequently
\begin{align*}
 \int_K\mu(P_K\cap(P_K+t))\,d\mu(t)
 &=\int_K\int_K 1_{P_K}(x)1_{P_K}(x-t)\,d\mu(x)\,d\mu(t)\\
 &=\int_K1_{P_K}(x)\,p\,d\mu(x)=p^2.
\end{align*}
Hence $p=p^2$, contradicting $p=1/2$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The connected component is an open real vector group}
\begin{theorem}[Topological additive decomposition]\label{pma:lc:thm:lca}
Let $G$ be a Hausdorff locally compact second-countable abelian group with
a Borel invariant total order. There are a finite $d\ge0$ and a countable
discrete torsion-free abelian group $D$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{pma:lc:eq:lcasplit}
 G\cong_{\mathrm{top.grp}}\R^d\times D.
\end{equation}
The identity component $C=G^\circ$ is open and is the $\R^d$ factor.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The principal structure theorem for locally compact abelian groups supplies
an open subgroup $H\cong\R^d\times K$, where $K$ is compact; see
\cite[p.~4]{Dobben} and the classical references recorded there.
The image of $K$ is a compact subgroup of $G$, so \cref{pma:lc:lem:compact}
forces $K=0$. Thus $H\cong\R^d$ is open and connected. It is also
closed, as every open subgroup is closed. Therefore $H=G^\circ=C$.

As an abstract abelian group, $C$ is divisible and hence injective.
There is a homomorphism $r:G\to C$ extending the identity of $C$.
The restriction of $r$ to the open neighborhood $C$ of zero is continuous,
so $r$ is continuous at zero and hence everywhere. Let $D=\ker r$.
Each coset of $C$ meets $D$ in exactly one point; in particular $D$ is
discrete. The maps $(c,e)\mapsto c+e$ and $g\mapsto(r(g),g-r(g))$
are continuous inverses. Second countability makes the family of disjoint
open cosets countable. Finally $D$ is torsion-free because it is a subgroup
of the ordered group $G$.
\end{proof}

For completeness, the injectivity fact just used can be obtained by the
usual maximal-extension argument. Given a homomorphism from a subgroup
$B$ of an abelian group into a divisible group, adjoin one new element
$x$. If a least positive $n$ has $nx\in B$, choose an $n$th root of its
image; otherwise assign any image to $x$. Each choice extends the
homomorphism to $B+\Z x$. Zorn's lemma then gives an extension to the
whole group. Its use here is purely algebraic, with continuity recovered
from the openness of $C$.

\subsection{A Borel order on the real component is a linear flag}
\begin{lemma}[Finite-dimensional Borel-order lemma]\label{pma:lc:lem:flag}
Every Borel invariant total order on $(\R^d,+)$ is, after an invertible
real-linear change of coordinates, the standard lexicographic order.
For $d>0$ it has a positive order unit.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Proceed by induction on $d$. The assertion is trivial for $d=0$. Let
$P$ be the strict positive cone when $d>0$. Ordered divisibility implies
that $P$ is stable under positive rational scaling and rational convex
combinations. The sets $P,-P,\{0\}$ cover $\R^d$, so the Baire category
theorem implies that $P$ is not meager. Since $P$ is Borel, it has the
Baire property and is comeager in some nonempty open set $U$.

If $z\in U+U$, then $U\cap(z-U)$ is nonempty and open. Both $P$ and
$z-P$ are comeager in this intersection, so it contains $x\in P$ with
$z-x\in P$. Hence $z\in P+P\subseteq P$. This proves that $P$ has
nonempty interior.

Let $F=\overline P$. Rational convexity and passage to limits show that
$F$ is a closed convex real cone. It is proper: the nonempty open set
$-\Int(P)$ is disjoint from $P$, and therefore from its closure.
Finite-dimensional separation yields a nonzero real-linear functional
$\ell$ with $\ell(F)\subseteq[0,\infty)$. Totality then gives
\begin{equation}\label{pma:lc:eq:halfspace}
 \{x:\ell(x)>0\}\subseteq P\subseteq\{x:\ell(x)\ge0\}.
\end{equation}
Indeed, an element with positive $\ell$ cannot be zero or negative.
The restriction of the order to $\ker\ell$ is again Borel. Apply the
induction hypothesis there, and extend its coordinate functionals to
$\R^d$, with $\ell$ as first coordinate. Equation \eqref{pma:lc:eq:halfspace}
and the induction hypothesis give the required lexicographic rule.

In those coordinates, $u=(1,0,\ldots,0)$ is an order unit: for any
$v=(v_1,\ldots,v_d)$ choose an ordinary integer $N>|v_1|$. Then
$-Nu<v<Nu$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
We did not assume that the given order respects multiplication of vectors
by arbitrary positive real numbers. That compatibility is a conclusion of
Borelness and finite dimensionality. Applying an ordered-vector-space
classification without first establishing this point would leave a gap.
\end{remark}

\section{Proof and scope of arithmetic rigidity}
\label{pma:lc:sec:rigidity}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{pma:lc:thm:headline}]
Apply \cref{pma:lc:thm:lca} to the additive group of $A$. Its identity component
$C$ is an open real vector group $\R^d$, and $A/C$ is countable.
If $d>0$, then $C$ is uncountable and divisible. By \cref{pma:lc:lem:flag}, the
restricted order has an order unit $u\in C$. The amplification theorem
therefore gives an injection
\[
 C\hookrightarrow A/C,\qquad c\longmapsto uc+C.
\]
This contradicts countability of $A/C$. Hence $d=0$. The zero subgroup is
then open, so the topology is discrete. A second-countable discrete space
is countable.
\end{proof}

\noindent\writetag{} Source~09 reaches the same conclusion, discreteness and
countability, without local compactness but with Borel scalar maps and a cone
with the Baire property (\cref{pma:ser:thm:discrete}); the two hypotheses
are incomparable. Source~06's \cref{pma:cp:thm:zero} is a third result of
the kind, on the nonnegative cone: if addition and multiplication are both
continuous for a Polish topology on the cone, then $0$ is isolated.

\begin{corollary}[No compatible ordered multiplication]\label{pma:lc:cor:noexpansion}
Let $G$ be an uncountable discretely ordered abelian group with a Borel
order and a locally compact Polish group topology. There is no
commutative unital ring multiplication on its underlying set, with unit
the named least positive element, which respects the specified order.
This remains true when multiplication is allowed to be non-Borel.
\end{corollary}

\begin{corollary}[Positive-cone semirings]\label{pma:lc:cor:semiring}
Let $G$ satisfy the hypotheses of \cref{pma:lc:cor:noexpansion}, and let
$M=G_{\ge0}$. There is no commutative unital semiring multiplication on
$M$ with the given addition and unit, such that the product of two
nonzero elements is nonzero.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The cancellative additive monoid $M$ has Grothendieck group $G$.
A commutative semiring multiplication extends by distributivity to this
group: $(a-b)(c-d)=ac+bd-ad-bc$. The assumed absence of zero divisors
on positive elements makes products of positive elements positive in
$G$. It would therefore yield the ring forbidden by
\cref{pma:lc:cor:noexpansion}.
\end{proof}

The group-topology hypothesis is important. A topology on a cancellative
positive monoid need not automatically give its Grothendieck group the
same desirable separation and local-compactness properties. Thus our
result is not a theorem about every possible Polish presentation of a
positive arithmetic model. Similarly, the theorem does not replace the
general Polish-space result of \cite{Glazer}; it is a different refinement
with a stronger topological assumption and much weaker multiplicative
assumptions.

The proof uses no standard-residue axiom, no square roots, no induction
scheme in the ring, and no compatibility between multiplication and limits.
The only induction in \cref{pma:lc:lem:finite} is ordinary external induction on
a finite integer. These distinctions make the theorem suitable for
applications to weak arithmetic and integer parts.

\section{Signed Presburger arithmetic and a uniform elimination lemma}
\label{pma:lc:sec:presburger}

\begin{definition}[$\Z$-group]
A $\Z$-group is an ordered abelian group with least positive element $1$
such that for each ordinary $m\ge1$ and each $x\in G$ there are unique
$q\in G$ and $r\in\{0,\ldots,m-1\}$ satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{pma:lc:eq:division}
 x=mq+r1.
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

Equivalently, $\Z1$ is a convex subgroup and $G/\Z1$ is divisible.
For the implication from quotient divisibility, write $x=mq+k1$, perform
ordinary Euclidean division on $k$, and absorb its quotient into $q$.
Uniqueness follows because $|r-s|<m$ cannot be a nonzero multiple of $m$
in a discretely ordered group. The converse follows immediately from
\eqref{pma:lc:eq:division}. Convexity of $\Z1$ follows from the finite-interval
argument of \cref{pma:lc:lem:finite}, which only needs the group order.

We use the language
\[
 \pres=\{+,-,0,1,<,\equiv_m:m=2,3,\ldots\},
 \qquad x\equiv_m y\iff x-y\in mG.
\]
The equivalence between $\Z$-groups and models of signed Presburger
arithmetic, and quantifier elimination in this language, are standard
\cite{Cluckers}. We give the finite-witness argument to identify precisely
what transfers from ordinary integers to nonstandard groups.

\begin{lemma}[Coset-invariant finite witnesses]\label{pma:lc:lem:cooper}
Fix an ordinary $m\ge1$. Let $P(y)$ be a predicate invariant under adding
\emph{every element of $mG$}. Let $a_i,b_j$ be finite lists of elements
of a $\Z$-group $G$. Then
\[
 \exists y\left(\bigwedge_i a_i<y\ \wedge\ \bigwedge_j y<b_j\ \wedge P(y)\right)
\]
is decided by a finite test as follows. If lower bounds occur, let
$a=\max_i a_i$ and test $y=a+s1$ for $1\le s\le m$. If only upper
bounds occur, let $b=\min_jb_j$ and test $y=b-s1$ for $1\le s\le m$.
If no bounds occur, test $y=r1$ for $0\le r<m$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Only completeness of the candidate list needs proof. Suppose first that
$y>a$ is a witness. Euclidean division of $y-a$ gives
$y-a=mq+r1$, $0\le r<m$. If $r>0$, then $q\ge0$; otherwise
$q\le-1$ would imply $y-a\le-1$. Set $s=r$ in this case.
If $r=0$, positivity implies $q\ge1$, and we set $s=m$.
In either case
\[
 a<a+s1\le y,\qquad y-(a+s1)\in mG.
\]
Thus $P(a+s1)$ holds and every upper bound remains satisfied. With only
upper bounds, apply the reflected argument to $b-y>0$. With no bounds,
use the remainder of $y$ modulo $mG$. The reverse implications are
immediate because every accepted candidate is itself a witness.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Classical Presburger elimination, in the needed form]
\label{pma:lc:thm:qe}
The axioms of $\Z$-groups admit quantifier elimination in $\pres$ and
form the complete theory of $(\Z,+,-,0,1,<,\equiv_m)_{m\ge2}$.
Their nonnegative cones are models of the full first-order theory
$\Th(\N;0,1,+,<)$, including all its induction instances.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
After disjunctive normal form, it suffices to eliminate one existential
variable from a conjunction. Negated inequalities and equalities become
finite combinations of strict inequalities using discreteness. Negated
congruences become finite disjunctions of the other ordinary residue
classes. Atoms not involving the eliminated variable are carried along.

Normalize the nonzero integer coefficients of the eliminated variable
$x$ by taking an ordinary common multiple $L>0$ of their absolute values
and replacing $Lx$ by $y$, with the additional condition $y\in LG$.
For example, with $a\ne0$ and $k=L/|a|$, an inequality $ax<t$ becomes
$\sgn(a)y<kt$, and
\[
 ax+t\in nG\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \sgn(a)y+kt\in nkG.
\]
The equivalence uses torsion-freeness: $kz\in nkG$ if and only if
$z\in nG$. Equalities with coefficient $1$ or $-1$ permit substitution.
What remains is a finite collection of upper and lower bounds on $y$
and a Boolean combination $P(y)$ of congruences. This predicate is
invariant under $mG$, where $m$ is a common multiple of the finitely
many moduli, including $L$.

Apply \cref{pma:lc:lem:cooper}. Choosing a maximum or minimum among a finite
list can itself be expressed by a finite disjunction of comparisons.
Thus the output is quantifier-free. Repeating eliminates all quantifiers.
A quantifier-free sentence concerns only ordinary integer numerals,
their order, and their standard residues, so it has the same truth value
in every $\Z$-group as in $\Z$. This proves completeness.

Finally, the nonnegative cone is definable, and any formula in its
language translates to a group formula by restricting its quantifiers
to $x\ge0$. The cone therefore satisfies exactly the first-order
sentences true in $\N$. In particular the translated induction instances
hold; they are consequences, not additional assumptions of the construction.
\end{proof}

\noindent\writetag{} \Cref{pma:lc:lem:cooper} is Part~I's coset-invariant
finite search, \cref{pma:pr:lem:finite} (source~05) and source~04's
\cref{pma:pr:thm:boundary}, and \cref{pma:lc:thm:qe} is the classical
\cref{pma:pr:thm:qe}; source~08 proves them again in its own notation.

\begin{remark}[The transfer condition is coset invariance]
For nonstandard $G$, invariance under the single ordinary shift $m1$
does not by itself imply invariance under $mG$. The latter is what the
proof needs and what congruence formulas supply. This distinction already
appears in the earlier companion manuscript \cite{s08:PriorPres}. ProveIt's
integer-specific periodicity lemmas must not be generalized merely by
replacing the carrier $\Z$ with an arbitrary ordered group.
\end{remark}

\section{An exact pointed topological-group classification}
\label{pma:lc:sec:classification}

\begin{theorem}[Locally compact signed Presburger groups]
\label{pma:lc:thm:classification}
Let $(G,+,<,0,1)$ be a $\Z$-group equipped with a locally compact Polish
group topology and a Borel order. There exist a finite $d\ge0$ and a
countable $\Z$-group $(E,+,<_{E},0,1_E)$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{pma:lc:eq:pointedsplit}
 (G,+,1)\cong_{\mathrm{top.grp},1}
 (\R^d\times E,+,(0,1_E)),
\end{equation}
where $E$ is discrete. On the identity component the given order is
real-linearly conjugate to the lexicographic order.

Conversely, every such pair $(d,E)$ admits a Borel $\Z$-group order on
the displayed pointed topological group. The theorem classifies the
pointed topological additive groups, not all possible full orders on them.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By \cref{pma:lc:thm:lca}, write $G=C\oplus D$ topologically, with
$C\cong\R^d$ and $D$ countable discrete. A divisible subgroup cannot
contain a nonzero ordinary integer by \cref{pma:lc:lem:finite}. Hence
$C\cap\Z1=0$.

Write the named element as $1=(v,e)$ in this splitting. The element
$e\in D$ has infinite order: otherwise a nonzero ordinary multiple of
$1$ would belong to $C$. Define a homomorphism on $\Z e$ by
$ne\mapsto nv$ and extend it to $f:D\to C$ using divisibility of $C$.
Since $D$ is discrete, $f$ is continuous. The shear
\[
 (c,x)\longmapsto(c-f(x),x)
\]
is a topological group automorphism taking $1$ to $(0,e)$.
Transport the order along it and let $E=\{0\}\times D$ with the
restricted order and named unit $1_E=e$.

The element $1_E$ is least positive in $E$, because it is least positive
in the whole group. If $(0,x)=m(c,y)+r(0,e)$ is standard division in
$G$, then $mc=0$ forces $c=0$. Thus the quotient lies in $E$, proving
its standard-residue property. The component order is handled by
\cref{pma:lc:lem:flag}.

Conversely, put the usual lexicographic order on $\R^d$, and compare
pairs first in this vector coordinate and then in $E$. The element
$(0,1_E)$ is least positive; vector divisibility together with division
in $E$ gives \eqref{pma:lc:eq:division}. The order is Borel because the vector
comparison is a finite union of coordinate conditions and $E$ is
countable discrete. This constructs every asserted topological type.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why the order caveat matters]
The component $G^\circ$ need not be convex in the full ordered group.
For example, take the ordered additive group with coordinates
$(r,q,s,n)\in\R\times\Q\times\R\times\Z$, compared in that order,
and give $\R^2\times(\Q\times\Z)$ the product topology with the second
factor discrete. This is a $\Z$-group: the first three coordinates are
divisible and $n$ supplies the remainder. Its connected component has
$q=n=0$, but a positive element in the $q$ coordinate lies between
positive elements at the two real scales. Thus countable discrete
scales can be interleaved with real scales.

There is a precise invariant distinguishing this example from a block
lexicographic product with all real coordinates first. Define
\[
 B_C=\{g\in G: |g|<c\text{ for every }c\in C_{>0}\}.
\]
In a block product $\R^d\times E$ with the vector coordinate compared
first, $B_C=\{0\}\times E$, so $G=C+B_C$. In the displayed interleaved
example, comparison with arbitrarily small positive vectors in each
real coordinate shows that $B_C$ consists exactly of the integer
coordinate. Hence $C+B_C$ misses every element with $q\ne0$.
Both $C$ and $B_C$ are preserved by an ordered topological-group
isomorphism, so the example has no such block-order presentation.
This does not affect the pointed additive-group decomposition proved above.
\end{remark}

\section{Positive cones: exact regularity and the dimension threshold}
\label{pma:lc:sec:cones}

A useful feature of finite-dimensional lexicographic order is that even
its arbitrary cuts have low descriptive complexity.

\begin{lemma}[Cuts in finite lexicographic powers]\label{pma:lc:lem:cuts}
Every upper set in $\R^d$ with the lexicographic order is $\Dtwo$ in the
usual Euclidean topology. No definability hypothesis on the upper set is
needed.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Induct on $d$. For $d=0$ there are only the empty and full sets. For
$d>0$, let $U$ be a nonempty proper upper set. Consider the first
coordinates of its elements. They form a nonempty upper subset of $\R$
with a finite lower endpoint $a$. The endpoint is finite because an
excluded first coordinate gives a lower bound and an included one gives
an upper bound for the infimum. If all first coordinates occurred, the
upper-set condition would make $U$ the whole space, contrary to assumption.
For first coordinate $>a$ every point belongs to $U$, and for first
coordinate $<a$ none does. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{pma:lc:eq:cutform}
 U=\{x:x_1>a\}\ \cup\ \bigl(\{a\}\times U'\bigr),
\end{equation}
where $U'$ is an upper set in lexicographic $\R^{d-1}$, possibly empty or
full. By induction $U'$ is both $F_\sigma$ and $G_\delta$.
Equation \eqref{pma:lc:eq:cutform} expresses $U$ as an $F_\sigma$ set. The
corresponding formula for its complement does the same, so $U$ is also
$G_\delta$. Empty and full upper sets are immediate.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Cone regularity and local compactness]\label{pma:lc:thm:cones}
Let $G$ satisfy \cref{pma:lc:thm:lca}, and write $G^\circ\cong\R^d$. Then:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $G_{\ge0}$ and the order relation are $\Dtwo$;
\item $M=G_{\ge0}$ with its subspace topology is Polish, and addition
      $M\times M\to M$ is continuous;
\item $M$ is locally compact if and only if $d\le1$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The cosets of $C=G^\circ$ are a countable family of clopen subsets of
$G$. Translate each to $C$ and use the linear coordinates of
\cref{pma:lc:lem:flag}. In each coset the intersection with $M$ is an upper
set for the induced lexicographic order. By \cref{pma:lc:lem:cuts}, each such
intersection and its relative complement are $F_\sigma$ in the coset,
and hence in $G$. Taking countably many unions shows that both $M$ and
$G\setminus M$ are $F_\sigma$. The order relation follows by the
continuous difference map. This proves the first assertion.

A $G_\delta$ subspace of a Polish space is Polish \cite{Kechris}.
The addition on $M$ is the restriction of the continuous group operation,
with its image in the subspace $M$, giving the second assertion.

If $d=0$, $M$ is discrete. If $d=1$, each nonempty coset slice is a real
line or an open or closed half-line, by the proof of \cref{pma:lc:lem:cuts}.
Each is locally compact. As the slices are open in $M$, their union is
locally compact.

Suppose now $d\ge2$. The slice at the identity component is the
nonnegative lexicographic cone in $\R^d$. If $M$ had a compact
neighborhood $K$ of zero, then for some $\epsilon>0$ its intersection
with $C$ would contain all points of this cone in the Euclidean ball
of radius $\epsilon$. In particular, for sufficiently large $n$,
\[
 x_n=(\epsilon/n,-\epsilon/2,0,\ldots,0)\in K.
\]
But in $G$ this sequence converges to
\[
 x=(0,-\epsilon/2,0,\ldots,0)<0.
\]
The inclusion $M\hookrightarrow G$ is continuous, so $K$ is compact
and therefore closed in the Hausdorff space $G$. This would put $x$
in $K\subset M$, a contradiction.
\end{proof}

\noindent\writetag{} Part~(1) is the locally compact case of source~07's
\cref{pma:cp:thm:cones}, proved here through \cref{pma:lc:lem:cuts}; parts~(2)
and~(3) are new. \Cref{pma:lc:prop:exactrank} below is an instance of
\cref{pma:pr:thm:orderoptimal} and \cref{pma:cp:prop:closedorder}.

\begin{corollary}[The locally compact positive Presburger case]
\label{pma:lc:cor:positiveclass}
In the class of positive cones of locally compact Borel signed
Presburger-group presentations, an uncountable cone is locally compact
exactly when the connected component of its signed group is one-dimensional.
Every cone in this class is Polish and has continuous addition.
\end{corollary}

This is a classification within the stated presentation class. It does
not assume, or prove, that every topological Presburger monoid arises
from a locally compact topological group in this manner.

\subsection{The order bound is genuinely rank two}
\begin{proposition}[Order is neither open nor closed]\label{pma:lc:prop:exactrank}
For an uncountable model in \cref{pma:lc:thm:classification}, the strict order
on $M=G_{\ge0}$ is $\Dtwo$ but neither open nor closed in $M^2$.
The same is true of the weak order.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Here $d>0$. Choose $c_n>0$ in $C$ with $c_n\to0$, for example the
points $(1/n,0,\ldots,0)$ in the flag coordinates. The component is
divisible, so $c_n>1$ for every $n$ by \cref{pma:lc:lem:finite}.
The pairs $(0,c_n)$ belong to the strict order and converge to $(0,0)$,
which does not; the relation is not closed. The point $(0,1)$ belongs
to the strict order, whereas $(c_n,1)$ does not and converges to it;
the relation is not open. Its complement after exchanging coordinates
is the weak order, giving both failures there as well. The upper
$\Dtwo$ bound follows from \cref{pma:lc:thm:cones} and restriction to $M^2$.
\end{proof}

There is no contradiction between continuous addition and this exact
rank-two behavior. Arithmetic comparison is a Borel relation in a topology
that is intentionally different from the discrete order topology.

\section{Definable relations and continuous standard division}
\label{pma:lc:sec:definability}

\begin{theorem}[Uniform definable regularity]\label{pma:lc:thm:definable}
In every model of \cref{pma:lc:thm:classification}, all Presburger-definable
subsets of $G^k$, with parameters and arbitrary finite $k$, are $\Dtwo$.
The same holds for definable subsets of the positive cone $M^k$ in its
relative topology. For each ordinary $m\ge1$, the quotient and remainder
maps in \eqref{pma:lc:eq:division} are continuous, both on $G$ and on $M$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Use the pointed splitting $G\cong\R^d\times E$. Since the real factor
is divisible,
\[
 mG=\R^d\times mE.
\]
This is clopen, because $E$ is discrete. Every congruence condition is
therefore the inverse image of a clopen set under a continuous affine
map. Every comparison is $\Dtwo$ by \cref{pma:lc:thm:cones}. Equality is
closed and hence $\Dtwo$ in a metrizable space. Affine integer-linear
terms are continuous. Quantifier elimination in \cref{pma:lc:thm:qe} now
expresses each definable set as a finite Boolean combination of
$\Dtwo$ sets, which is again $\Dtwo$.

For positive-cone formulas, restrict quantifiers to $x\ge0$, apply group
quantifier elimination, and then restrict the resulting set to $M^k$.
This preserves the stated relative Borel class.

The remainder map $r_m$ has the finitely many clopen fibers
$r1+mG$, $0\le r<m$, so it is continuous into the discrete finite set
of ordinary residues. Multiplication by the ordinary integer $m$ is a
homeomorphism from $G$ onto $mG$: in the product splitting it is
$(v,e)\mapsto(mv,me)$, whose inverse is continuous on its image.
Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{pma:lc:eq:continuousdivision}
 q_m(x)=(m\cdot)^{-1}\bigl(x-r_m(x)1\bigr)
\end{equation}
is continuous. If $x\ge0$, the quotient $q_m(x)$ is nonnegative;
otherwise $q_m(x)\le-1$ would imply $x\le-m+(m-1)=-1$.
Restricting these maps to $M$ proves the final claim.
\end{proof}

\noindent\writetag{} This is the statement of \cref{pma:pr:thm:definableborel}
and \cref{pma:cp:thm:general}(iii) for a class that also admits the
interleaved, non-block orders of the remark after
\cref{pma:lc:thm:classification}. It answers source~07's question on uniform
definability bounds (\cref{pma:cp:sec:questions}) within that class.

\subsection{A model in every finite real dimension}
Take $E=\Z$ and order $\R^d\times\Z$ lexicographically, with the
integer coordinate last. Write
\begin{equation}\label{pma:lc:eq:Md}
 G_d=\R^d\lex\Z,\qquad M_d=(G_d)_{\ge0}.
\end{equation}
For $x=(v,n)$,
\begin{equation}\label{pma:lc:eq:explicitdivision}
 r_m(v,n)=n\bmod m,
 \qquad q_m(v,n)=\left(v/m,\left\lfloor n/m\right\rfloor\right).
\end{equation}
Thus standard division is explicit and continuous. Every $M_d$ is a
Polish model of full Presburger arithmetic with continuous addition.
For $d=1$, \eqref{pma:lc:eq:Md} is exactly \eqref{pma:lc:eq:realmodel}; for $d\ge2$
the cone is not locally compact although the signed group is.

The case $d=1$ therefore provides the same affirmative construction for
the question cited in \cref{pma:lc:sec:intersection}, already given in
\cite{s08:PriorPres}. The advance here is the classification and dimensional
boundary, not a second priority claim for that example.

\noindent\writetag{} $M_1$ is Part~I's $\MR$; for $d\ge2$ the models $M_d$ are
new to the collection.

\subsection{Why ordinary scalar operations are not ring multiplication}
All maps $x\mapsto nx$ for ordinary $n\in\Z$ are continuous group
homomorphisms. This remains compatible with the complete absence of an
order-compatible ring multiplication in uncountable models. Presburger
arithmetic only provides these externally indexed scalar maps, not a
binary multiplication of arbitrary elements.

Likewise, low Borel complexity of every definable relation does not make
all definable functions continuous. For instance, truncated subtraction
on the positive cone can fail to be continuous. A claim of continuity
requires an argument such as \eqref{pma:lc:eq:continuousdivision}, not merely
quantifier elimination.

\section{Even unordered multiplication has few Borel scalars}
\label{pma:lc:sec:scalars}

The main theorem forbids multiplication preserving the order, without
any measurability assumption. We now remove order compatibility instead
and ask how much measurable multiplication can survive. In this section
the specified Borel $\Z$-group order and the multiplication may be
unrelated; their named additive unit $1$ is the same.

\subsection{Automatic continuity and component rigidity}
\begin{lemma}[Classical automatic continuity]\label{pma:lc:lem:auto}
A Borel homomorphism between Polish groups is continuous.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
We give the abelian case needed here. Let $h:G\to H$ be Borel and
additive. For a neighborhood $W$ of zero in $H$, choose an open
neighborhood $V$ with $V-V\subseteq W$. Separability gives countably
many translates of $V$ covering $H$. Their Borel preimages cover the
Baire space $G$, so one preimage $B$ is nonmeager.

A nonmeager set with the Baire property has $B-B$ as a neighborhood of
zero. Indeed, it is comeager in some nonempty open set $U$; for small
$t$, the set $U\cap(t+U)$ is nonempty open, and the two translated
comeager sets meet, putting $t$ in $B-B$. It follows that $h^{-1}(W)$
contains a neighborhood of zero, because $h(B-B)\subseteq V-V$.
Thus $h$ is continuous at zero, and hence everywhere. This is the
