usual Baire-category automatic-continuity argument; see also
\cite{Kechris} for the Polish-group setting.
\end{proof}

\noindent\writetag{} \Cref{pma:lc:lem:auto} is the first half of source~09's
\cref{pma:ser:lem:automatic}.

\begin{lemma}[Injective maps cannot move a component into itself from outside]
\label{pma:lc:lem:preimage}
Let $G\cong\R^d\times E$ with $E$ discrete, and let $C=G^\circ$.
Every continuous injective additive endomorphism $h:G\to G$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{pma:lc:eq:preimage}
 h(C)=C,\qquad h^{-1}(C)=C.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Continuity and connectedness give $h(C)\subseteq C$. The restriction
to $C$ is a continuous additive map between finite-dimensional real
vector groups, hence real-linear: first use rational scalars, then
continuity and rational approximation for real scalars. Injectivity
makes this linear endomorphism bijective. If $h(g)\in C$, choose
$c\in C$ with $h(c)=h(g)$. Injectivity gives $g=c$, proving the
preimage statement.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The Borel-scalar subring}
\begin{definition}
For a ring multiplication on a topological additive group, define
\[
 \Bsc=\{a\in A: L_a:A\to A,\ x\mapsto ax,\ \text{is Borel}\}.
\]
We call this the \emph{Borel-scalar subring} once its subring property
has been proved.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Countably many Borel scalar multipliers]\label{pma:lc:thm:scalars}
Let $(G,+,<,0,1)$ be a locally compact Borel Polish $\Z$-group, and let
$A$ be any commutative unital integral-domain multiplication on its
underlying additive group, with this same $1$. Multiplication need not
respect the order. Then $\Bsc$ is a countable closed discrete subring,
and the component quotient map is injective on it. In particular:
\begin{enumerate}
\item no nonzero element of $C=G^\circ$ has a Borel scalar map;
\item if the pointed topological group is $\R^d\times\Z$, then
      $\Bsc=\Z1$;
\item if $G$ is uncountable, multiplication cannot be separately Borel
      in every scalar, and therefore cannot be jointly Borel.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
We have $C\cap\Z1=0$ and $1\notin C$ by the additive discreteness
and divisibility argument used in \cref{pma:lc:thm:classification}. If
$a\in C\setminus\{0\}$ and $L_a$ were Borel, then it would be
continuous by \cref{pma:lc:lem:auto}. It is injective because $A$ is a domain.
But $L_a(1)=a\in C$, contradicting \cref{pma:lc:lem:preimage}. This proves
$\Bsc\cap C=\{0\}$.

The maps $L_0,L_1$ are continuous. If $a,b\in\Bsc$, then
$L_{a-b}=L_a-L_b$ is Borel, and $L_{ab}=L_a\circ L_b$ is Borel.
Thus $\Bsc$ is a subring, and it contains $\Z1$. If two of its
elements have the same coset modulo $C$, their difference belongs to
$\Bsc\cap C$ and is zero. Its projection into the countable discrete
quotient $G/C$ is therefore injective.

Each open component contains at most one element of $\Bsc$. Within
that component a singleton is closed, and its complement is open.
Taking the union of these complements over all components proves that
$\Bsc$ is closed in $G$; the same component neighborhoods make it
discrete. It is countable because the quotient is countable.

If the pointed quotient is $\Z$ generated by the image of $1$, every
$a\in\Bsc$ has the same image as $n1$ for some ordinary $n$.
Injectivity of the projection gives $a=n1$. Finally, an uncountable
$G$ has $d>0$ and nonzero elements in $C$, whose scalar maps are not
Borel. Sections of a jointly Borel multiplication would all be Borel,
so joint Borelness is impossible as well.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Two independent obstructions]
Theorem~\ref{pma:lc:thm:headline} uses order-compatible multiplication, but no
regularity of its scalar maps. Theorem~\ref{pma:lc:thm:scalars} does not require
order compatibility, but bounds which scalar maps can be Borel. Neither
statement is obtained merely by rephrasing the other.
\end{remark}

\section{Examples isolating the hypotheses}
\label{pma:lc:sec:sharpness}

\subsection{The countable conclusion is optimal}
Every countable discretely ordered ring has a discrete Polish additive
group topology with Borel order and continuous multiplication. Ordinary
$\Z$ is the smallest example, but there are non-Archimedean ones, such
as $\Z[X]$ ordered by the sign of the highest nonzero coefficient.
Thus the conclusion is discreteness and countability, not that the
ordered ring must be isomorphic to $\Z$.

\subsection{Second countability cannot simply be deleted}
\begin{example}[An uncountable locally compact ordered ring presentation]
\label{pma:lc:ex:nonsep}
Let
\begin{equation}\label{pma:lc:eq:polyring}
 A_*=\Z+X\R[X]
\end{equation}
with the leading-coefficient order, so $X$ is positive and greater than
all ordinary integers. This is a discretely ordered domain. Give its
additive group the product decomposition
\begin{equation}\label{pma:lc:eq:nonsep}
 A_*=(\R X)\oplus D_*,\qquad
 D_*:=\Z\oplus X^2\R[X],
\end{equation}
where $\R X$ has its usual real-line topology and $D_*$ is discrete.
Then the additive group is Hausdorff and locally compact, the order is
Borel, and the ring is uncountable. The topology is not second-countable
or separable.
\end{example}
\begin{proof}
The leading-coefficient rule defines an ordered domain, and every
positive nonconstant polynomial exceeds all ordinary integers.
Positive constants are positive integers, so $1$ is least positive.
The product in \eqref{pma:lc:eq:nonsep} is locally compact. Its discrete factor
has cardinality $\mathfrak c$, making the family of open components
uncountable.

For Borelness, a component with nonzero degree-at-least-two part has a
constant sign, independent of the $X$ coefficient and integer constant.
The union of all such positive components is clopen. On components
with no degree-at-least-two part, positivity is the ordinary condition
$r>0$, with the boundary $r=0$ governed by the integer constant.
These conditions and their complements are countable unions of closed
sets. Thus the positive cone is in fact $\Dtwo$ in this metrizable,
nonseparable topology.
\end{proof}

Here $V=\R X$ has the order unit $X$, and
\[
 |A_*/V|=|V|=\mathfrak c.
\]
So the cardinal lower bound of \cref{pma:lc:thm:amplification} is attained.
Multiplication by $X$ takes the connected line $\R X$ injectively into
the discrete $X^2\R$ coordinate. It is not continuous. It is not even
Borel: the inverse images of open sets obtained from arbitrary subsets
of that discrete coordinate give arbitrary subsets of the real line,
including non-Borel ones. No topological-ring claim is made for this
example.

\subsection{Borelness of the order is also essential}
\begin{example}[A locally compact Polish additive presentation with wild order]
\label{pma:lc:ex:wildorder}
Fix $d\ge1$. As rational vector spaces, both $\R^d$ and $X\R[X]$ have
dimension $\mathfrak c$. Choose a $\Q$-linear isomorphism
$\theta:\R^d\to X\R[X]$, and define
\[
 \Theta:\R^d\times\Z\longrightarrow A_*,\qquad
 \Theta(v,n)=n+\theta(v).
\]
Transport the ring structure and the leading-coefficient order of $A_*$
along $\Theta$, while retaining the usual product topology on
$\R^d\times\Z$. This gives an uncountable discretely ordered domain
with a locally compact Polish additive-group topology. Its order is
necessarily non-Borel by \cref{pma:lc:thm:headline}.
\end{example}

The dimension assertion uses ordinary ZFC basis theory: an infinite
dimensional vector space over a countable field has cardinality equal
to its basis cardinality, and a countable direct sum of copies of
$\R$ still has rational dimension $\mathfrak c$. This construction is
not effective and is not proposed as a usable notation system.

Independently give the same carrier the \emph{different}, standard Borel
lexicographic $\Z$-group order. The transported multiplication is not
compatible with that order, but \cref{pma:lc:thm:scalars} applies to it and
gives the exact identity
\[
 \Bsc=\Z1.
\]
Thus the Borel-scalar theorem has concrete algebraic realizations rather
than merely a conditional conclusion about nonexistent ring structures.

\subsection{Other boundaries}
Discreteness of the arithmetic order is indispensable: the ordinary real
field is uncountable, locally compact, and has continuous operations and
Borel order, but has no least positive element. Totality also matters:
our compact-order and finite-dimensional arguments do not apply to a
partial order without additional hypotheses.

We do not present a separable non-locally-compact counterexample with all
the other hypotheses of \cref{pma:lc:thm:headline}. The general Polish-group
problem is left as a research question, rather than being called a
settled sharpness result.

\section{Consequences for surreal and omnific presentations}
\label{pma:lc:sec:omnific}

\subsection{A set-sized no-go theorem for actual omnific rings}
ProveIt's surreal-number guide describes its construction of the ordered
surreal field, its omnific integer ring, the least-positive-element
property, and the constant-term map \cite{s08:RepoSurreal}. Only elementary
finite normal forms and the discrete order are needed below; none of
the repository's stronger unrefereed research claims is an input.

\begin{corollary}[Omnific additive topologies]\label{pma:lc:cor:omnific}
Let $A\subseteq\Oz$ be an uncountable set-sized unital subring, with
its inherited ordered-ring structure. There is no Hausdorff locally
compact second-countable additive-group topology on $A$ for which the
inherited order is Borel. This holds without requiring multiplication
to have any topological or measurable regularity.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Since $1\in A$ and $1$ is least positive in $\Oz$, it is least positive
in $A$. Apply \cref{pma:lc:thm:headline}.
\end{proof}

\noindent\mergetag{} Source~06 equips the nonnegative cone of the same ring
$\Z+t\R[t]$ with a locally compact Polish topology in which addition and
multiplication are continuous (\cref{pma:cp:thm:polynomial}). There is no
conflict with \cref{pma:lc:cor:omnific}: that topology lives on the cone and
is induced by no locally compact second-countable group topology on the ring
(\cref{pma:cp:sec:meet}).

This is not merely the observation that a Polish space has cardinality
at most continuum. The concrete ring
\begin{equation}\label{pma:lc:eq:ozpoly}
 \Z+\omega\R[\omega]
 =\left\{n+\sum_{j=1}^k r_j\omega^j:
       n\in\Z,\ r_j\in\R,\ k<\omega\right\}
\end{equation}
has cardinality exactly $\mathfrak c$, so its cardinality alone permits
a Polish coding. Finite surreal normal forms show that it is an ordered
copy of $A_*$ in \eqref{pma:lc:eq:polyring}. The theorem obstructs the
combination of Borel order, locally compact additive-group topology,
and the actual ordered multiplication even at this set-sized level.

\subsection{Finite additive fragments have excellent topology but no ring expansion}
\begin{corollary}[Finite surreal scales]\label{pma:lc:cor:finitescales}
For every ordinary $d\ge1$, let
\begin{equation}\label{pma:lc:eq:surrealGd}
 H_d=\left\{n+r_1\omega+\cdots+r_d\omega^d:
            n\in\Z,\ r_j\in\R\right\}\subseteq\Oz.
\end{equation}
Its inherited ordered additive group, with coefficient topology
$\R^d\times\Z$, is a locally compact Borel $\Z$-group. Its nonnegative
cone is Polish with continuous addition and continuous standard quotient
and remainder. That cone is locally compact exactly for $d=1$.
All its Presburger-definable relations are $\Dtwo$.

No alternative commutative unital ring multiplication on $H_d$ can
respect this addition, order, and named unit. In particular, failure
of closure under the actual surreal multiplication cannot be repaired
by inventing a different order-compatible multiplication on the same
carrier.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
In descending coefficient order $(r_d,\ldots,r_1,n)$, addition and
order identify $H_d$ with $G_d$ of \eqref{pma:lc:eq:Md}. Standard division
is \eqref{pma:lc:eq:explicitdivision}. Apply \cref{pma:lc:thm:cones,pma:lc:thm:definable}
and \cref{pma:lc:cor:noexpansion}. Alternatively, the last assertion follows
already from algebraic amplification with
$V=\sum_{j=1}^d\R\omega^j$ and order unit $u=\omega^d$:
$H_d/V\cong\Z$ is countable but $V$ is not.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Implications for exact symbolic systems}
These results distinguish three legitimate design choices. One may retain
finite surreal additive scales and continuous addition, accepting that
there is no compatible full multiplication. One may enlarge to a genuine
set-sized ordered ring, abandoning local compactness or separability of
an additive-group topology. Or one may transport the algebra to a
convenient Polish additive space and accept a non-Borel inherited order.
The examples in \cref{pma:lc:sec:sharpness} make the last two alternatives
mathematically explicit.

The earlier support-barrier manuscript concerns a different obstruction:
which exact Hahn or omnific normal-form families admit Borel coefficient
observations \cite{s08:PriorBorel}. Our obstruction requires neither
unrestricted supports nor an analysis of support validity. It already
appears for the finite polynomial ring \eqref{pma:lc:eq:ozpoly}.

\section{A formalization plan with explicit trust boundaries}
\label{pma:lc:sec:formalization}

The proof should be formalized in layers. Its algebraic core is much
smaller than its locally compact group input, so formalization need not
begin with Haar measure or the full theory of real Lie groups.

\paragraph{Layer A: finite-order arithmetic.}
Formalize that $[0,n1]$ contains exactly the ordinary numerals in a
discretely ordered group. Deduce that a subgroup disjoint from $\Z1$
has no nonzero element bounded by a standard integer, and that a
divisible subgroup has this property. The induction index is a natural
number in the metatheory of the formal development.

\paragraph{Layer B: amplification.}
For a subgroup $V$, an element $u\in V$, and the explicit order-unit
predicate, prove $uv\in V\Rightarrow v=0$. Package
\eqref{pma:lc:eq:cosetmap} as an injective additive map into the quotient
additive group. Then formalize \eqref{pma:lc:eq:domination} for finitely
supported coefficient families and derive the ordered direct-sum
embedding. No topological assumption, infinite series, or proper class
occurs in this layer.

\paragraph{Layer C: the topological interface.}
State a reusable interface consisting of an open connected subgroup
$C$, a topological additive-group isomorphism $C\cong\R^d$, and a
countable quotient. Prove arithmetic rigidity from this interface and
the Borel finite-dimensional flag lemma. Separately discharge the
interface using the compact-order obstruction and the principal LCA
structure theorem. A conditional theorem with this interface is useful,
but must not be advertised as an unconditional formalization of the
whole locally compact result.

\paragraph{Layer D: Presburger transfer.}
Prove \cref{pma:lc:lem:cooper} over an abstract $\Z$-group with a predicate
invariant on cosets of $mG$. Prove the group theory of standard division
before connecting to syntax. ProveIt already supplies syntax, coefficient
normalization, and an executable integer decision procedure, according
to its inspected guide \cite{s08:RepoPres}; the semantic transfer must
be proved rather than inferred from that guide.

\paragraph{Layer E: topology of presentations.}
Formalize lexicographic cuts by finite dimension, prove their
$F_\sigma$ and $G_\delta$ descriptions, and derive the cone dimension
threshold and continuity of the standard quotient and remainder maps.
The scalar theorem then reuses an automatic-continuity theorem and
finite-dimensional linear algebra.

No Lean files are included as if they had been checked. The companion
Python script validates finite exact-arithmetic examples, not proofs
in Lean or in any other kernel. In particular it does not test Haar
measure, nonmeager sets, arbitrary Borel orders, cardinal injections,
or the principal LCA structure theorem.

\section{Further research questions and concrete next targets}
\label{pma:lc:sec:questions}

\begin{question}[Remove local compactness]\label{pma:lc:q:removelc}
Can an uncountable discretely ordered ring have a Polish additive-group
topology with Borel order when multiplication has no regularity at all?
\end{question}
The proof here uses local compactness to obtain a finite-dimensional
open component with an order unit. A non-locally-compact Polish group
can have an infinite-dimensional component or be totally disconnected
without being discrete. An extension would need a substitute for that
finite-dimensional argument, perhaps an uncountable subgroup to which
\cref{pma:lc:thm:amplification} applies and a suitably small quotient. Neither
a proof nor a counterexample is claimed here.

\noindent\writetag{} Source~09's \cref{pma:ser:thm:discrete} answers this
question negatively when every scalar map $x\mapsto ax$ is Borel and the
positive cone has the Baire property; without any regularity of
multiplication it stays open.

\begin{question}[Topological group completion of positive models]\label{pma:lc:q:completion}
Which locally compact Polish cancellative Presburger monoids admit a
Hausdorff locally compact Polish topology on their Grothendieck groups
that induces the original topology on the positive cone?
\end{question}
A positive answer under usable hypotheses would convert
\cref{pma:lc:cor:positiveclass} into a broader classification of positive
arithmetic presentations. The relevant issue is topological embedding
of the monoid into its group completion, not the elementary algebraic
existence of that completion. Conditions on translations, quotient maps,
and neighborhoods of zero are natural starting points.

\noindent\writetag{} Source~09 asks the same question as
\cref{pma:ser:q:cones}. Source~06's cone of $\Z+t\R[t]$ is a data point
(\cref{pma:cp:sec:meet}): a locally compact Polish cone with continuous
addition, whose group of differences has no locally compact second-countable
group topology with Borel order, by \cref{pma:lc:thm:headline}.

\begin{question}[Classify the orders, not only the pointed groups]
For a fixed countable $\Z$-group $E$, classify Borel $\Z$-group orders
on $\R^d\times E$ up to continuous additive order isomorphism fixing $1$.
\end{question}
The real component carries a finite flag, but discrete coordinates can
occur between real scales, as the example following
\cref{pma:lc:thm:classification} shows. The cut description in
\cref{pma:lc:lem:cuts} and the continuous separating homomorphisms of the
classical measurable-order literature suggest finite flag data together
with countable extension data. Determining which data are complete
invariants is a concrete next classification problem.

\begin{question}[Weaken Borel order carefully]
Which hereditary measurability assumptions on an invariant order suffice
for arithmetic rigidity? In particular, what replaces Borelness if
restrictions to every compact subgroup and every closed real subspace
are required to be Haar measurable or to have the Baire property?
\end{question}
An ambient Haar-measurable set can have uncontrolled behavior on a
null closed subgroup. The present proof intentionally uses Borelness
to avoid that problem. A weakening should state restriction hypotheses
explicitly rather than treating ambient measurability as automatically
hereditary.

\begin{question}[Cardinal and topological size spectra]
For nonseparable locally compact additive presentations of discretely
ordered rings, what cardinalities of the component quotient can occur
relative to the identity component and the weight of the topology?
\end{question}
Example~\ref{pma:lc:ex:nonsep} attains $|A/C|=|C|=\mathfrak c$ with a real
component. The direct-sum strengthening in
\cref{pma:lc:thm:amplification} records more than one injection. It may impose
additional ordered-group or module constraints on possible quotients
even when it yields no stronger bare infinite-cardinal inequality.

\begin{question}[Realize Borel-scalar subrings]
For fixed $\R^d\times E$, which countable subrings can arise as
$\mathcal B(A)$ for integral-domain multiplications on the additive group?
\end{question}
The projection of $\mathcal B(A)$ into $E$ is injective, and it contains
the ordinary integers. When $E=\Z$ the answer is exactly $\Z$.
For larger $E$, continuous scalar maps act real-linearly on the
component and induce additive maps on $E$; compatibility of these two
actions and their extension data may give a useful algebraic
realization criterion.

\begin{question}[Effective and reverse-mathematical strength]
What is the exact effective content of extracting a finite real flag
from a code for a Borel order, and of proving the resulting rigidity
statement over weak base theories?
\end{question}
The current ZFC proof uses category, separation, Haar measure, and
an LCA structure theorem. It is not an analysis of their strength in
second-order arithmetic. Glazer's separate Question~1 about the strength
of his theorem \cite{Glazer} remains outside the results proved here;
an argument for the locally compact fragment should not be substituted
for an answer to that question.

\noindent\writetag{} \Cref{pma:sec:glazer} discusses Glazer's Question~1 and
the speculation that follows his Question~2.

\begin{question}[Certified amplification and notation design]
Can the algebraic amplification theorem be kernel-checked first, and
then used as an automatic obstruction to proposed finite-scale
omnific or non-Archimedean ring interfaces?
\end{question}
A small certificate could consist of a divisible or integer-disjoint
additive subgroup, an order unit, and an upper bound on the quotient's
size. This would separate a universally reusable algebraic theorem
from application-specific normal-form calculations. The finite-scale
examples in \cref{pma:lc:cor:finitescales} are a natural first target, followed
by the Borel-scalar theorem and the full topological interface.

\section{Conclusions of Part IV}

Locally compact arithmetic separates cleanly into an additive possibility
and a multiplicative obstruction. Uncountable Polish Presburger models
with continuous addition are available, and their locally compact signed
group presentations have a precise finite-dimensional structure. Their
order relations have exact rank-two regularity, and their standard
quotient and remainder operations remain continuous.

But a discretely ordered ring cannot use that topology in the uncountable
second-countable case. The obstruction does not ask multiplication to be
Borel: it uses multiplication only to create an impossible injection
from a real component into a countable component quotient. When order
compatibility is dropped instead, only countably many scalar maps can
remain Borel. This gives a concrete structural connection between
Glazer's arithmetic-presentation questions and ProveIt's finite surreal,
omnific, and Presburger developments, while keeping the unresolved
Polish-group and formal-verification questions explicit.

\part{Polish topologies at the Hahn--Puiseux--Levi-Civita boundary}\label{pma:part:ser}

\noindent\writetag{} This part is source~09 of batch~86,
\emph{Polishability at the Hahn--Puiseux--Levi-Civita Boundary: Automatic
order bounds, local Polish hulls, and countable omnific integer parts}
(dated October~3, 2026; ``prepared with AI assistance''), pinned to
repository commit \texttt{4af956fe0}. All unmarked text is source~09's; its
labels carry the prefix \texttt{pma:ser:}. It continues \cref{pma:part:bor}:
source~03 asked when Hahn-series codes form a \emph{standard Borel} space with
observable coefficients, and source~09 asks when the additive group of a
series field can be made \emph{Polish} with measurable arithmetic. Its
``preceding support manuscript'' \cite{s09:PriorSupport} is source~03
(Part~II), and its Presburger companion \cite{s09:PriorPresburger} is
source~05 (Part~I). Its finite checks are shipped as
\fn{code/09-hahn-polishability-verify.py} with the record
\fn{data/09-hahn-polishability-verification.json}.

\paragraph{Convention \writetag.} In this part \emph{$t$ is a positive
infinitesimal}, exponents of $t$ that are negative give infinite elements, and
the surreal realization is $t^q\mapsto\omega^{-q}$ (\cref{pma:ser:eq:surreal}).
This is the reverse of \cref{pma:part:cp}, where $t$ is positive infinite, and
agrees with source~03's increasing-$t$ convention; 03's growth variable is
$X=t^{-1}$. Source~09's macros for its fields are renamed only in the source:
the Puiseux field $\Pk$ is printed exactly as before and is not 03's cone
$\PP$ (\cref{pma:sec:notation}).

\noindent\textbf{Reading routes.}
Sections~\ref{pma:ser:sec:category}--\ref{pma:ser:sec:hahn} contain the main obstruction and
classification. Sections~\ref{pma:ser:sec:puiseux}--\ref{pma:ser:sec:complexity} explain the
Puiseux/Levi-Civita contrast. Sections~\ref{pma:ser:sec:realclosed}--\ref{pma:ser:sec:surreal}
connect the analysis to real closed fields, arithmetic, and ProveIt.
The final sections separate proved conclusions from further research.

\section{The research intersection and the questions answered}
\label{pma:ser:sec:intro}

Elliot Glazer's \emph{A Topological Tennenbaum Theorem}
\cite{Glazer} studies Borel arithmetic on uncountable Polish spaces.
Its Theorem~2 excludes continuity of either arithmetic operation for Borel
models of $Q+I\Delta_0+\mathrm{Exp}$, and its Corollary~1 excludes continuous
addition with Borel multiplication for uncountable Polish models of
$Q+\mathrm{IOpen}$. This provides a specific, documented intersection with
ProveIt's work on ordered algebra, exact Hahn normal forms, omnific integers,
and formal arithmetic. No inference about Glazer's present employment or
endorsement is needed.

The ProveIt source collection distinguishes formalized constructions from
unrefereed research reports. Its surreal documentation describes the normal-form
realization of the surreal field and the integer-constant, nonnegative-growth
normal forms of the omnific integers \cite{s09:ProveIt}. We use those as motivation
and as a possible implementation interface, not as substitutes for proofs of
the topological assertions below.

Two earlier manuscripts prepared for this research program already address
Polish Presburger positive cones and descriptive support barriers
\cite{s09:PriorPresburger,s09:PriorSupport}. In particular, the preceding support
manuscript discusses the coanalytic obstruction for unrestricted rational Hahn
supports. Repeating that obstruction would not answer the next question:
\emph{even when exact series codes form a standard Borel space, can their
additive group be made Polish without losing measurable arithmetic?}

The answer is sensitive to which series are admitted. Fix a countable ordered
field $k$, and use $t$ as a positive infinitesimal. Three natural fields are
\begin{align}
 \Pk&=\bigcup_{n\geq1}k((t^{1/n!})), \label{pma:ser:eq:Pintro}\\
 \Lk&=\left\{\sum_{q\in\Q}a_qt^q:
  \supp(a)\cap(-\infty,B]\text{ is finite for every }B\in\Q\right\},
  \label{pma:ser:eq:Lintro}\\
 \Hk&=k((t^\Q)). \label{pma:ser:eq:Hintro}
\end{align}
They are, respectively, the Puiseux field, the left-finite Levi-Civita field,
and the full rational Hahn field. Their inclusions are strict. The middle
field, not the largest or the descriptively simplest one, admits the desired
Polish arithmetic.

\subsection{The central conclusions}

The term \emph{admissible} is made precise in Definition~\ref{pma:ser:def:admissible}.
It requires a Polish additive group, a Baire-measurable positive cone, and Borel
multiplication by each fixed scalar. It does not initially require jointly
continuous multiplication.

\begin{statementbox}
\textbf{Main results.}
For a nonzero ordered abelian group $\Gamma$, the full Hahn field
$k((t^\Gamma))$ has an admissible Polish topology if and only if $k$ is
countable and $\Gamma\cong\Z$ as an ordered group.
For countable ordered $k$, $\Pk$ has no admissible Polish topology, while
$\Lk$ has exactly one: its complete valuation topology.
Every discretely ordered ring with an admissible Polish additive group is
countable. Every integer part of an admissible Polish ordered field is
countable and closed discrete.
\end{statementbox}

There is also a sharp local converse. Every countable family of rational
Hahn series lies in a complete separable subfield, and these Polish subfields
have no maximal member. Exactly $2^{\aleph_0}$ of them are needed to cover
the full Hahn field (Theorem~\ref{pma:ser:thm:covernumber}). Over $\ka$ they can be
chosen real closed and truncation-closed, with canonical countable omnific
integer parts. Thus the Levi-Civita field is not the largest possible positive
construction: the global obstruction concerns simultaneous freedom of
prefixes, not any one prescribed countable support pattern.

The main advance over a support-code obstruction is quantification over
\emph{all} admissible Polish topologies on the abstract field. The Hahn
classification is not defeated by abandoning coefficientwise coding. The
Puiseux obstruction is stronger still in a different direction: the code
domain is already $F_\sigma$, but no compatible Polish group topology can
support the required arithmetic.

For $k=\ka$, the real algebraic numbers, both $\Pk$ and $\Lk$ are real closed
and share the same explicitly described countable integer part. Its
nonnegative cone satisfies open induction. The integer-part algebra therefore
does not distinguish these two sharply different topological situations.

\subsection{What is and is not claimed as new}

The article gives complete arguments for its central classifications and
regularity conclusions. The category tools are classical. The construction
and completeness of Levi-Civita fields belong to established non-Archimedean
analysis; see, for example, Shamseddine--Berz \cite{SB}. Generalized series and
Newton--Puiseux theory are classical, with a useful account in Poonen
\cite{Poonen}. The coanalytic support obstruction and the negative-infinitesimal
floor correction are already present in the preceding project materials.

The contribution developed here is their combination into a
presentation-invariant classification, the Puiseux versus Levi-Civita
Polishability separation, the local Polish-hull and exact field-cover theorems, and the resulting
integer-part and floor-regularity statements. These are proposed research contributions relative to the inspected
materials. They are not accompanied by a certified global priority claim.
We answer the precisely formulated questions in this article; we do not claim
to have newly resolved either of Glazer's printed open questions.

\section{Conventions: four different notions of presentation}
\label{pma:ser:sec:conventions}

All fields, groups, supports, and topological spaces in the main arguments are
sets in ZFC. Every ordered field has its specified field-compatible linear
order. A \emph{discretely ordered ring} is a commutative ordered unital ring
whose least positive element is $1$. An \emph{integer part} of an ordered
field $F$ is a discretely ordered unital subring $I\subseteq F$ such that each
$x\in F$ has an $a\in I$ satisfying $a\leq x<a+1$.

A Polish space is separable and completely metrizable. A set has the
\emph{Baire property} when it differs from an open set by a meager set.
Every Borel set has this property. A Polish additive group has jointly
continuous addition and continuous negation.

\begin{definition}[Admissible Polish arithmetic]\label{pma:ser:def:admissible}
A topology $\tau$ on an ordered field or discretely ordered ring $F$ is
\emph{admissible} when:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $(F,+,\tau)$ is a Polish topological group;
\item $F_{>0}$ has the Baire property in $\tau$;
\item for each $a\in F$, the map $L_a:F\to F$, $L_a(x)=ax$, is Borel.
\end{enumerate}
An admissible Polish presentation through another carrier means a transported
topology satisfying these conditions. It is therefore equivalent to an
admissible topology on the original algebraic carrier.
\end{definition}

The following distinctions prevent several false conclusions.
A standard Borel presentation supplies measurable codes and operations, but
need not supply continuous addition. A \emph{Polishable Borel group} is one
whose given Borel structure comes from a Polish group topology. An admissible
ordered-field topology may initially be allowed to change the Borel structure;
our absolute nonexistence results exclude that possibility too. Finally, a
Polish topology on a \emph{positive cone} with continuous addition is only a
topological monoid. It is not a Polish topology on the group of differences.

In particular, the ring theorem below cannot be applied by silently adjoining
negative elements to a Polish model of Presburger arithmetic. Extending the
algebra is easy; extending the topology with all the required properties is a
separate problem.

For a Hahn series $f=\sum a_\gamma t^\gamma$, the support is well ordered in
$\Gamma$, and
\[
 v(f)=\min\supp(f)\quad(f\ne0),\qquad v(0)=+\infty.
\]
The order is determined by the coefficient at the least support exponent.
Positive exponents represent small quantities. In particular, if
$\alpha<\beta$ and $c\in k_{>0}$, then $ct^\alpha>t^\beta$.
We distinguish this formal order from the ordinary absolute value of a
coefficient when $k$ happens to be a subfield of $\R$.

\section{Category forces local order bounds}
\label{pma:ser:sec:category}

The mechanism is a short application of classical category theory. We include
the needed proofs to make the hypothesis boundary visible. Pettis's theorem,
automatic continuity, and the open mapping theorem are classical; Chen
\cite{Chen} reviews their relationship and develops localic counterparts.

\begin{lemma}[Pettis, additive form]\label{pma:ser:lem:pettis}
Let $G$ be a Polish group and let $A\subseteq G$ have the Baire property and be
nonmeager. Then $A-A$ contains a neighborhood of $0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
There is a nonempty open set $U$ on which $A$ is comeager. Choose a nonempty
open $V$ and a neighborhood $W$ of $0$ so small that $V\subseteq U$ and
$V-g\subseteq U$ for every $g\in W$. For each such $g$, both $A\cap V$ and
$(g+A)\cap V$ are comeager in $V$. Their intersection is nonempty because a
nonempty open subspace of a Polish space is Baire. An element $x$ of the
intersection satisfies $x\in A$ and $x-g\in A$, so $g\in A-A$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Automatic continuity and open mapping]\label{pma:ser:lem:automatic}
A Borel homomorphism between Polish groups is continuous. A continuous
surjective homomorphism between Polish groups is open.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For the first assertion, let $\phi:G\to H$ be Borel and let $U$ be a
neighborhood of $0$ in $H$. Choose an open neighborhood $V$ with
$V-V\subseteq U$. Separability gives a countable cover of $H$ by translates
of $V$. Their inverse images are Borel and cover $G$, so one inverse image
$A$ is nonmeager. Then $A-A\subseteq\phi^{-1}(U)$, and
Lemma~\ref{pma:ser:lem:pettis} gives continuity at $0$.

For the second assertion, let $U$ be a neighborhood of $0$ in $G$, and choose
an open neighborhood $V$ with $V-V\subseteq U$. Countably many translates of
$V$ cover $G$, hence their images cover $H$. The set $\phi(V)$ is analytic,
and therefore has the Baire property. At least one of its translates is
nonmeager. Pettis gives a neighborhood of $0$ inside
$\phi(V)-\phi(V)\subseteq\phi(U)$.
\end{proof}

The descriptive-set-theoretic facts about analytic sets used here are standard
\cite{Kechris}. In particular, continuous images of Polish spaces are analytic,
analytic sets have the Baire property, and an analytic set with analytic
complement is Borel.

\begin{lemma}[A Baire order is locally bounded]\label{pma:ser:lem:bound}
Let $G$ be a nontrivial linearly ordered abelian group with a Polish group
topology. If $P=\{x:x>0\}$ has the Baire property, some neighborhood of $0$
is contained in a bounded order interval $[-a,a]$, with $a>0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For a discrete group, take the neighborhood $\{0\}$ and any $a>0$.
Suppose the topology is not discrete. Then $\{0\}$ is nowhere dense.
Inversion is a homeomorphism and takes $P$ to $-P$. If $P$ were meager, then
$G=P\cup(-P)\cup\{0\}$ would be meager in itself, contrary to the Baire
category theorem. Thus $P$ is nonmeager.

Choose a nonempty open set $U$ on which $P$ is comeager, and choose
$p\in P\cap U$. Let $V$ be a symmetric open neighborhood of $0$ with
$W=p+V\subseteq U$. The reflection $x\mapsto 2p-x$ maps $W$ onto itself.
Consequently, both $P\cap W$ and $(2p-P)\cap W$ are comeager in $W$. The set
\[
 A=W\cap P\cap(2p-P)
\]
has the Baire property and is nonmeager. Every $x\in A$ satisfies
$0<x<2p$. Therefore $A-A\subseteq(-2p,2p)$. Pettis gives a neighborhood of
$0$ inside this interval. Take $a=2p$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Automatic order-topology refinement]\label{pma:ser:thm:refinement}
Every admissible Polish topology on an ordered field refines its order
topology. More generally, the same conclusion holds for a Polish additive
group topology with Baire positive cone and continuous fixed scalar maps.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
In the admissible case every $L_a$ is continuous by
Lemma~\ref{pma:ser:lem:automatic}. Choose a neighborhood $U$ of $0$ with
$U\subseteq[-a,a]$ as in Lemma~\ref{pma:ser:lem:bound}. For any $\varepsilon>0$, put
\[
 c=\frac{a+1}{\varepsilon}>0.
\]
Then $L_c^{-1}(U)$ is a neighborhood of $0$, and its elements satisfy
\[
 |x|\leq\frac{a}{c}
 =\varepsilon\frac{a}{a+1}<\varepsilon.
\]
Thus every interval $(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)$ contains a neighborhood of
$0$. Translating this statement at every point of any order interval proves
that the interval is open in the given topology.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Uniqueness when the order topology is Polish]
\label{pma:ser:cor:unique}
If the order topology of an ordered field is a Polish group topology, then it
is the only possible admissible Polish topology on that field.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The identity from an admissible topology to the order topology is a continuous
bijective group homomorphism between Polish groups. It is open by
Lemma~\ref{pma:ser:lem:automatic}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Order-free hypothesis for real closed fields]
\label{pma:ser:cor:realorder}
Let $F$ be real closed. If its additive group is Polish and its multiplication
is jointly Borel, then its unique field order is Borel. Consequently,
Theorem~\ref{pma:ser:thm:refinement} applies without separately assuming measurable order.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The positive cone is the image of $F\setminus\{0\}$ under the Borel map
$x\mapsto x^2$, so is analytic. Its complement is the union of $\{0\}$ with
the image of $F\setminus\{0\}$ under $x\mapsto -x^2$, hence is analytic too.
The analytic/coanalytic criterion gives Borelness. The scalar maps are sections
of multiplication and therefore Borel.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The last corollary uses \emph{joint} Borel multiplication to obtain a Borel
square map. Merely postulating Borelness of each fixed scalar map does not by
itself justify substituting the variable into both multiplication arguments.
\end{remark}

\section{Discretely ordered rings and integer parts}
\label{pma:ser:sec:rings}

\begin{theorem}[Automatic discreteness]\label{pma:ser:thm:discrete}
An admissible Polish topology on a discretely ordered ring is discrete.
In particular, the ring is countable.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Lemma~\ref{pma:ser:lem:bound}, choose a neighborhood $U$ of $0$ contained in
$[-a,a]$, where $a>0$. Multiplication by $b=a+1$ is a continuous additive
homomorphism. Thus $W=L_b^{-1}(U)$ is a neighborhood of $0$.
If $x\ne0$, discrete order gives $|x|\geq1$, whence
$|bx|\geq b>a$. Such an $x$ cannot belong to $W$. Therefore $W=\{0\}$.
A discrete separable space is countable.
\end{proof}

\noindent\writetag{} \Cref{pma:ser:thm:discrete} and source~08's
\cref{pma:lc:thm:headline} have the same conclusion under incomparable
hypotheses: 09 needs Borel scalar maps and a cone with the Baire property but
no local compactness, 08 needs local compactness but no regularity of
multiplication. Together they answer source~08's \cref{pma:lc:q:removelc} under
the Borel-scalar hypothesis only. The non-example below is the group of
Part~I's real model.

\begin{corollary}[A non-Polishable Borel group criterion]\label{pma:ser:cor:ringborel}
Let $R$ be an uncountable standard Borel discretely ordered ring whose order,
addition, negation, and multiplication are Borel. Its additive Borel group is
not Polishable.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
A compatible Polish group topology would preserve the stipulated Borel order
and scalar maps and would contradict Theorem~\ref{pma:ser:thm:discrete}.
\end{proof}

This result needs no induction scheme. That does not make it a theorem with
weaker hypotheses than Glazer's arithmetic result: here a Polish
\emph{group of differences} is assumed from the outset. Glazer's theorem
concerns the arithmetic domain itself. These are different boundaries.

\begin{theorem}[All integer parts are countable]\label{pma:ser:thm:integerparts}
Let $F$ have an admissible Polish topology. Every discretely ordered unital
subring $I\subseteq F$ is countable and closed discrete. In particular this
holds for every integer part, without any measurability assumption on $I$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{pma:ser:thm:refinement}, the interval $(-1,1)$ is open in $F$.
Its intersection with $I$ is $\{0\}$, so every translate of a sufficiently
small order interval meets $I$ in at most one point. For example, two distinct
points of $I$ cannot lie in an interval of radius $1/3$, because their
nonzero difference has absolute value at least $1$.

Each point of $I$ therefore has an open neighborhood containing no other
point of $I$. A countable base supplies distinct base elements for distinct
points, proving countability. To see closedness, suppose $x\in\overline I$.
The interval $(x-1/3,x+1/3)$ contains at most one point $a$ of $I$, and must
contain one by closure. If $x\ne a$, a smaller neighborhood of $x$ avoiding
$a$ contradicts $x\in\overline I$. Hence $x=a\in I$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[An important non-example]
The lexicographically ordered group $\R\times\Z$, with its ordinary product
group topology, has a Borel discrete order: $(0,1)$ is its least positive
element. It is uncountable and Polish. This does not contradict
Theorem~\ref{pma:ser:thm:discrete}, since no ordered-ring structure with the required
scalar maps has been supplied. The scalar $a+1$ in the proof is a genuine
ring-theoretic resource.
\end{example}

\section{A complete classification for full Hahn fields}
\label{pma:ser:sec:hahn}

The existence and algebraic operations of full Hahn fields are classical
\cite{Poonen}. We use the entire well-ordered-support field, not a smaller
convergence-restricted subfield.

\begin{lemma}[A bounded increasing sequence in a noncyclic group]
\label{pma:ser:lem:gamma}
Let $\Gamma\ne0$ be an ordered abelian group. Either $\Gamma$ is
order-isomorphic to $\Z$, or there are
\[
 0<\gamma_0<\gamma_1<\cdots<\delta
\]
with $\delta\in\Gamma_{>0}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Suppose first that $\Gamma$ has no least positive element. Choose
$0<\varepsilon_0<\delta$ and then recursively
$0<\varepsilon_{n+1}<\varepsilon_n$. Put
$\gamma_n=\delta-\varepsilon_n$.

Suppose instead that $\varepsilon$ is the least positive element.
If every positive element is bounded by a positive integer multiple of
$\varepsilon$, then for every $x>0$ choose the least $n$ with
$x\leq n\varepsilon$. The difference
$x-(n-1)\varepsilon$ is positive and at most $\varepsilon$, hence equals
$\varepsilon$. Thus $x=n\varepsilon$ and $\Gamma=\Z\varepsilon$.
Otherwise some $\delta$ exceeds every $n\varepsilon$, and
$\gamma_n=(n+1)\varepsilon$ has the required properties.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Disjoint intervals in a Hahn field]\label{pma:ser:lem:separated}
Let $H=k((t^\Gamma))$ with $\Gamma\ne0$.
If $k$ is uncountable, its order topology has an uncountable family of
pairwise disjoint nonempty open intervals.
If $\Gamma$ is not order-isomorphic to $\Z$, it has such a family of
cardinality $2^{\aleph_0}$, even with centers having only $0$--$1$ coefficients.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For uncountable $k$, fix $\delta>0$. The intervals
\[
 (c-t^\delta,c+t^\delta),\qquad c\in k,
\]
are disjoint: every positive nonzero constant dominates $2t^\delta$.

For noncyclic $\Gamma$, use Lemma~\ref{pma:ser:lem:gamma}. For
$b\in2^{\N}$ define
\[
 f_b=\sum_{n\geq0} b(n)t^{\gamma_n}.
\]
This is a legal Hahn series: each support is a subset of the increasing
sequence $(\gamma_n)$ and hence is well ordered. If $b\ne c$, let $m$ be
the first index where they differ. Then
$v(f_b-f_c)=\gamma_m<\delta$, so
$|f_b-f_c|>2t^\delta$. The intervals
$(f_b-t^\delta,f_b+t^\delta)$ are pairwise disjoint.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Full Hahn classification]\label{pma:ser:thm:hahn}
For an ordered field $k$ and nonzero ordered abelian group $\Gamma$,
\[
 k((t^\Gamma))\text{ admits an admissible Polish topology}
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 |k|=\aleph_0\text{ and }\Gamma\cong\Z
 \text{ as ordered groups}.
\]
In the positive case the topology is unique and is the Laurent valuation
topology; all field operations, including inversion away from $0$, are
continuous.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
An admissible topology refines the order topology by
Theorem~\ref{pma:ser:thm:refinement}. A second-countable space has only countably many
pairwise disjoint nonempty open sets, since one can choose a distinct basic
open set inside each. Lemma~\ref{pma:ser:lem:separated} therefore forces $k$ countable
and $\Gamma\cong\Z$.

Conversely identify $\Gamma$ with $\Z$ and use the metric
\[
 d(f,g)=\min\{1,2^{-v(f-g)}\},\qquad 2^{-\infty}=0.
\]
A Cauchy sequence eventually stabilizes all coefficients of exponent at most
$N$, for every integer $N$. Stabilization for $N=0$ also gives a common finite
negative principal part for its tail. The stabilized coefficients therefore
define a Laurent series, and the sequence converges to it. Finite Laurent
polynomials form a countable dense set because $k$ is countable.

The valuation identities
$v(fg)=v(f)+v(g)$ and
$v(f+g)\geq\min\{v(f),v(g)\}$ give continuity of addition and multiplication.
For $f\ne0$, sufficiently small changes do not change $v(f)$, and
\[
 v(f^{-1}-g^{-1})=v(f-g)-v(f)-v(g)
\]
gives continuity of inversion.
The order and valuation topologies agree: a valuation ball sufficiently far
above the leading exponent of a positive $\varepsilon$ lies inside
$(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)$, while a sufficiently small monomial interval
lies inside any prescribed valuation ball. Thus the order is open and the
topology is admissible. Uniqueness follows from Corollary~\ref{pma:ser:cor:unique}.
\end{proof}

\noindent\writetag{} Source~03's \cref{pma:bor:thm:field} is the Borel
counterpart: $\R((t^\Gamma))$ has a coefficient-observable standard Borel
parametrization if and only if $\Gamma$ is scattered. The two results are
consistent: for $\Gamma=\Z^d$, $d\ge2$, the canonical support domain is Borel
(03's \cref{pma:bor:thm:finitelex}) but no admissible Polish topology exists
(the corollary below). For real coefficients, \cref{pma:ser:lem:separated}
excludes an admissible Polish topology on every field containing $\R$ and a
nonzero infinitesimal, among them 03's real Puiseux field $\Pui$, which
nevertheless has Borel codes (\cref{pma:bor:sec:Puiseux}).

\begin{corollary}[The obstruction is not a coding artifact]
For any countable ordered field $k$, the full field $k((t^\Q))$ has no
admissible Polish topology, regardless of how its elements are encoded.
For finite lexicographic powers $\Gamma=\Z^d$, $d\geq2$, the same conclusion
holds even when the canonical support domain is Borel.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Both ordered groups are noncyclic. In the second assertion the relevance of
Borel support coding is only comparative; the topological conclusion uses
Theorem~\ref{pma:ser:thm:hahn} alone.
\end{proof}

The excluded case $\Gamma=0$ is deliberate. It reduces to the coefficient
field itself; for example $\R$ with its usual topology is admissible. The
nontrivial infinitesimal scale is exactly what separates distinct constants
in Lemma~\ref{pma:ser:lem:separated}.

For countable $k$ and countable noncyclic $\Gamma$, the order density and
cellularity of the full Hahn field are both exactly $2^{\aleph_0}$: the lemma
gives the lower bound, and $|k^\Gamma|=2^{\aleph_0}$ gives the upper bound.

\section{The Puiseux obstruction: a Borel field that is not Polishable}
\label{pma:ser:sec:puiseux}

A second category obstruction does not use an order at all.

\begin{theorem}[Countable proper-subfield cover]\label{pma:ser:thm:cover}
Let $F$ be an uncountable field whose additive group has a Polish topology.
Assume every fixed scalar map is Borel. Then $F$ cannot be the union of
countably many Borel proper subfields.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Suppose $F=\bigcup_n K_n$, with each $K_n$ a Borel proper subfield.
By the Baire category theorem some $K_n$ is nonmeager. As it is an additive
subgroup, Pettis shows that $K_n=K_n-K_n$ is open.
Choose $a\in F\setminus K_n$. The maps $L_a$ and $L_{a^{-1}}$ are Borel
homomorphisms, hence continuous; thus $L_a$ is a homeomorphism and $aK_n$ is
open. But
\[
 K_n\cap aK_n=\{0\}.
\]
Indeed, a nonzero $x=ay$ with $x,y\in K_n$ would imply $a=x/y\in K_n$.
It follows that $\{0\}$ is open. The Polish group would be discrete and
countable, a contradiction.
\end{proof}

The Borel hypothesis on the covering subfields matters. A category argument
cannot simply be applied to arbitrary nonmeasurable subfields. In the Puiseux
application the subfields will actually be closed under \emph{every}
admissible topology, so no measurability assumption is being hidden.

Let $k$ now be a countable ordered field, and set
\[
 K_n=k((t^{1/n!})),\qquad \Pk=\bigcup_{n\geq1}K_n.
\]
The factorial convention makes the chain increasing. Each $K_n$ is a proper
subfield of $\Pk$, because rational monomials of denominators not dividing
$n!$ occur at later stages.

\begin{lemma}[Closed stages]\label{pma:ser:lem:stages}
Each $K_n$ is closed in the order topology of $\Pk$, and hence closed under
every admissible Polish topology on $\Pk$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If $f\notin K_n$, some nonzero coefficient $f_q$ has
$q\notin(1/n!)\Z$. Every $g$ agreeing with $f$ at all exponents at most $q$
still lies outside $K_n$. The set
\[
 \{g:v(g-f)>q\}
\]
is an order-open neighborhood of $f$. To check order-openness, a monomial
interval of exponent greater than $q$ about any of its points lies inside it.
Thus the complement of $K_n$ is open. Theorem~\ref{pma:ser:thm:refinement} transfers
this closedness to any admissible topology.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Absolute Puiseux non-Polishability]\label{pma:ser:thm:puiseux}
The Puiseux field $\Pk$ over a countable ordered field has no admissible
Polish topology. In its canonical standard Borel presentation, even without
mentioning the order, no compatible Polish additive-group topology can make
all fixed scalar maps Borel.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The field is uncountable, since $k((t))$ already contains all binary power
series. For an arbitrary admissible topology, Lemma~\ref{pma:ser:lem:stages} makes the
proper subfields $K_n$ closed, and Theorem~\ref{pma:ser:thm:cover} applies.
For the canonical Borel structure, $K_n$ is defined by the vanishing of all
coefficients outside $(1/n!)\Z$, hence is Borel. The same theorem applies
directly, without an order hypothesis.
\end{proof}

The ordinary valuation topology on $\Pk$ is separable and metrizable, but is
not complete. More strongly, it is meager in itself: each closed stage $K_n$
has empty interior. Given $f\in K_n$ and a valuation ball about $f$, add a
monomial $t^q$ of sufficiently large rational exponent with
$q\notin(1/n!)\Z$. The resulting point is in the ball but outside $K_n$.
Thus merely exhibiting an incomplete metric is not the reason for
Theorem~\ref{pma:ser:thm:puiseux}; the theorem rules out every admissible replacement.

\section{The Levi-Civita completion: a positive and unique presentation}
\label{pma:ser:sec:lc}

The classical Levi-Civita field is often defined with real coefficients.
Here the coefficient field is deliberately \emph{countable}. This changes
separability decisively. Our proofs work for every countable ordered $k$;
the algebraic and completeness arguments also work without an order on $k$.

\begin{definition}[Left-finite support]
A set $S\subseteq\Q$ is \emph{left-finite} if
$S\cap(-\infty,B]$ is finite for every rational $B$.
The field candidate $\Lk$ consists of all functions $f:\Q\to k$ with
left-finite support, written $\sum f_qt^q$.
\end{definition}

A nonempty left-finite set has a least element. Indeed, if $b\in S$, the
finite nonempty set $S\cap(-\infty,b]$ has a least element, which is least
in $S$. A left-finite support is either finite or an increasing sequence
tending to $+\infty$. In particular, it is a legal Hahn support.

\begin{proposition}[Field operations]\label{pma:ser:prop:lcfield}
The usual coefficientwise addition and convolution multiplication turn
$\Lk$ into a subfield of $k((t^\Q))$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
A finite union of left-finite supports is left-finite, so addition is defined
and preserves the support condition. Let $S,T$ be nonempty left-finite sets
with minima $s_0,t_0$. If $s+t\leq B$, then
$s\leq B-t_0$ and $t\leq B-s_0$. There are only finitely many such pairs.
Thus convolution coefficients are finite sums, and the product has left-finite
support. The coefficient at the least exponent of a nonzero product is the
product of the two leading coefficients, so the product is nonzero.

Write a nonzero series in the form
\[
 f=a t^q(1+h),\qquad a\in k^\times,\quad q\in\Q,
 \quad h=0\ \text{or}\ v(h)=\delta>0.
\]
For $h\ne0$, define
\[
 g=\sum_{j\geq0}(-h)^j.
\]
At exponents at most $B$, only indices $j$ with $j\delta\leq B$ can
contribute, apart from the constant term $j=0$. There are finitely many such
indices, and each corresponding power has finite support below $B$.
Therefore $g$ is left-finite and all coefficient manipulations are finite
below any prescribed cutoff. The geometric identity gives $(1+h)g=1$.
Hence $f^{-1}=a^{-1}t^{-q}g\in\Lk$. The case $h=0$ is immediate.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[A complete separable field topology]\label{pma:ser:thm:lc}
For countable ordered $k$, the metric
\begin{equation}\label{pma:ser:eq:metric}
 d(f,g)=\min\{1,2^{-v(f-g)}\},\qquad 2^{-\infty}=0,
\end{equation}
makes $\Lk$ an uncountable Polish topological field. Its topology equals the
order topology and is the unique admissible Polish topology on $\Lk$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The valuation inequality gives the ultrametric inequality for $d$.
Let $(f_j)$ be Cauchy. For each integer $N\geq0$ there is an index $j_N$
such that all $f_j$, $j\geq j_N$, have exactly the same coefficients at every
exponent at most $N$. Choose the $j_N$ increasing. These stabilized finite
truncations are compatible, and their union defines a coefficient function
$f$. For every bound $B$ choose an integer $N\geq\max\{0,B\}$.
The support of $f$ below $B$ is contained in the finite stabilized support
below $N$. Thus $f\in\Lk$. Stabilization also shows $f_j\to f$.

Finite rational-exponent polynomials over $k$ form a countable set. Truncating
any left-finite series above successively larger integers proves density.
The binary series $\sum_{n\geq0}b(n)t^n$ show uncountability.

Addition, multiplication, and inversion are continuous by the valuation
identities used in Theorem~\ref{pma:ser:thm:hahn}. For completeness, continuity of
multiplication at $(f,g)$ follows from
\[
 (f+h)(g+u)-fg=fu+gh+hu
\]
and the lower bound on the valuation of a sum. If $v(h)>v(f)$ and $f\ne0$,
then $v(f+h)=v(f)$, and the inverse-difference identity proves continuity of
inversion there.

For $\varepsilon>0$, every series of valuation strictly greater than
$v(\varepsilon)$ has absolute ordered value less than $\varepsilon$.
Conversely, an interval of radius $t^{B+1}$ lies inside the ball
$\{x:v(x)>B\}$. Translation gives equality of the two topologies.
The positive cone is open, so the topology is admissible. Uniqueness follows
from Corollary~\ref{pma:ser:cor:unique}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Completion and strict inclusions]\label{pma:ser:prop:completion}
The valued-field completion of $\Pk$, and also of the rational-function
field $k(t^\Q)$, is $\Lk$. Moreover,
\[
 k(t^\Q)\subseteq\Pk\subsetneq\Lk\subsetneq\Hk.
\]
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Each Puiseux support lies on a discrete rational lattice and is bounded
below, so is left-finite. Both $\Pk$ and $k(t^\Q)$ contain every finite
rational-exponent polynomial. These polynomials are dense in the complete
field $\Lk$, proving the completion assertion.

For strictness of the first displayed strict inclusion, use
\begin{equation}\label{pma:ser:eq:unboundeddenom}
 x=\sum_{n\geq2}t^{n+1/n}.
\end{equation}
Its exponents are increasing and unbounded, so $x\in\Lk$. Their reduced
denominators are exactly $n$, since $\gcd(n^2+1,n)=1$. They cannot all divide
one fixed factorial, so $x\notin\Pk$.
For the other strict inclusion use
\begin{equation}\label{pma:ser:eq:boundedhahn}
 y=\sum_{n\geq2}t^{1-1/n}.
\end{equation}
The support is increasing of order type $\omega$, hence well ordered,
but is infinite below $1$. Thus $y\in\Hk\setminus\Lk$.
\end{proof}

The partial sums of \eqref{pma:ser:eq:unboundeddenom} are valuation-Cauchy. The partial
sums of \eqref{pma:ser:eq:boundedhahn} are not: their successive differences have
valuations bounded above by $1$. A Hahn-summable family need not be a
convergent sequence of partial sums in the valuation topology. This is the
precise support distinction behind the completion boundary.

\begin{proposition}[The topology is not locally compact]\label{pma:ser:prop:notlccompact}
No point of $\Lk$ has a compact neighborhood.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Every neighborhood of $0$ contains a valuation ball
$V_B=\{f:v(f)>B\}$. Fix $q>B$ and infinitely many distinct coefficients
$c_j\in k$. Then $c_jt^q\in V_B$, and the mutual distances are the same
positive number $\min\{1,2^{-q}\}$. Thus no set containing $V_B$ is totally
bounded, and it cannot be compact. Translation deals with other points.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why real coefficients do not work]
The usual real-coefficient Levi-Civita field is complete in its valuation
topology but is not separable in that topology. More strongly, it has no
admissible Polish topology at all: the constant-indexed disjoint intervals
from Lemma~\ref{pma:ser:lem:separated} lie inside this smaller field as well.
The positive construction is therefore not a claim that the conventional
real-coefficient field is Polish.
\end{remark}

\section{A prefix criterion for separability}
\label{pma:ser:sec:prefix}

The Hahn and Levi-Civita results can be organized without referring to a
particular coding of supports. Let $F$ be any subfield of $k((t^\Q))$ that
contains $k$ and all rational monomials. For $B\in\Q$, let
\[
 \pi_B(f)=(f_q)_{q\leq B},\qquad
 V_B=\{f\in F:v(f)>B\}.
\]
The prefix $\pi_B(f)$ may be infinite. It need not itself represent an
element of $F$.

\begin{theorem}[Countable prefixes characterize order separability]
\label{pma:ser:thm:prefix}
The following conditions are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item the valuation topology of $F$ is separable;
\item the order topology of $F$ is second countable;
\item for every integer $N\geq0$, the set
$\pi_N(F)=\{\pi_N(f):f\in F\}$ is countable.
\end{enumerate}
Every admissible Polish topology on $F$ forces these conditions.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The order and valuation topologies agree by the monomial comparison in
Theorem~\ref{pma:ser:thm:lc}. The prefix classes at cutoff $N$ are exactly the open
cosets of $V_N$. A dense countable set meets every such coset, proving
(i)$\Rightarrow$(iii).

Conversely, choose one representative of each prefix class for every integer
$N\geq0$. The union $D$ of these countably many countable sets is dense:
every valuation neighborhood contains a coset at an integer cutoff and hence
one of the chosen representatives. The cosets themselves give a countable
base. This proves (iii)$\Rightarrow$(i),(ii), and (ii)$\Rightarrow$(i) is
immediate.

Finally, in an admissible topology each $V_N$ is open by
Theorem~\ref{pma:ser:thm:refinement}. Its pairwise disjoint cosets are therefore
countable in number. This gives (iii) even when the candidate Polish topology
is strictly finer than the valuation topology.
\end{proof}

For $\Lk$, each prefix is a finite rational-exponent polynomial, so there are
only countably many. The same holds for $\Pk$. For $\Hk$, a binary family
supported below a fixed bound gives continuum many different prefixes.
Thus the prefix criterion captures the Hahn obstruction but cannot by itself
capture the Puiseux obstruction: separability and complete metrizability
remain distinct questions.

A useful sufficient condition follows. If $F$ has countably many prefixes at
each cutoff and is complete for the valuation metric, then its order topology
is Polish and admissible; it is then the unique admissible topology. Whether
all admissible intermediate-field presentations must arise this way is left
as a sharply stated further question in Section~\ref{pma:ser:sec:questions}.

\section{Exact descriptive complexity of the three code domains}
\label{pma:ser:sec:complexity}

In this section $k$ is a countable ordered field with the discrete topology,
and all domains are regarded as subsets of the Polish product $k^\Q$.
The phrase ``complete'' refers to continuous reductions, with zero-dimensional
Polish source spaces. This is a complexity statement about coefficient codes;
it is not a statement that their subspace topology is the desired group
topology.

\begin{theorem}[Puiseux codes]\label{pma:ser:thm:pcodes}
The coefficient-code domain $\Pk\subseteq k^\Q$ is $\Sf$-complete.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For positive integers $n$ and nonnegative integers $M$, the set of all
coefficient functions supported on
\[
 (1/n!)\Z\cap[-M,\infty)
\]
is closed in $k^\Q$. Every such support defines a Puiseux series, and every
Puiseux series occurs for some $n,M$. Hence $\Pk$ is $F_\sigma$.

Let $\FIN\subseteq2^\N$ be the set of binary sequences with finite support,
a standard $\Sf$-complete set. Define a continuous map by placing the bit
$b(m)$ at exponent $1-1/(m+2)$ and placing zero at every other exponent.
Finite support gives a polynomial. Infinite support gives infinitely many
nonzero exponents in the bounded interval $[1/2,1)$, which cannot lie in one
discrete rational lattice. The map reduces $\FIN$ to $\Pk$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Levi-Civita codes]\label{pma:ser:thm:lcodes}
The coefficient-code domain $\Lk\subseteq k^\Q$ is $\Pt$-complete.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
At a fixed integer cutoff $N\geq0$, having finite support below $N$ is
$F_\sigma$: take the union over finite $S\subseteq\Q\cap(-\infty,N]$ of
the closed condition that all coefficients below $N$ outside $S$ vanish.
Intersect over $N$ to obtain a $\Pt$ definition.

The set
\[
 \FIN^\N=\{b\in2^{\N\times\N}:
             \text{each row }b(n,\cdot)\text{ has finite support}\}
\]
is $\Pt$-complete. Place the coefficient $b(n,m)$ at
\begin{equation}\label{pma:ser:eq:rowcode}
 q(n,m)=n+1-\frac1{m+2},
\end{equation}
and zero elsewhere. These rational exponents are pairwise distinct.
If one row is infinite, its support is infinite below $n+1$, so the resulting
code is not left-finite. If every row is finite, only finitely many rows can
contribute below any prescribed bound, and each contributes finitely many
terms. The map is continuous and gives the required reduction.
\end{proof}

\noindent\writetag{} These are the Borel levels of source~03's
\cref{pma:bor:thm:finitelex} ($\WO(\Z)$ is $\boldsymbol\Sigma^0_2$-complete,
$\WO(\Z^d_{\mathrm{lex}})$ is $\boldsymbol\Pi^0_3$-complete for $d\ge2$),
attained here by different sets.

For completeness, the usual hardness inputs can be obtained directly.
Given an $F_\sigma$ set $A=\bigcup_n C_n$ in Baire space, take the $C_n$
closed and increasing. At stage $s$, let $m_s(x)$ be the least $n\leq s$
whose closed-set tree is compatible with the length-$s$ prefix of $x$, or
$s+1$ if there is none. The values $m_s$ are nondecreasing. Output a $1$
exactly when the value increases. This is a continuous map into $2^\N$;
it has finitely many $1$s exactly when $x\in A$. Coordinatewise application
to a countable intersection of $F_\sigma$ sets proves the completeness of
$\FIN^\N$.

\begin{theorem}[Full rational Hahn codes; classical support obstruction]
\label{pma:ser:thm:hcodes}
The coefficient-code domain $\Hk\subseteq k^\Q$ is $\Pc$-complete.
Consequently, it has no standard Borel parametrization with Borel coefficient
observations and exactly one code for each series.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A support fails to be well ordered exactly when there is an infinite strictly
decreasing sequence of rational exponents with nonzero coefficients.
Existence of that sequence is an analytic condition. Thus $\Hk$ is coanalytic.

For hardness use the standard $\Pc$-complete set $\WF$ of well-founded
trees on $\N$ \cite{Kechris}. Give $\N^{<\N}$ the Kleene--Brouwer order:
proper extensions precede their prefixes, and incompatible sequences are
compared at their first differing coordinate. This is a countable linear
order, so it embeds into $\Q\cap(0,1)$. Fix such an embedding $e$.
For a tree $T$, put coefficient $1$ at $e(s)$ exactly when $s\in T$, and
put zero elsewhere. The map is continuous.
An infinite branch gives a descending chain in the Kleene--Brouwer order.
Conversely, well-founded induction on a well-founded tree shows that the
order below each node is a countable ordered sum of the child orders,
followed by the node itself; it is therefore a well-order. Hence
$T\in\WF$ exactly when the resulting support is well ordered.

If a standard Borel space parametrized all Hahn series uniquely and every
coefficient were Borel, its coefficient map into $k^\Q$ would be a Borel
injection with range exactly $\Hk$. The Lusin--Souslin theorem would make
that range Borel, contradicting completeness.
\end{proof}

\noindent\writetag{} \Cref{pma:ser:thm:hcodes} is a second proof of the
non-scattered half of source~03's \cref{pma:bor:thm:dichotomy,pma:bor:thm:field}
(via \cref{pma:bor:lem:KB}) and of the collection's lightface results
\replabel{onot:neg:lem:kbtree} and \replabel{onot:neg:thm:pionesone}; source~09
credits ``the preceding project report'' and claims no priority for it.

The last theorem is included to complete the comparison, not to reclaim the
project's earlier coanalytic support result \cite{s09:PriorSupport}. It is also
logically weaker than the absolute topological obstruction for a different
reason: it requires coefficient-observable codes, whereas
Theorem~\ref{pma:ser:thm:hahn} does not.

\begin{proposition}[The Polish Levi-Civita topology preserves the Borel codes]
\label{pma:ser:prop:sameborel}
The valuation topology on $\Lk$ has the same Borel sets as the subspace
Borel structure inherited from $k^\Q$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Each coefficient map is continuous for the valuation topology: fixing the
entire prefix up to its exponent fixes that coefficient. Thus coefficient
Borel sets are valuation-Borel.
In the other direction, take the countable base consisting of balls centered
at finite rational-exponent polynomials with nonnegative integer cutoffs.
A basic ball is defined by countably many coefficient equalities. It is
therefore Borel in the coefficient structure. Every valuation-open set is a
countable union of these basic balls, proving the converse inclusion.
\end{proof}

Both $\Pk$ and $\Lk$ carry Borel field operations in their coefficient
structures. For $\Lk$ this follows from Proposition~\ref{pma:ser:prop:sameborel} and
continuous valuation arithmetic. On $\Pk$ the same Borel equality holds for
its countable valuation base; its field operations are valuation-continuous.
This does not imply that $\Pk$ is completely metrizable as a group.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering\small
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.25}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}l>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
\toprule
Field & Exact coefficient-code complexity & Admissible Polish arithmetic\\
\midrule
$\Pk$ & $\Sf$-complete & None; proper-subfield cover obstruction\\
$\Lk$ & $\Pt$-complete & Exists and is unique; valuation completion\\
$\Hk$ & $\Pc$-complete & None; uncountably many separated prefixes\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\caption{Countable ordered coefficients and rational exponents throughout.
A low Borel rank does not predict Polishability.}
\label{pma:ser:tab:trichotomy}
\end{table}

\section{Real closedness and completion without a defect argument}
\label{pma:ser:sec:realclosed}

To connect these fields to integer-part arithmetic, we specialize to a real
closed coefficient field. A useful proof avoids importing a full theory of
henselian defect. The sole substantial algebraic input is the classical
Newton--Puiseux theorem in characteristic zero: for an algebraically closed
field $K$ of characteristic zero, $\bigcup_n K((t^{1/n!}))$ is algebraically
closed. Poonen gives this as Corollary~7 \cite{Poonen}.

\begin{lemma}[Completion preserves algebraic closedness]
\label{pma:ser:lem:completionac}
The metric completion of a non-Archimedean valued algebraically closed field
is algebraically closed.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Write $|\cdot|$ for the multiplicative real-valued absolute value; it is not
the truncated metric. Let $K$ be the field and $\widehat K$ its completion.
Take a monic polynomial
\[
 P(X)=X^d+\sum_{i<d}a_iX^i\in\widehat K[X],\qquad d\geq1.
\]
Choose monic polynomials $P_n\in K[X]$ of degree $d$ whose coefficients
converge to those of $P$, and positive real numbers $\varepsilon_n\to0$
such that every coefficient of $P_{n+1}-P_n$ has absolute value at most
$\varepsilon_n$. There is a common real bound $M\geq1$ for $1$ and for the
absolute values of all coefficients of all $P_n$.
Every root $z$ of any $P_n$ satisfies $|z|\leq M$: otherwise the monic term
would strictly dominate every lower-degree term.

Choose a root $z_0$ of $P_0$. Having chosen $z_n$, factor
$P_{n+1}(X)=\prod_{j=1}^d(X-w_j)$ over $K$. Since $P_n(z_n)=0$,
\[
 \prod_{j=1}^d|z_n-w_j|
 =|P_{n+1}(z_n)|
 \leq \varepsilon_n M^{d-1}.
\]
Some root $w_j$ therefore satisfies
$|z_n-w_j|\leq(\varepsilon_n M^{d-1})^{1/d}$.
Choose it as $z_{n+1}$. Consecutive distances tend to zero, and the
ultrametric inequality then makes $(z_n)$ Cauchy: the distance between any
two late terms is at most the largest intervening consecutive distance.
Let $z\in\widehat K$ be its limit. Continuity of polynomial evaluation and
coefficient convergence give $P(z)=0$. Thus every nonconstant polynomial
has a root, which is equivalent to algebraic closedness.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Real closed Puiseux and Levi-Civita fields]
\label{pma:ser:thm:rc}
If $k$ is a real closed ordered field, then $\Pk$ and $\Lk$ are real closed.
In particular, this applies to the countable field $k=\ka$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let $K=k(i)$, which is algebraically closed because $k$ is real closed.
Splitting coefficients into their real and imaginary parts gives
\[
 \mathcal P_K=\Pk(i),\qquad \mathcal L_K=\Lk(i).
\]
For the first identity, finitely many denominator bounds can be replaced by
one common bound; for the second, a finite union of left-finite supports is
left-finite. By Newton--Puiseux, $\mathcal P_K$ is algebraically closed.
By Proposition~\ref{pma:ser:prop:completion} and Lemma~\ref{pma:ser:lem:completionac}, so is
$\mathcal L_K$. The fields $\Pk$ and $\Lk$ are ordered and adjoining $i$
makes them algebraically closed; the standard characterization of real
closed fields therefore proves the assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[A ring-language separation]\label{pma:ser:cor:rclang}
For $k=\ka$, the field $\Pk$ has no Polish additive-group topology with
jointly Borel multiplication, even without a separately specified measurable
order. The field $\Lk$ does have such a topology, and it is unique.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Real closedness makes the order Borel by Corollary~\ref{pma:ser:cor:realorder}.
Apply Theorems~\ref{pma:ser:thm:puiseux} and~\ref{pma:ser:thm:lc}.
\end{proof}

Real closedness here is a classical algebraic property proved using a
classical input; it is not the proposed novelty. Its role is to remove the
order-measurability hypothesis from the final field-language contrast.

\section{Local Polish hulls, unbounded support ranks, and an exact cover number}
\label{pma:ser:sec:hulls}

The failure of Polishability for the full Hahn field is global, not an
obstruction to accommodating any prescribed countable collection of its
elements. This section proves that much stronger local positive statement.
In particular, the Levi-Civita field is a minimal completion, not a maximal
Polish subfield of the Hahn field.

\begin{lemma}[Metric completeness of the rational Hahn field]
\label{pma:ser:lem:hahncomplete}
For every field $k$, $k((t^\Q))$ is complete for the metric
$d(f,g)=\min\{1,2^{-v(f-g)}\}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For a Cauchy sequence, every prefix up to an integer $N\geq0$ eventually
stabilizes, just as in Theorem~\ref{pma:ser:thm:lc}. This time a stabilized prefix
need not have finite support, but it has well-ordered support because it is
a prefix of a Hahn series. The compatible stabilized prefixes define a
coefficient function $f$.
Its support is well ordered: given any nonempty subset $S$ of that support,
choose $s\in S$ and an integer $N\geq\max\{0,s\}$. The nonempty set
$S\cap(-\infty,N]$ has a least element since it lies in the stabilized
well-ordered prefix. That element is also least in $S$, as all remaining
points exceed $N$. Thus $f$ is a Hahn series, and stabilization shows that
it is the limit of the original sequence.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Countable-parameter Polish hulls]\label{pma:ser:thm:hulls}
Let $k$ be countable and ordered, $H=k((t^\Q))$, and $A\subseteq H$ countable.
Then
\begin{equation}\label{pma:ser:eq:hull}
 F_A=\overline{k(t^\Q)(A)}^{\,v}\ \subseteq H
\end{equation}
is a complete separable ordered subfield containing $\Lk\cup A$.
Its valuation topology is its unique admissible Polish topology.
It is the smallest valuation-closed subfield of $H$ containing
$k(t^\Q)\cup A$.
Every valuation-complete separable intermediate field
$\Lk\subseteq F\subseteq H$ arises in this way.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The algebraic field $k(t^\Q)(A)$ is countable. Its closure is separable and
is complete by Lemma~\ref{pma:ser:lem:hahncomplete}. Closure preserves addition and
multiplication by continuity, and preserves inversion at every nonzero limit
by the inverse-difference identity. Thus $F_A$ is a subfield.
The closure of $k(t^\Q)$ in $H$ is exactly $\Lk$: it is dense in $\Lk$,
and $\Lk$ is complete and hence closed in the metric space $H$.
Therefore $\Lk\subseteq F_A$.
The valuation and order topologies agree, and
Corollary~\ref{pma:ser:cor:unique} gives uniqueness. Minimality follows directly
from taking closure. Finally, for a complete separable intermediate field
$F$, choose a countable valuation-dense subset $A\subseteq F$.
Completeness makes $F$ closed in $H$, and \eqref{pma:ser:eq:hull} then gives
$F_A=F$.
\end{proof}

Taking $A=\{y\}$ for the bounded infinite support in
\eqref{pma:ser:eq:boundedhahn} immediately gives
\[
 \Lk\subsetneq F_{\{y\}}\subsetneq H.
\]
The second strict inclusion follows from Theorem~\ref{pma:ser:thm:hahn}.
Thus bounded infinite supports do not individually prevent Polish field
arithmetic. What is impossible is allowing all independently varying bounded
prefixes at once.

\begin{theorem}[A support bound for every separable family]
\label{pma:ser:thm:rankbound}
Let $X\subseteq H=k((t^\Q))$ be valuation-separable, and let $D\subseteq X$
be countable and dense. Then
\begin{equation}\label{pma:ser:eq:rankbound}
 \operatorname{ot}(\supp f)
 \leq\beta_D:=\sup_{d\in D}\operatorname{ot}(\supp d)<\omega_1
 \qquad(f\in X).
\end{equation}
Here the empty support has order type $0$. In particular, every admissibly
Polish intermediate field has a uniform countable support-order bound.
There is no uniform countable bound valid for all Polish hulls $F_A$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For $f\in X$ and every integer $N\geq0$, density supplies a $d_N\in D$
with $v(f-d_N)>N$. Their prefixes through $N$ agree. Hence
\[
 \operatorname{ot}(\supp f\cap(-\infty,N])
 \leq\operatorname{ot}(\supp d_N)\leq\beta_D.
\]
These prefixes are increasing initial segments whose union is the support
of $f$, so their order types have supremum
$\operatorname{ot}(\supp f)$. This proves \eqref{pma:ser:eq:rankbound}.
The supremum defining $\beta_D$ is countable because $D$ and all rational
supports are countable.

For an admissible topology, a countable dense set remains valuation-dense
by Theorem~\ref{pma:ser:thm:refinement}, so the preceding argument applies.
For the last assertion, let $\alpha$ be any countable ordinal. Embed its
linear order into $\Q\cap(0,1)$ and let $h_\alpha$ have coefficient $1$
on that support and $0$ elsewhere. It belongs to the Polish hull
$F_{\{h_\alpha\}}$ and has support order type $\alpha$.
\end{proof}

\noindent\writetag{} Source~03's \cref{pma:bor:thm:bounded} proves a uniform
countable support bound for every Borel coefficient-observable family by
analytic boundedness; \cref{pma:ser:thm:rankbound} is the elementary
counterpart for separable families, as source~09 says below.

This bound uses separability and prefix stabilization, not analytic
boundedness or a Borel-domain hypothesis. It is a specialized elementary
counterpart of the support bounds in the preceding Borel-support manuscript.
It also makes the failure of maximality explicit.

\begin{corollary}[No maximal admissibly Polish intermediate field]
\label{pma:ser:cor:nomax}
Every admissibly Polish field $F$ with
$k(t^\Q)\subseteq F\subseteq H$ is properly contained in another such
field. The larger field can have an element whose support order type exceeds
all support order types in $F$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Choose a countable dense set $D$ in the admissible topology of $F$ and let
$\beta_D$ be as in Theorem~\ref{pma:ser:thm:rankbound}. Choose a Hahn series $h$
with support order type $\beta_D+1$.
The hull $F_{D\cup\{h\}}$ contains the valuation closure of $D$, and hence
contains $F$. It also contains $h\notin F$. Theorem~\ref{pma:ser:thm:hulls} proves
that it is admissibly Polish.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Real-closed, truncation-closed hulls}

With $k=\ka$, a hull can be required to preserve both real closedness and
all rational truncations. Full Hahn real closedness follows from the
classical algebraic-closedness theorem for Hahn fields with algebraically
closed coefficients and divisible exponent group
\cite[Corollary~4]{Poonen}, applied after adjoining $i$.

\begin{theorem}[A strengthened hull with a countable truncation core]
\label{pma:ser:thm:rchull}
Let $H=\ka((t^\Q))$ and let $A\subseteq H$ be countable. There exist a
countable real closed truncation-closed subfield $B\subseteq H$ and a field
$C=\overline B^{\,v}$ such that
\[
 k(t^\Q)\cup A\subseteq B\subseteq C,
\]
$C$ is real closed and admissibly Polish, and every rational prefix of every
$f\in C$ belongs to $B$:
\begin{equation}\label{pma:ser:eq:trunccore}
 T_{\leq q}(C)\subseteq B\qquad(q\in\Q).
\end{equation}
In particular, $C$ is truncation-closed and contains $\Lk\cup A$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Start with the countable field $B_0=k(t^\Q)(A)$.
